Oblique derivative boundary value problems on families of planar domains Thanks: Supported in part by NSF grant DMS-1500162
Ziming Shi

Abstract
. We consider second-order elliptic equations with oblique derivative boundary conditions, defined on a family of bounded domains in ℂ \mathbb{C} that depend smoothly on a real parameter λ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \lambda\in[0,1] . We derive sharp regularity properties of the solutions in all variables, including the parameter λ \lambda . More specifically we show that the solution and its derivatives are continuous in all variables, and the Hölder norms of the space variables are bounded uniformly in λ \lambda .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
30E25, 30G20, 35J25, 45E05

1.
Introduction

In this paper we study the domain dependence of solutions to the oblique boundary value problem on the plane. We consider the following problem on a family of “smoothly varying” bounded domains
\Omega^{\lambda}
in
\mathbb{C}
that depend on some parameter
\lambda\in[0,1]
:

(1.1)

\mathcal{L}^{\lambda}u^{\lambda}=f^{\lambda}\quad\text{in $\Omega^{\lambda}$ },\qquad\frac{du^{\lambda}}{d\Upsilon^{\lambda}}=\gamma^{\lambda}\quad\text{on $b\Omega^{\lambda}$. }

Here
\mathcal{L}^{\lambda}
is a second-order elliptic operator defined by

(1.2)

\mathcal{L}^{\lambda}u^{\lambda}=a^{\lambda}(x,y)u^{\lambda}_{xx}+2b^{\lambda}(x,y)u^{\lambda}_{xy}+c^{\lambda}(x,y)u^{\lambda}_{yy}+d^{\lambda}(x,y)u^{\lambda}_{x}+e^{\lambda}(x,y)u^{\lambda}_{y},

and
\Upsilon^{\lambda}
is an arbitrary non-vanishing directional vector field along
b\Omega^{\lambda}
. We assume
a^{\lambda}>0
and the uniform ellipticity condition:

(1.3)

a^{\lambda}c^{\lambda}-(b^{\lambda})^{2}\geq\delta_{0}>0,\quad\forall\;\lambda\in[0,1].

We show that if all the coefficient functions
a^{\lambda},b^{\lambda},c^{\lambda},d^{\lambda},e^{\lambda},F^{\lambda},\gamma^{\lambda}
depend on the parameter
\lambda
“smoothly”. Then with certain suitable conditions to guarantee the existence and uniqueness of the problem (
1.1
), the family of solutions
u^{\lambda}
will also depend on the parameter “smoothly”.

We now make the concept of domain dependence precise.
Let
\mathbb{N}
be the set of non-negative integers. Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\mathbb{C}
, and let
k,j\in\mathbb{N}
satisfy
k\geq j
. Given a family of functions
u^{\lambda}
defined on
\Omega
,
we say that
u^{\lambda}
is in the space
\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
if for every integer
i
with
0\leq i\leq j
,
[\lambda\mapsto\partial{}_{\lambda}^{i}u^{\lambda}]
is a continuous map from
[0,1]
to
C^{k-i}(\overline{\Omega})
and a bounded map from
[0,1]
to
C^{k-i+\mu}(\overline{\Omega})
. Our main result is

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
k,j
be in
\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
with
k\geq j
, and let
0<\mu<1
.
Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\mathbb{C}
with
\mathcal{C}^{k+2+\mu}
boundary. Let
\Gamma^{\lambda}
be a family of maps that embed
\Omega
onto
\Omega^{\lambda}
, with
\Gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+2+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
and
\Gamma^{0}
being the identity map.
Suppose that
a^{\lambda},b^{\lambda},c^{\lambda},d^{\lambda},e^{\lambda},f^{\lambda}
are functions in
\Omega^{\lambda}
, such that
a^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda},b^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda},c^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
,
d^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda},e^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda},f^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
, and suppose that
\Upsilon^{\lambda},\gamma^{\lambda}
are functions on
b\Omega^{\lambda}
such that
\Upsilon^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda},\gamma^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(b\Omega)
.
Suppose certain well-posedness conditions (see Proposition
3.8
) are given so that (
1.1
) with (
1.3
) has a unique solution for each
\lambda
. Then
u^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+2+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
.

The existence and uniqueness theory for fixed
\lambda
are classical results and we shall review them in Section 3.

In Bertrand-Gong
[
1
]
the analogous results were proved for the Dirichlet and Neumann problems for the Laplacian equation. As a corollary, they proved a parameter version of the Riemann mapping theorem, namely, for any family of simply connected domains
\Omega^{\lambda}
given by the embeddings
\Gamma^{\lambda}:\overline{\Omega}\to\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}}
with
\Gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
, there exists a family of Riemann mappings
R^{\lambda}:\Omega^{\lambda}\to\mathbb{D}
such that
R^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
, where
\mathbb{D}
is the unit disk.
We shall use this result in our proof of Theorem
1.1
when the domains are simply-connected.

For fixed
\lambda
, we apply the classical theory of Vekua to solve equation (
1.1
).
The idea is to first find an isothermal coordinates in which the problem takes the form

(1.4)

\Delta U+a(x,y)U_{x}+b(x,y)U_{y}=f(x,y),\quad\alpha U_{x}-\beta U_{y}=\gamma.

By setting
w=u+iv
for
u=U_{x}
and
v=-U_{y}
, (
1.4
) takes the form

(1.5)

\displaystyle\partial{}_{\overline{z}}w+Aw+B\overline{w}=F,\quad\text{in $\Omega$,}

(1.6)

\displaystyle Re[\overline{l}w]=\gamma,\quad\text{on $b\Omega$,}

where we assume
l=\alpha+i\beta
is nowhere vanishing on
b\Omega
. Problem (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) is called a
generalized Riemann-Hilbert problem
. (The classical Riemann-Hilbert problem is the special case when
A,B,F\equiv 0
.)
Vekua tackled this problem by introducing the theory of generalized analytic functions. His idea is to find an integral representation formula for a solution
w
satisfying equation (
1.5
). The boundary condition (
1.6
) then translates into a singular integral equation on curves for which there is well-known theory.

For our problem on domains with parameter, we will first reduce the problem to the
\overline{\partial}
form (
1.5
)-(
1.6
). If the domains are simply-connected, we can pull back the problem onto the fixed unit disk
\mathbb{D}
by using the parameter version of the Riemann mapping theorem proved in
[
1
]
. The solution is shown to satisfy a Fredholm integral equation on
\mathbb{D}
, and the smoothness in parameter follows from a standard compactness argument.

The problem becomes more difficult for families of multiply-connected domains. In this case one may also pull back the equations onto some fixed domain. However, now the pull back maps are no longer biholomorphic, so this procedure will destroy the
\overline{\partial}
form (
1.5
). Thus we are forced to deal with variable domains in this case. We use Vekua’s idea to reduce the problem to a singular integral equation on
b\Omega^{\lambda}
, and then reduce it into a Fredholm equation in order to use compactness argument.

We mention that one could certainly obtain an estimate of the form

\|w^{\lambda}\|_{C^{k+1+\mu}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})}\leq C_{\lambda}\|f^{\lambda}\|_{C^{k+\mu}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})},

for each fixed
\lambda
, by solving the problem on each fixed domain. However this estimate is not useful since in general we do not have any information as to how the the constant
C_{\lambda}
depends on
\lambda
.

Following Vekua, we will henceforth call (
1.5
)-(
1.6
) Problem A for
\overline{\partial}
or simply Problem A.
There is a vast literature on the oblique derivative boundary value problem. See for example Miranda
[
4
, Chapter 3]
and Hörmander
[
3
, Chapter 10]
.

The paper is organized as follows:

In Section 2, we introduce the notations used in our paper. We review the definition of Hölder spaces with parameter as were used in
[
6
]
and
[
1
]
, and we show some basic properties of these spaces. In particular, we prove a result (Proposition
2.3
) which will be used repeatedly throughout the paper to simplify our arguments.
Section 3 contains an exposition of Vekua’s theory on the solvability and regularity of Problem A without parameter.
The solvability properties are determined by the index of the problem (see Definition
3.1
) and the number of connected components of the boundary
b\Omega
.
All the results in this section can be found in
[
7
]
.
In Section 4, we prove Theorem
1.1
for families of simply-connected domains
\Omega^{\lambda}
.
In Section 5 we examine how the kernel in Vekua’s integral solution formula depends on
\lambda
. These results will be used in Section 6 to prove Theorem
1.1
for families of multiply-connected domains
\Omega^{\lambda}
. For our proof we are able to avoid taking derivatives on the kernel as was done in
[
1
]
.

In the end we attach an appendix, where we provide either complete proofs or precise references to the various claims used in earlier proofs. For the reader’s convenience we also include a few of Vekua’s results that we used frequently in the paper.
For the constants appearing in our estimate, we use the notation
C_{1,0}
(resp.
C_{k+\mu,j}
) to indicate that it depends on
\|\Gamma^{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1,0}(\Omega)}
(resp.
\|\Gamma^{\lambda}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\Omega)}
). For a family of functions
u^{\lambda}
defined on
\Omega^{\lambda}
, we shall write either
u^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
or
u^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
. We shall write
|f|_{k+\mu}
instead of
|f|_{C^{k+\mu}(\overline{\Omega})}
whenever the domain
\Omega
is clear from the context.

Acknowledgment
.

The author would like to express his deep gratitude to Professor Xianghong Gong and Professor Xiaojun Huang for providing innumerable inspiration, encouragement and guidance through many years, without which this project would never be completed. The author would also like to thank the anonymous referee for reading the paper carefully and for many valuable suggestions.

2.
Hölder spaces with parameter

In this section we introduce Hölder spaces with parameter as in
[
1
]
, which can be used to study boundary value problems on families of domains.

We denote by
C^{r}(\overline{\Omega})
,
r\geq 0
the space of complex-valued functions which are Hölder continuous with exponent
r
on a bounded domain
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{C}
.
When the domain
\Omega
in question is clear, we also write
|f|_{r}
in place of
|f|_{C^{r}(\overline{\Omega})}
.
The space
C^{r-}(\overline{\Omega})
consists of those functions that are in
C^{r-\varepsilon}(\overline{\Omega})
, for all
\varepsilon>0
.
We write
\mathbb{D}
for the unit disk in
\mathbb{C}
, and we write
dA(z)=\frac{i}{2}dz\wedge d\overline{z}
for the area element in
\mathbb{C}
.
The Cauchy-Green or
\overline{\partial}
solution operator on a domain
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{C}
is defined by

(2.1)

T_{\Omega}f(z):=-\frac{1}{\pi}\iint_{\Omega}\frac{f(\zeta)}{\zeta-z}\,dA(\zeta),\quad z\in\Omega.

For
1\leq p\leq\infty
and
\nu\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote by
L_{p,\nu}(\mathbb{C})
the space of functions
f
satisfying

(2.2)

f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{D}),\quad\quad f_{\nu}(z):=|z|^{-\nu}f\left(\frac{1}{z}\right)\in L^{p}(\mathbb{D}),\quad

with the norm
|f|_{L_{p,\nu}(\mathbb{C})}=|f|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{D})}+|f_{\nu}|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{D})}
.
The space
L_{p,\nu}(\mathbb{C})
is a Banach space with this norm. Intuitively the lower index
\nu
describes the rate of growth of
f
at
\infty
, and we have
L_{p,\nu_{1}}(\mathbb{C})\subset L_{p,\nu_{2}}(\mathbb{C})
, if
\nu_{1}>\nu_{2}
. It is easy to see that
f_{\nu}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{D})
if and only if

\int_{\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{D}}|u|^{\nu p-4}|f(u)|^{p}\,dA(u)<\infty.

For
0<\mu<1
, we let
L_{\mu}^{p}(\Omega)
denote the set of functions
f
that satisfy
f\in L^{p}(\Omega)
,
f\equiv 0
outside
\Omega
, and

B(f,\Omega,\mu,p)=\sup_{h\in\mathbb{C}}\frac{\left(\iint_{\Omega}\left|f(z+h)-f(z)\right|^{p}\,dA(z)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}}{|h|^{\mu}}<\infty.

Then
L_{\mu}^{p}(\Omega)
is a Banach space with the norm:

(2.3)

|f|_{L_{\mu}^{p}(\Omega)}=|f|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+B(f,\Omega,\mu,p).

We say that
w
is a solution to the equation

(2.4)

\partial{}_{\overline{z}}w+Aw+B\overline{w}=F,\quad\text{in $\Omega$},

if
w,\partial{}_{\overline{z}}w\in L^{1}(\Omega)
, and the above equation holds in the sense of distributions in
\Omega
. We denote by
\mathcal{L}_{p}(A,B,F,\Omega)
(resp.
\mathcal{L}_{p,2}(A,B,F)
) the set of
w\in L^{1}(\Omega)
that satisfy (
2.4
) with
A,B,F\in L^{p}(\Omega)
(resp.
L_{p,2}(\mathbb{C})
) If
F\equiv 0
, we write
\mathcal{L}_{p}(A,B,\Omega)
(resp.
\mathcal{L}_{p,2}(A,B)
) for the spaces defined as above.

We adopt the following notations for differences of functions:

(2.5)

\delta_{z,\zeta}(f(\cdot,\lambda)):=f(z,\lambda)-f(\zeta,\lambda),\quad\quad\delta^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}f(z,\cdot):=f(z,\lambda_{1})-f(z,\lambda_{2}).

We denote the difference quotient with respect to the parameter by

(2.6)

D^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}f(\cdot):=\frac{f(\cdot,\lambda)-f(\cdot,\lambda_{0})}{\lambda-\lambda_{0}}.

To study boundary value problems on families of domains, we use the Hölder space with parameter
\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega_{\Gamma}})
from
[
1
]
(our space is the same as the
\mathcal{B}
space in
[
1
]
.) First we define the spaces for a fixed domain:

Definition 2.1
.

Let
k,j
be in
\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
with
k\geq j
, and let
0<\mu<1
. Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\mathbb{C}
with
C^{k+\mu}
boundary, and let
\{u^{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in[0,1]}
be a family of functions on
\Omega
. We say that
\{u^{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in[0,1]}
is in the class
\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}_{\ast}(\overline{\Omega})
, if for all
0\leq i\leq j
,
|\partial{}_{\lambda}^{i}u^{\lambda}|_{k+\mu}
are uniformly bounded in
\lambda
, and
\partial{}_{\lambda}^{i}u^{\lambda}
is continuous in
\lambda
in the
C^{k}(\overline{\Omega})
norm. The norm on
\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}_{\ast}(\overline{\Omega})
is defined to be

(2.7)

|u^{\lambda}|_{k+\mu,j}:=\sup_{{0\leq i\leq j,\lambda\in[0,1]}}\{|\partial{}_{\lambda}^{i}u^{\lambda}|_{k+\mu}\}.

Similarly we define
\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}_{\ast}(b\Omega)
by replacing
\Omega
with
b\Omega
in the above expressions.

For our problem with parameter, we consider functions defined on a family of domains
\Omega^{\lambda}
, for
\lambda\in[0,1]
. We assume there is an embedding
\Gamma^{\lambda}:\Omega\to\Omega^{\lambda}
, where
\Gamma^{0}
is the identity map from
\Omega
to itself.

Now we make the following observation. Suppose for each
\lambda\in[0,1]
,
u^{\lambda}
is defined on an open set
V
with
\cup_{\lambda}\Omega^{\lambda}\subset\subset V
. Let
\Gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}_{\ast}(\overline{\Omega})
, and
u^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}_{\ast}(V)
. But then
\widetilde{u}^{\lambda}(z):=u^{\lambda}(\Gamma^{\lambda}(z))
is not in the space
\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}_{\ast}(\overline{\Omega})
, since when one takes derivative in
\lambda
, by the chain rule one needs to take the space derivative of
u^{\lambda}
.
Hence instead of
\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}_{\ast}(\overline{\Omega})
, we shall adopt the following definition in
[
1
]
for a family of functions defined on a family of domains:

Definition 2.2
.

Let
k,j,\mu
be as in Theorem
1.1
. Let
\{\Gamma^{\lambda}:\overline{\Omega}\to\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}}\}_{\lambda\in[0,1]}
be in the class
\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}_{\ast}(\overline{\Omega})
. Let
u^{\lambda}
be a family of functions defined on
\Omega^{\lambda}
, and put
\widetilde{u}(z,\lambda):=u^{\lambda}(\Gamma^{\lambda}(z))
. We say that

u^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}_{\ast}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega_{\Gamma}})\ \text{ if $\widetilde{u}(z,\lambda)\in\mathcal{C}_{\ast}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})$},\quad u^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}_{\ast}^{k+\mu,j}(b\Omega_{\Gamma})\ \text{if $\widetilde{u}(z,\lambda)\in C_{\ast}^{k+\mu,j}(b\Omega)$}.

For
0\leq j\leq k
, define the spaces

\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega}_{\Gamma}):=\bigcap_{i=0}^{j}\mathcal{C}_{\ast}^{k-i+\mu,i}(\overline{\Omega}_{\Gamma}),\quad\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(b\Omega_{\Gamma}):=\bigcap_{i=0}^{j}\mathcal{C}_{\ast}^{k-i+\mu,i}(b\Omega_{\Gamma}).

The space
\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega}_{\Gamma})
is a Banach space with the norm defined by

\|u^{\lambda}\|_{k+\mu,j}:=\max_{0\leq i\leq j}

\{|\widetilde{u}(z,\lambda)|_{k-i+\mu,i}\}
. In other words, we say that
u^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega}_{\Gamma})
if
|\partial{}_{\lambda}^{i}\widetilde{u}|_{k-i+\mu}
is uniformly bounded in
\lambda
, and
\partial{}_{\lambda}^{i}\widetilde{u}
is continuous in the
C^{k-i}(\overline{\Omega})
norm, for each
i
with
0\leq i\leq j
.
Since
|\partial{}_{\lambda}^{i^{\prime}}u|_{k-i+\mu}\leq|\partial{}_{\lambda}^{i^{\prime}}u|_{k-i^{\prime}+\mu}
, for any
i^{\prime}
,
0\leq i^{\prime}\leq i
, we have

\|u^{\lambda}\|_{k+\mu,j}=\max_{0\leq i\leq j}\{|\partial{}_{\lambda}^{i}\widetilde{u}(z,\lambda)|_{k-i+\mu}\},\quad\widetilde{u}(z,\lambda):=u^{\lambda}(\Gamma^{\lambda}(z)).

We denote the restriction of
\Gamma^{\lambda}
to
b\Omega
by
\rho^{\lambda}(s)
, where
s
is the arclength element on
b\Omega
. Then
dt^{\lambda}=(\rho^{\lambda})^{\prime}(s)ds
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
k
be a non-negative integer and
0<\mu<1
. Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

(i) For any
0<\mu^{\prime}<\mu
,
C^{k+\mu^{\prime}}(\overline{\Omega})
is relatively compact in
C^{k+\mu}(\overline{\Omega})
.

(ii) Let
\{f^{\lambda}\}
be a family of functions in the class
C^{k+\mu}(\overline{\Omega})
such that
|f^{\lambda}|_{k+\mu}
is uniformly bounded in
\lambda
, and
f^{\lambda}
is continuous in
\lambda
in the
|\cdot|_{0}
-norm. Then
f^{\lambda}
is continuous in
\lambda
in the
|\cdot|_{k+\mu^{\prime}}
-norm, for any
0<\mu^{\prime}<\mu
.

Proof.

(i) Taking
\varepsilon<\frac{\mu-\mu^{\prime}}{\mu}
(so that
\mu(1-\varepsilon)-\mu^{\prime}>0
), we have

\displaystyle\|g_{n}\|_{k+\mu^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{x,y\in\Omega}\frac{|D^{k}g_{n}(x)-D^{k}g_{n}(y)|}{|x-y|^{\mu^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x,y\in\Omega}\frac{|D^{k}g_{n}(x)-D^{k}g_{n}(y)|^{1-\varepsilon}}{|x-y|^{\mu^{\prime}}}\sup_{x,y\in\Omega}|D^{k}g_{n}(x)-D^{k}g_{n}(y)|^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\|g_{n}\|_{k+\mu,0}\right)\sup_{x,y\in\Omega}|x-y|^{\mu(1-\varepsilon)-\mu^{\prime}}|g_{n}|_{k}^{\varepsilon}\leq C\left(\|g_{n}\|_{k+\mu,0}\right)|g_{n}|_{k}^{\varepsilon},

where the constant
C
depends on
\Omega
,
\mu,\mu^{\prime},\varepsilon
. By the Ascoli-Arzela theorem, there exists a subsequence
\{g_{n_{j}}\}
which converges uniformly to some function
g_{\ast}
in the
|\cdot|_{k}
norm. Using the above estimates, the sequence
\{g_{n_{j}}\}
is Cauchy in the
|\cdot|_{k+\mu^{\prime}}
norm, hence must converge to some
C^{k+\mu^{\prime}}(\overline{\Omega})
function, which is just
g_{\ast}
.

(ii) Seeking contradiction, suppose there exists some
\lambda_{0}
and a sequence
\lambda_{j}\to\lambda_{0}
such that
|f^{\lambda_{j}}-f^{\lambda_{0}}|_{k+\mu^{\prime}}\geq\delta>0
. By part (i) and passing to a subsequence if necessary,
f^{\lambda_{j}}
converges to some function
\widehat{f}
in the
|\cdot|_{k+\mu^{\prime}}
-norm, and thus

(2.8)

|\widehat{f}-f^{\lambda_{0}}|_{k+\mu^{\prime}}\geq\delta>0.

On the other hand, we have

|\widehat{f}-f^{\lambda_{0}}|_{0}\leq|\widehat{f}-f^{\lambda_{j}}|_{0}+|f^{\lambda_{j}}-f^{\lambda_{0}}|_{0}.

The right-hand side converges to
0
by assumption, so
\widehat{f}\equiv f^{\lambda_{0}}
, which contradicts (
2.8
). Hence
f^{\lambda}
is continuous in
\lambda
in the
|\cdot|_{k+\mu^{\prime}}
norm.
∎

The following result shows that the definition
\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega}_{\Gamma})
is independent of the embedding
\Gamma^{\lambda}
. For this reason we shall from now on write
\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
instead of
\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega}_{\Gamma})
.

Proposition 2.4

(
[
1
, Lemma 2.2]
)
.

Let
k,j,\mu
be as in Theorem
1.1
.
Let
\overline{\Omega}
be a bounded domain with
b\Omega\in C^{k+\mu,j}\cap C^{1}
. Let
\Gamma^{\lambda}_{1}
and
\Gamma^{\lambda}_{2}
be two embeddings from
\overline{\Omega}
to
\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}}
.
If
\Gamma_{i}^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})\cap\mathcal{C}^{1,0}(\overline{\Omega})
. Then

\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega}_{\Gamma_{1}})=\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega}_{\Gamma_{2}})
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
k,j,\mu
be as in Theorem
1.1
. Let
\Omega,\Omega^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and
F:\overline{\Omega}\times[0,1]\to\overline{\Omega^{\prime}}\times[0,1]
be a
\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}
-mapping, defined by

F(x,y,\lambda)=(f_{1}(x,y,\lambda),f_{2}(x,y,\lambda),\lambda):=(\xi,\eta,\tau).

Suppose that
f(\cdot,\cdot,\lambda)=(f_{1}(\cdot,\cdot,\lambda),f_{2}(\cdot,\cdot,\lambda))
are bijections from
\overline{\Omega}
to
\overline{\Omega^{\prime}}
for each fixed
\lambda
, and the Jacobian matrix
DF
has non-vanishing determinant in
\overline{\Omega}\times[0,1]
. Then
F^{-1}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\prime}}\times[0,1])
.

The proof can be done by induction on
k,j
. The details are left to the reader.

Proposition 2.6

(
[
1
, Corollary 9.4]
)
.

Let
k,j,\mu
be as in Theorem
1.1
. Let
\Omega
be a simply-connected domain in
\mathbb{C}
with
b\Omega\in C^{k+1+\mu}
, and let
\Gamma^{\lambda}:\overline{\Omega}\to\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}}
be in the class
\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
. Then there exists a family of Riemann mappings
R^{\lambda}:\Omega^{\lambda}\to\mathbb{D}
such that
\widetilde{R}(z,\lambda):=R^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda}(z)\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega}).

Proposition 2.7
.

Keep the assumptions in the last theorem. Let
R^{\lambda}
be the family of Riemann mappings in Proposition
2.6
. For each
\lambda\in[0,1]
, let
(R^{\lambda})^{-1}:\mathbb{D}\to\Omega^{\lambda}
be the inverse of
R^{\lambda}
. Then
(R^{\lambda})^{-1}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
.

Proof.

Define
F\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega}\times[0,1]):\overline{\Omega}\times[0,1]\to\overline{\mathbb{D}}\times[0,1]
by

F(z,\lambda)=(\widetilde{R}(z,\lambda),\lambda):=(R(\Gamma(z,\lambda),\lambda),\lambda).

Since
\Gamma(\cdot,\lambda)
is an embedding from
\Omega
to
\Omega^{\lambda}
, and
R(\cdot,\lambda)
is a Riemann mapping, the map
\widetilde{R}
is a bijection for each fixed
\lambda
, and its Jacobian is invertible. It follows that the Jacobian of
F
is invertible (when
j\geq 0
.) By Lemma (
2.5
),
F
is invertible and the inverse
F^{-1}
is in
\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\mathbb{D}}\times[0,1])
.
F^{-1}
is given by

F^{-1}(w,\tau)=(\widetilde{R}^{-1}(w,\tau),\tau).

Write
R^{-1}(z,\lambda)=\Gamma(\widetilde{R}^{-1}(z,\lambda),\lambda)
.
Since
\Gamma\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
, and
\widetilde{R}^{-1}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
, we obtain
R^{-1}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
.
∎

In order to reduce our oblique derivative boundary value problem to Problem A for
\overline{\partial}
, we need to change coordinates to reduce the second-order terms in
\mathcal{L}u
in the elliptic operator to Laplacian. This is the classical problem of finding isothermal coordinate on the plane. We state some of the results which are well-known.

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\mathbb{C}
, and consider the following elliptic equation defined on
\Omega
:

(2.9)

a(x,y)u_{xx}+2b(x,y)u_{xy}+c(x,y)u_{yy}+F\left(x,y,u,u_{x},u_{y}\right)=0,

where the derivatives are taken in the sense of distributions. We assume the following uniform ellipticity condition:

d=ac-b^{2}\geq d_{0}>0\quad\text{a.e. in $\Omega$. }

(i) Suppose
\Omega
has Lipschitz boundary, and
a,b,c\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
, for
2<p<\infty
. Then there exists a 1-1 map
\psi:z=x+iy\mapsto w=\xi+i\eta
on
\Omega
such that
\psi\in W^{2,p}(\Omega)\subset C^{1+\alpha_{p}}(\overline{\Omega})
, for
\alpha_{p}:=\frac{p-2}{p}
. Moreover, if we set
u(x,y)=\widetilde{u}(\psi(x,y))
, then
u
is a solution to equation (
2.9
) if and only if
\widetilde{u}
is a solution to the following equation

\widetilde{u}_{\xi\xi}+\widetilde{u}_{\eta\eta}+F_{1}\left(\xi,\eta,\widetilde{u},\widetilde{u}_{\xi},\widetilde{u}_{\eta}\right)=0

on the domain
\psi(\Omega)
.

(ii) For
k\geq 0
and
0<\mu<1
, let
\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\mathbb{C}
with
C^{k+1+\mu}
boundary. Suppose
a,b,c\in C^{k+1+\mu}(\overline{\Omega})
. Then the coordinate map
\psi
constructed in
(i)
belongs to the class
C^{k+2+\mu}(\overline{\Omega})
.

(iii) The Jacobian of the map
\psi
is positive at each point of
\Omega
:

J(z)=\left|\partial{}_{z}w\right|^{2}-\left|\partial{}_{\overline{z}}w\right|^{2}>0,\quad z\in\Omega.

The reader can refer to
[
7
]
Theorems 2.5, 2.9 and 2.12 for the proofs of above statements.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\mathbb{C}
with
C^{1}
boundary, and let
\psi
be the map from Proposition
2.8
. In particular
\psi\in C^{1}(\overline{\Omega})
. Then
\psi
defines a one-to-one onto homeomorphism from
\Omega
to
\Omega^{\prime}
. Let
I
be a non-vanishing vector field along
b\Omega
, and
\psi_{\ast}(I)
be the push-forward of
I
by
\psi
, so that
\psi_{\ast}
is a non-vanishing vector field along
b\Omega^{\prime}
. Then

[I]_{b\Omega}=[\psi_{\ast}I]_{b\Omega^{\prime}},

where
[I]_{b\Omega}
denotes the change in the argument of
I(z)
as
z
moves along
b\Omega
once in the positive direction.

The proof is left to the reader.
We now prove a result on the parameter version of isothermal coordinates:

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
k,j,\mu
be as in Theorem
1.1
.
Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\mathbb{C}
with
C^{k+2+\mu}
boundary, and
\Gamma^{\lambda}:\Omega\to\Omega^{\lambda}
be a family of embeddings in the class
\mathcal{C}^{k+2+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
, with
\Gamma^{0}
being the identity map.
Keep the assumptions in Theorem
1.1
. Consider the second-order elliptic equation with oblique derivative boundary condition:

(2.10)

\begin{gathered}a^{\lambda}(x,y)u^{\lambda}_{xx}+2b^{\lambda}(x,y)u^{\lambda}_{xy}+c^{\lambda}(x,y)u^{\lambda}_{yy}+d^{\lambda}(x,y)u^{\lambda}_{x}+e^{\lambda}(x,y)u^{\lambda}_{y}=f^{\lambda}(x,y),\text{in $\Omega^{\lambda}$.}\\
\alpha^{\lambda}u_{x}^{\lambda}+\beta^{\lambda}u_{y}^{\lambda}=\gamma^{\lambda},\quad\text{on $b\Omega^{\lambda}$.}\end{gathered}

Suppose
a^{\lambda},b^{\lambda},c^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
,
d^{\lambda},e^{\lambda},f^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
,
\alpha^{\lambda},\beta^{\lambda},\gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
. Then for all
|\lambda|<\varepsilon_{0}
for some small
\varepsilon_{0}
, there exists a family of coordinate maps
\varphi^{\lambda}:\Omega^{\lambda}\to D^{\lambda}
with
\varphi^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
, and the functions
\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}(\tau^{\lambda}):=u^{\lambda}((\varphi^{\lambda})^{-1}(\tau^{\lambda}))
(
\tau^{\lambda}=s^{\lambda}+it^{\lambda}
) satisfies:

\displaystyle\Delta\,\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}+p^{\lambda}(s^{\lambda},t^{\lambda})\,\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}_{s^{\lambda}}+q^{\lambda}(s^{\lambda},t^{\lambda})\,\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}_{t^{\lambda}}=h^{\lambda}(s^{\lambda},t^{\lambda}),\quad\text{in $D^{\lambda}$, }

\displaystyle\nu_{1}^{\lambda}\,\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}_{s}+\nu_{2}^{\lambda}\,\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}_{t}=g^{\lambda}(s^{\lambda},t^{\lambda}),\quad\text{on $bD^{\lambda}$, }

where
p^{\lambda},q^{\lambda},h^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{D^{\lambda}})
,
\nu_{1}^{\lambda},\nu_{2}^{\lambda},g^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{D^{\lambda}})
.
Moreover, the winding number of the vector field
\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}=(\alpha^{\lambda},\beta^{\lambda})
along
b\Omega^{\lambda}
is the same as the winding number of
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\lambda}=(\nu_{1}^{\lambda},\nu_{2}^{\lambda})
along
bD^{\lambda}
.

Proof.

First we pull back (
2.10
) by the maps
\Gamma^{\lambda}
to the fixed domain
\Omega
. Let
v^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta):=u^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta)
. Then
v^{\lambda}
satisfies

(2.11)

\displaystyle\widetilde{a}^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta)v^{\lambda}_{\xi\xi}+2\widetilde{b}^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta)v^{\lambda}_{\xi\eta}+\widetilde{c}^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta)v^{\lambda}_{\eta\eta}+\widetilde{d}^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta)v^{\lambda}_{\xi}+\widetilde{e}^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta)v^{\lambda}_{\eta}=\widetilde{f}^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta),\;\text{in $\Omega$.}

(2.12)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\alpha}^{\lambda}v_{\xi}^{\lambda}+\widetilde{\beta}^{\lambda}v_{\eta}^{\lambda}=\widetilde{\gamma}^{\lambda},\quad\text{on $b\Omega$.}

The second-order coefficients
\widetilde{a}^{\lambda},\widetilde{b}^{\lambda},\widetilde{c}^{\lambda}
are given by

(2.13)

\begin{gathered}\widetilde{a}^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta)=(a^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda})(\xi^{\lambda}_{x})^{2}+2(b^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda})\xi^{\lambda}_{x}\xi^{\lambda}_{y}+(c^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda})(\xi^{\lambda}_{y})^{2},\\
\widetilde{b}^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta)=(a^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda})\xi^{\lambda}_{x}\eta^{\lambda}_{x}+(b^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda})\left(\xi^{\lambda}_{x}\eta^{\lambda}_{y}+\xi^{\lambda}_{y}\eta^{\lambda}_{x}\right)+(c^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda})(\xi^{\lambda}_{y}\eta^{\lambda}_{y}),\\
\widetilde{c}^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta)=(a^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda})(\eta^{\lambda}_{x})^{2}+2(b^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda})\eta^{\lambda}_{x}\eta^{\lambda}_{y}+(c^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda})(\eta^{\lambda}_{y})^{2}.\end{gathered}

Hence
\widetilde{a}^{\lambda},\widetilde{b}^{\lambda},\widetilde{c}^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
. The first-order coefficients
\widetilde{d}^{\lambda},\widetilde{e}^{\lambda}
are linear combinations of the products of
a^{\lambda},b^{\lambda},c^{\lambda},d^{\lambda},e^{\lambda}
and the first and second derivatives of
(\Gamma^{\lambda})^{-1}
, and hence
\widetilde{d}^{\lambda},\widetilde{e}^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
. Since
\widetilde{f}^{\lambda}=f^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda}
, we have
\widetilde{f}^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
.

For the boundary coefficients, we have

\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\alpha}^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta)\\
\widetilde{\beta}^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial(\Gamma^{\lambda})^{-1}}{\partial x}(\Gamma^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta))&\frac{\partial(\Gamma^{\lambda})^{-1}}{\partial y}(\Gamma^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta))\\
\frac{\partial(\Gamma^{\lambda})^{-1}}{\partial x}(\Gamma^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta))&\frac{\partial(\Gamma^{\lambda})^{-1}}{\partial y}(\Gamma^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta))\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha^{\lambda}(\Gamma^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta))\\
\beta^{\lambda}(\Gamma^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta))\end{pmatrix},

or put it differently,
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\lambda}=(\Gamma^{\lambda})^{-1}_{\ast}(\mathcal{U}^{\lambda})
.
It follows that
\widetilde{\alpha}^{\lambda},\widetilde{\beta}^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
, and
\widetilde{\gamma}^{\lambda}=\gamma^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
.
Since
\Gamma^{0}
is the identity map, the above matrix is close to the identity matrix (in matrix norm) when
\lambda
is close to
0
. Consequently we observe that for
|\lambda|
small, the winding number of the vector field
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\lambda}(\widetilde{\alpha}^{\lambda},\widetilde{\beta}^{\lambda})
along
b\Omega
is the same as that of
(\alpha^{\lambda},\beta^{\lambda})
along
b\Omega^{\lambda}
:

\frac{1}{2\pi}[\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\lambda}]_{b\Omega}=\frac{1}{2\pi}[\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}]_{b\Omega^{\lambda}}=\chi_{0}.

Since
\Gamma^{0}
is the identity map, by (
2.13
) we have
\widetilde{a}^{0}=a^{0}
,
\widetilde{b}^{0}=b^{0}
and
\widetilde{c}^{0}=c^{0}
. Therefore for
|\lambda|<\varepsilon
, we have
\widetilde{d}^{\lambda}:=\widetilde{a}^{\lambda}\widetilde{c}^{\lambda}-(\widetilde{b}^{\lambda})^{2}\geq\varepsilon_{0}>0
, in other words equation (
2.11
) is uniformly elliptic for
\lambda
close to
0
.
Applying Proposition
2.8
for
\lambda=0
, we get a map
\Psi_{0}:\Omega=\Omega^{0}\to D^{0}
, such that
\Psi_{0}\in C^{k+2+\mu}(\overline{\Omega_{0}})
. Writing
\Psi_{0}(\xi,\eta)=(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
, one can transform equations (
2.11
) and (
2.12
) to

(2.14)

\displaystyle\Delta\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}+a_{0}^{\lambda}\mathcal{V}_{\xi^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}}^{\lambda}+2b_{0}^{\lambda}\mathcal{V}_{\xi^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}^{\lambda}+c_{0}^{\lambda}\mathcal{V}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}^{\lambda}+d_{0}^{\lambda}\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{\xi^{\prime}}+e_{0}^{\lambda}\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{\eta^{\prime}}=f_{0}^{\lambda},\quad\text{in $D^{0}$; }

(2.15)

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}^{\lambda}\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{\xi^{\prime}}+\beta_{0}^{\lambda}\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{\xi^{\prime}}=\gamma_{0}^{\lambda},\quad\text{on $bD^{0}$.}

Here
\mathcal{V}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})=v\circ\Psi_{0}^{-1}(\xi^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
,
a_{0}^{0}=b_{0}^{0}=c_{0}^{0}=0
, and
|a_{0}^{\lambda}|,|b_{0}^{\lambda}|,|c_{0}^{\lambda}|<\varepsilon
if
|\lambda|<\delta_{0}
for some small positive
\delta_{0}
.
By expressions similar to (
2.13
), we see that the second-order coefficients
a_{0}^{\lambda},b_{0}^{\lambda},c_{0}^{\lambda}
are linear combinations of the products of
\widetilde{a}^{\lambda}\circ\Psi^{-1}_{0},\widetilde{b}^{\lambda}\circ\Psi^{-1}_{0},\widetilde{c}^{\lambda}\circ\Psi^{-1}_{0}
and the first space derivatives of
\Psi_{0}
, and thus they are in the class
\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{D_{0}})
. The first-order coefficients
d_{0}^{\lambda},e_{0}^{\lambda}
are linear combinations of products of
\widetilde{a}^{\lambda}\circ\Psi^{-1}_{0},\widetilde{b}^{\lambda}\circ\Psi^{-1}_{0},\widetilde{c}^{\lambda}\circ\Psi^{-1}_{0},\widetilde{d}^{\lambda}\circ\Psi^{-1}_{0},\widetilde{e}^{\lambda}\circ\Psi^{-1}_{0}
and the first and second derivatives of
\Psi_{0}
, so
d_{0}^{\lambda},e_{0}^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{D_{0}})
.
In a similar way
f^{\lambda}_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{D^{0}})
and
\alpha_{0}^{\lambda},\beta_{0}^{\lambda},\gamma_{0}^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{D^{0}})
.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{0}^{\lambda}=(\alpha^{\lambda}_{0},\beta^{\lambda}_{0})
. Then
\mathcal{U}_{0}^{\lambda}=(\Psi_{0})_{\ast}\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\lambda}
.
By Lemma
2.9
, the winding number of
\mathcal{U}_{0}^{\lambda}=(\alpha^{\lambda}_{0},\beta^{\lambda}_{0})
is the same as the winding number of
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\lambda}
:
\frac{1}{2\pi}[\mathcal{U}_{0}^{\lambda}]_{bD^{0}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}[\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}^{\lambda}]_{b\Omega}=\chi_{0}
.
Rewrite equation (
2.14
) as

(2.16)

a_{1}^{\lambda}\mathcal{V}_{\xi^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}}^{\lambda}+2b_{0}^{\lambda}\mathcal{V}_{\xi^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}^{\lambda}+c_{1}^{\lambda}\mathcal{V}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}^{\lambda}+d_{0}^{\lambda}\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{\xi^{\prime}}+e_{0}^{\lambda}\mathcal{V}^{\lambda}_{\eta^{\prime}}=f_{0}^{\lambda},\quad\text{in $D^{0}$,}

where
a_{1}^{\lambda}=1+a_{0}^{\lambda}
and
c_{1}^{\lambda}=1+c_{0}^{\lambda}
. For
|\lambda|<\delta_{1}
, the determinant for the above equation satisfies

d_{0}^{\lambda}=a_{1}^{\lambda}c_{1}^{\lambda}-2b_{0}^{\lambda}\geq\varepsilon_{1}>0.

By above we have that
a_{1}^{\lambda},b_{0}^{\lambda},c_{1}^{\lambda},d^{\lambda}_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(D^{0})
.
Let
\psi^{\lambda}:\zeta^{\prime}=\xi^{\prime}+i\eta^{\prime}\mapsto\tau^{\lambda}=s^{\lambda}+it^{\lambda}
be a family of coordinate systems that satisfies:

\tau^{\lambda}_{\overline{\zeta^{\prime}}}+q^{\lambda}(\zeta^{\prime})\tau^{\lambda}_{\zeta^{\prime}}=0,\quad q^{\lambda}=\frac{a_{1}^{\lambda}-\sqrt{d_{0}^{\lambda}}-ib_{0}^{\lambda}}{a_{1}^{\lambda}+\sqrt{d_{0}^{\lambda}}+ib_{0}^{\lambda}}.

Note that for
\lambda=0
, the map
\psi^{0}
is the identity map,
q^{0}\equiv 0
, and the above equation is trivially satisfied.

By Lemma
2.9
,
\psi^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+2+\mu,j}(\overline{D^{0}})
. Let
\psi^{\lambda}:D^{0}\to D^{\lambda}
.
Then (
2.16
) and (
2.15
) are transformed into

\displaystyle\Delta\,\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}+p^{\lambda}(s^{\lambda},t^{\lambda})\,\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}_{s^{\lambda}}+q^{\lambda}(s^{\lambda},t^{\lambda})\,\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}_{t^{\lambda}}=h^{\lambda}(s^{\lambda},t^{\lambda}),\quad\text{in $D^{\lambda}$;}

\displaystyle\nu_{1}^{\lambda}\,\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}_{s}+\nu_{2}^{\lambda}\,\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}_{t}=g^{\lambda}(s^{\lambda},t^{\lambda}),\quad\text{on $bD^{\lambda}$, }

where
h^{\lambda}(s^{\lambda},t^{\lambda})=\frac{J^{\lambda}f^{\lambda}_{0}}{4\sqrt{d_{0}^{\lambda}}}
, and

\displaystyle p^{\lambda}=\frac{J^{\lambda}}{4\sqrt{d_{0}^{\lambda}}}\left(1+a_{1}^{\lambda}s^{\lambda}_{\xi^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}}+2b_{0}^{\lambda}s^{\lambda}_{\xi^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}+c_{1}^{\lambda}s^{\lambda}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}+d_{0}^{\lambda}s^{\lambda}_{\xi^{\prime}}+e^{\lambda}_{0}s^{\lambda}_{\eta^{\prime}}\right),

\displaystyle q^{\lambda}=\frac{J^{\lambda}}{4\sqrt{d_{0}^{\lambda}}}\left(1+a_{1}^{\lambda}t^{\lambda}_{\xi^{\prime}\xi^{\prime}}+2b_{0}^{\lambda}t^{\lambda}_{\xi^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}+c_{1}^{\lambda}t^{\lambda}_{\eta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}+d_{0}^{\lambda}t^{\lambda}_{\xi^{\prime}}+e^{\lambda}_{0}t^{\lambda}_{\eta^{\prime}}\right).

Hence
p^{\lambda},q^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{D^{\lambda}})
. Since
f^{\lambda}_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{D^{0}})
, we have
h^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{D^{\lambda}})
. Similarly it is easy to see
\nu_{1}^{\lambda},\nu_{2}^{\lambda},g^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{D^{\lambda}})
.
Let
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\lambda}=(\nu_{1}^{\lambda},\nu_{2}^{\lambda})
. Then
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\lambda}=\psi^{\lambda}_{\ast}(\mathcal{U}_{0}^{\lambda})
. Since
\psi^{0}_{\ast}
is the identity matrix, we see that the winding number of
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\lambda}
is the same as that of
\mathcal{U}_{0}^{\lambda}
for
|\lambda|
small:

\frac{1}{2\pi}[\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}]_{b\Omega^{\lambda}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}[\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\lambda}]_{bD^{\lambda}}=\chi_{0}.

We now put together the series of transformation from earlier

\varphi^{\lambda}=\psi^{\lambda}\circ\Psi^{0}\circ(\Gamma^{\lambda})^{-1}:\Omega^{\lambda}\longrightarrow D^{\lambda}.

Since
\Gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+2+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
,
\Psi^{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+2+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega_{0}})
, and
\psi^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+2+\mu,j}(\overline{D_{0}})
, we have
\varphi^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda}:\Omega\to D^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+2+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
.
∎

3.
A review of Vekua’s theory on fixed domains

In this section we review Vekua’s theory for solving oblique derivative boundary value problem. First we introduce Problem A (as in
[
7
]
) on a fixed domain.

Problem A
(Generalized Riemann-Hilbert problem).
Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\mathbb{C}
. Given functions
A,B,F
defined in
\Omega
and
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
defined on
b\Omega
. Find a solution
w(z)=u(z)+iv(z)
of the equation

(3.1)

\begin{cases}\mathcal{L}_{A,B}(w):\equiv\partial{}_{\overline{z}}w+A(z)w(z)+B(z)\overline{w(z)}=F(z)\quad\text{in $\Omega$;}\\
\alpha u+\beta v\equiv Re[\overline{l(z)}w]=\gamma\quad\text{on $b\Omega$, }\end{cases}

where
l:=\alpha+i\beta
and is assumed to have unit length.

Definition 3.1
.

The
index of Problem A
, denoted by
n
, is the winding number of
l
along
b\Omega
:

n:=\frac{1}{2\pi}[l]_{b\Omega}.

Here we denote by
[l]_{b\Omega}
the change in the argument of
l(t)
as
t
loops around the boundary
b\Omega
once counterclockwise.

For now let us assume that

(3.2)

\displaystyle b\Omega\in C^{1+\mu},\quad A,B,F\in L^{p}(\Omega),\quad 2<p<\infty;\quad l,\gamma\in C^{\mu}(\overline{\Omega}),\quad 0<\mu<1.

A priori, we seek a solution
w
in the class
C^{0}(\overline{\Omega})
.

First let us consider the case when
\Omega
is simply-connected. By the Riemann-mapping theorem it suffices to assume
\Omega=\mathbb{D}
. In this case
n=\frac{1}{2\pi}[l]_{S^{1}}
, where
S^{1}
is the boundary circle.
We now reduce the problem to a simpler form.
Set
q(t)=-\arg(l(t))+n\,\arg(t)
. Then
q
is a well-defined function on
S^{1}
and
q\in C^{\mu}(S^{1})
. We have

\displaystyle\overline{l(z)}

\displaystyle=e^{-i\arg(l(z))}=e^{p(z)+i(-\arg(l(z))+n\arg z)}e^{-p(z)}e^{-i(n\arg(z))}=e^{\chi(z)}e^{-p(z)}z^{-n}.

Here
\chi
is the holomorphic function in
\mathbb{D}
taking the value
p+iq
on
S^{1}
. It is defined by the Schwarz integral:

(3.3)

\chi(z):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{S^{1}}q(t)\frac{t+z}{t-z}\frac{dt}{t},\quad\quad z\in\mathbb{D}.

By Proposition
7.11
,
\chi\in C^{\mu}(\overline{\Omega})
.
Using the substitutions

(3.4)

\overline{l(z)}=z^{-n}e^{\chi(z)}e^{-p(z)},\quad w_{\ast}(z)=e^{\chi(z)}w(z),

w_{\ast}
is then a solution to the problem

(3.5)

\begin{cases}\partial{}_{\overline{z}}w_{\ast}+A_{\ast}w_{\ast}+B_{\ast}\overline{w_{\ast}}=F_{\ast},\quad\text{in $\mathbb{D}$; }\\
Re[z^{-n}w_{\ast}]=\gamma_{\ast},\quad\text{on $S^{1}$,}\end{cases}

where

(3.6)

A_{\ast}=A,\quad B_{\ast}=Be^{2iIm\,\chi(z)},\quad F_{\ast}=Fe^{\chi(z)},\quad\gamma_{\ast}(z)=\gamma(z)e^{p(z)}\quad\text{in $\mathbb{D}$.}

Conversely, given any solution
w_{\ast}
to the problem (
3.5
), a solution to the original problem (
3.1
) is given by

w(z)=e^{-\chi(z)}w_{\ast}(z)
. From now on we can assume the problem takes the form (
3.5
), and we write
w,A,B,F,\gamma
without the
\ast
.

By assumption
\partial{}_{\overline{z}}w\in L^{1}(\overline{\Omega})
and Proposition
7.4
,
w
satisfies the integral equation

(3.7)

w+T_{\mathbb{D}}(Aw+B\overline{w})=\Phi+T_{\mathbb{D}}F,\quad\text{in $\mathbb{D}$,}

where
\Phi
is some function holomorphic in
\mathbb{D}
.
Now in view of Proposition
7.2
, any continuous solution
w
of the above equation satisfies

\mathcal{L}_{A,B}(w)=F(z).

We would like to choose
\Phi
in such a way that the solution to equation (
3.7
) also satisfies the boundary condition
Re[z^{-n}w]=\gamma
on
S^{1}
, where
n
is the index of (
3.1
). Then such solution will solve the original Problem A.

First suppose
n\geq 0
. We write
\Phi
in the form

(3.8)

\displaystyle\Phi(z)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{0}(z)+\frac{z^{2n+1}}{\pi}\iint_{\mathbb{D}}\frac{\overline{A(\zeta)w(\zeta)}+\overline{B(\zeta)}w(\zeta)}{1-\overline{\zeta}z}\,dA(\zeta)-\frac{z^{2n+1}}{\pi}\iint_{\mathbb{D}}\frac{\overline{F(\zeta)}}{1-\overline{\zeta}z}\,dA(\zeta),

where
\Phi_{0}
is some function to be determined, and it is holomorphic in
\mathbb{D}
. Substituting (
3.8
) into equation (
3.7
), we obtain the following integral equation for
w
:

(3.9)

w(z)+P_{n}(Aw+B\overline{w})=\Phi_{0}(z)+P_{n}F,

where
P_{n}
is the operator defined by:

(3.10)

P_{n}\varphi(z):=-\frac{1}{\pi}\iint_{\mathbb{D}}\left(\frac{\varphi(\zeta)}{\zeta-z}+\frac{z^{2n+1}\overline{\varphi(\zeta)}}{1-\overline{\zeta}z}\right)\,dA(\zeta).

The integral equation (
3.9
) is of Fredholm type, which we now describe.
Denoting by
\mathbb{D}^{c}
the exterior of the unit disk, we can write
P_{n}\varphi
in the form

(3.11)

P_{n}\varphi(z)=T_{\mathbb{D}}\varphi(z)+z^{2n+1}T_{\mathbb{D}^{c}}\varphi_{1}(z),\quad\varphi_{1}(\zeta)=\zeta^{-1}(\overline{\zeta})^{-2}\overline{\varphi(\overline{\zeta}^{-1})}.

If
\varphi\in L^{p}(\mathbb{D}),2<p\leq\infty
,
\varphi_{1}
satisfies

\int_{\mathbb{D}}\left|\varphi_{1}\left(\zeta^{-1}\right)\right|^{p}|\zeta|^{-2p}dA(\zeta)=\int_{\mathbb{D}}\left|\varphi\left(\zeta\right)\right|^{p}|\zeta|^{3p-2p}dA(\zeta)<\infty.

According to Proposition
7.5
,
T_{\mathbb{D}^{c}}\varphi_{1}
is
\alpha_{p}
-Hölder continuous in the entire plane, for
\alpha_{p}=\frac{p-2}{p}
. By Proposition
7.3
the function
T_{\mathbb{D}}\varphi
is
\alpha_{p}
-Hölder continuous in the plane. Hence from (
3.11
)
P_{n}\varphi
is
\alpha_{p}
-Hölder continuous. It is easy to check that

Re[z^{-n}P_{n}\varphi]=0
on
S^{1}
.
By assumption,
F\in L^{p}(\mathbb{D})
for
2<p<\infty
, so by letting
F
be the
\varphi
above,
we see that
P_{n}F
is Hölder continuous in the entire plane, and
Re[z^{-n}P_{n}F(z)]=0
on
S^{1}
. As we will see below, the solution
w
is Hölder continuous in
\overline{\mathbb{D}}
, and thus
Aw+B\overline{w}\in L^{p}(\mathbb{D})
, for
p>2
and
Re[z^{-n}P_{n}(Aw+B\overline{w})]=0
on
S^{1}
.

Now by (
3.9
), if the holomorphic function
\Phi_{0}
satisfies the boundary condition

(3.12)

Re[z^{-n}\Phi_{0}(z)]=\gamma\quad\quad\text{on $S^{1}$,}

then the solution of the integral equation (
3.9
) is a solution of the boundary value problem (
3.5
).

By Proposition
7.19
, the general solution of the problem (
3.12
) is given by

\Phi_{0}(z)=\frac{z^{n}}{2\pi i}\int_{S^{1}}\gamma(t)\frac{t+z}{t-z}\frac{dt}{t}+\sum_{k=0}^{2n}c_{k}z^{k},

where the
c_{k}
’s are complex numbers satisfying the relations

(3.13)

c_{2n-k}=-\overline{c_{k}},\quad k=0,1,\dots,n.

Thus for
n\geq 0
, the problem is reduced to the equivalent integral equation of Fredholm type:

(3.14)

w+Q_{n}w=P_{n}F+\frac{z^{n}}{2\pi i}\int_{S^{1}}\gamma(t)\frac{t+z}{t-z}\frac{dt}{t}+\sum_{k=0}^{2n}c_{k}z^{k},

(see
[
7
, p. 225, (1.10)]
), where operator
Q_{n}
and
c_{k}
are defined by

(3.15)

\displaystyle Q_{n}w:=P_{n}(Aw+B\overline{w}),

(3.16)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{2n}c_{k}z^{k}=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}a_{k}(z^{k}-z^{2n-k})+ib_{k}(z^{k}+z^{2n-k})+ic_{0}z^{n},

with
a_{k},b_{k},c_{0}
being arbitrary real constants.
As shown earlier,
P_{n}F\in C^{\alpha_{p}}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
for
\alpha_{p}=\frac{p-2}{p}
. The second term on the right-hand side of equation (
3.14
) can be written as
z^{n}\mathcal{S}\gamma
, where
\mathcal{S}
is the Schwarz integral operator.
By Proposition
7.11
applied to
\gamma\in C^{\mu}(S^{1})
, one has
\mathcal{S}\gamma\in C^{\mu}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
. Hence the right-hand side of the equation lies in the class
C^{\nu}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
, for
\nu:=\min(\alpha_{p},\mu)
.

It is shown in
[
7
, p. 296]
that equation (
3.14
) has a unique solution
w
in the class
L^{q}(\mathbb{D})
,
\frac{p}{p-1}<q<\infty
, for any right-hand side function in the class
L^{q}(\mathbb{D})
.
By Theorem 1.25 in
[
7
, p. 50]
, one has
Q_{n}w\in C^{0}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
, and so it follows from equation (
3.14
) that
w\in C^{0}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
.
By Proposition
7.3
and Proposition
7.5
applied to expression (
3.11
) where we replace
\varphi
by
w
, we have
Q_{n}w\in C^{\alpha_{p}}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
. By equation (
3.14
) again we obtain that
w\in C^{\nu}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
,
\nu:=\min(\alpha_{p},\mu)
.

Therefore when the index
n\geq 0
, the solution to the non-homogeneous Problem A exists for any boundary data, and the homogeneous Problem A (
F\equiv 0
,
\gamma\equiv 0
) admits
2n+1

\mathbb{R}
-linearly independent solutions.

If
n<0
. Referring to
[
7
, p. 302]
, the solution to Problem A (see (
3.1
)) exists if and only if the following conditions are satisfied:

(3.17)

\frac{1}{2i}\int_{b\Omega}l(t)w^{\prime}_{i}(t)\gamma(t)\,dt-Re\iint_{\Omega}w^{\prime}_{i}(z)F(z)\,dx\,dy=0,

for
i=1,\dots,2k-1
,
k=-n
, and
(w^{\prime}_{1}),\dots,(w^{\prime}_{2k-1})
are linearly-independent solutions of the homogeneous adjoint problem
A^{\prime}
:

(3.18)

\begin{gathered}\partial_{\overline{z}}w-Aw-\overline{Bw}=0,\quad\text{in $\Omega$; }\quad Re[l(z)z^{\prime}(s)w(z)]=0,\quad\text{ on $b\Omega$. }\end{gathered}

Here we write
z^{\prime}(s)=\frac{dz(s)}{ds}
. In this case if the solution for Problem A (
3.1
) exists, then it is unique, and the solution can be expressed as the unique solution to the integral equation (see
[
7
, p. 300]
):

(3.19)

w+(Q_{k}^{\ast})w=P_{k}^{\ast}F+\frac{1}{\pi i}\int_{b\Omega}\frac{\gamma(t)\,dt}{t^{k}(t-z)},\quad k=-n,

where
P_{k}^{\ast}
and
Q_{k}^{\ast}
are defined to be

(3.20)

\displaystyle P_{k}^{\ast}f:=-\frac{1}{\pi}\iint_{\Omega}\left(\frac{f(\zeta)}{\zeta-z}+\frac{\overline{\zeta}^{2k-1}\overline{f(\zeta)}}{1-z\overline{\zeta}}\right)\,dA(\zeta),\quad(Q_{k}^{\ast})f:=P_{k}^{\ast}(B\overline{f}).

The above method of reducing the problem to a Fredholm integral equation on the domain no longer applies for multiply-connected domains. The geometry of the circle allows for the vanishing of the term
Re[z^{-n}P_{n}\varphi]
on
S^{1}
. To deal with the general case, Vekua introduced the theory of generalized analytic functions. We state here a few of his results without much elaborations. The reader can refer to
[
7
]
for details.

We say a function
w
belongs to the class
\mathcal{L}_{p,2}(A,B,\mathbb{C})
if
w
satisfies

\mathcal{L}_{A,B}(w)=0

in the sense of distributions, for
A,B\in\mathcal{L}_{p,2}(\mathbb{C})
. (The latter is defined in (
2.2
).)
In our application,
A,B
are defined only on
\Omega
in which case we extend
A,B
trivially to
\mathbb{C}
by setting them to be
0
on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\overline{\Omega}
.

Following Vekua, we call such
w
a
generalized analytic function
of the class
\mathcal{L}_{p,2}(A,B,\mathbb{C})
.
For fixed
t\in\mathbb{C}
, and
A,B\in L_{p,2}(\mathbb{C})
, consider the following integral equations (
[
7
, p 167]
):

(3.21)

X_{i}(z,t)-\frac{1}{\pi}\iint_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{A(\zeta)X_{i}(\zeta,t)+B(\zeta)\overline{X_{i}(\zeta,t)}}{\zeta-z}\,dA(\zeta)=g_{i}(z),\quad i=1,2,

where
g_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(t-z)^{-1},g_{2}=\frac{1}{2i}(t-z)^{-1}
.
It is proved in
[
7
, p. 156]
that equation (
3.21
) admits a unique solution in the class
L_{q,0}(\mathbb{C})
if
A,B\in L_{p,2}(\mathbb{C})
and the right-hand side lies in the class
L_{q,0}(\mathbb{C})
, for
q\geq\frac{p}{p-1}=p^{\prime}
.
Since
2<p<\infty
, we have
1<p^{\prime}<2
, and
\frac{1}{t-z}\in L_{q,0}(\mathbb{C})
, for any
p^{\prime}\leq q<2
. Equation (
3.21
) admits solution
X_{i}\in L_{q,0}(\mathbb{C})
for any
q\in[p^{\prime},2)
. For
z\neq t
,
X_{i}
satisfies

\partial{}_{\overline{z}}X_{i}(z,t)+A(z)X_{i}(z,t)+B(z)\overline{X_{i}(z,t)}=0,\quad i=1,2.

Furthermore, we have the following representation for
X_{i}
:

(3.22)

X_{1}(z,t)=\frac{e^{\omega_{1}(z,t)}}{2(t-z)},\quad\quad X_{2}(z,t)=\frac{e^{\omega_{2}(z,t)}}{2i(t-z)},

where

(3.23)

\omega_{i}(z,t)=\frac{t-z}{\pi}\iint_{\mathbb{C}}\frac{1}{(\zeta-z)(t-\zeta)}\left[A(\zeta)+B(\zeta)\frac{\overline{X_{i}(\zeta,t)}}{X_{i}(\zeta,t)}\right]\,dA(\zeta);

(3.24)

|\omega_{i}(z_{1},t)-\omega_{i}(z_{2},t)|\leq C_{p}|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{\alpha_{p}},\quad\alpha_{p}=\frac{p-2}{p};

(3.25)

|\omega_{i}(z,t)|\leq C_{p}|z-t|^{\alpha_{p}},\quad i=1,2.

Hence by (
3.22
),
X_{j}(\cdot,t)
is Hölder continuous everywhere in the plane except at
z=t
.

Define
G_{i}
, the
fundamental kernels of the class

\mathcal{L}_{p,2}(A,B,\mathbb{C})
as follows:

(3.26)

\displaystyle G_{1}(z,\zeta)=X_{1}(z,\zeta)+iX_{2}(z,\zeta),\quad G_{2}(z,\zeta)=X_{1}(z,\zeta)-iX_{2}(z,\zeta).

By (
3.22
) we have

(3.27)

\displaystyle G_{1}(z,\zeta)=\frac{e^{\omega_{1}(z,\zeta)}+e^{\omega_{2}(z,\zeta)}}{2(\zeta-z)},\qquad G_{2}(z,\zeta)=\frac{e^{\omega_{1}(z,\zeta)}-e^{\omega_{2}(z,\zeta)}}{2(\zeta-z)}.

The
G_{i}^{\prime}s
satisfy the relation:

(3.28)

\partial{}_{\overline{z}}G_{i}(z,\zeta)+A(z)G_{i}(z,\zeta)+B(z)\overline{G_{i}(z,\zeta)}=0,\quad i=1,2,

for any
z,\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
,
z\neq\zeta
. In addition we have the estimates

(3.29)

G_{1}(z,\zeta)=\frac{1}{\zeta-z}+O(|z-\zeta|^{-\frac{2}{p}}),\quad G_{2}(z,\zeta)=O(|z-\zeta|^{-\frac{2}{p}}).

Hence
G_{1}
behaves like the Cauchy kernel, modulo a mild singularity.

Denote by
G_{1}^{\prime}(z,\zeta)
and
G_{2}^{\prime}(z,\zeta)
the adjoint fundamental kernel of the adjoint equation

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(w^{\prime})=\partial{}_{\overline{z}}w^{\prime}-Aw^{\prime}-\overline{B}\overline{w}^{\prime}=F^{\prime}
.
The following relations hold: (
[
7
, p.174]
)

(3.30)

G_{1}(z,\zeta)=-G_{1}^{\prime}(\zeta,z),\quad\quad G_{2}(z,\zeta)=-\overline{G_{2}^{\prime}(\zeta,z)}.

We will denote by
G_{1}(z,t,\Omega)
and
G_{2}(z,t,\Omega)
the fundamental kernels of the class
\mathcal{L}_{p,2}(A,B,\mathbb{C})
, if
A\equiv B\equiv 0
in
\Omega^{c}=\mathbb{C}\setminus\overline{\Omega}
. Given
A,B\in L^{p}(\Omega)
, we let
\widetilde{A},\widetilde{B}
be the trivial extensions which vanish outside
\Omega
.

For the parameter problem, we need the following representation formula.

Proposition 3.2
.

[
7
, p. 176]

Suppose
w
is continuous in
\overline{\Omega}
and satisfies the equation
\mathcal{L}_{A,B}(w)=F
in
\Omega
,
where
A,B,F\in L^{p}(\Omega),p>2
. Then
w
has the representation formula:

(3.31)

\displaystyle w(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{b\Omega}G_{1}(z,\zeta)w(\zeta)\,d\zeta-G_{2}(z,\zeta)\overline{w(\zeta)}\,\overline{d\zeta}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{\pi}\iint_{\Omega}G_{1}(z,\zeta)F(\zeta)+G_{2}(z,\zeta)\overline{F(\zeta)}\,dA(\zeta),\quad z\in\Omega.

Remark 3.3
.

When
A\equiv B\equiv 0
, by formula (
3.23
) we have
\omega_{1}(z,t)\equiv\omega_{2}(z,t)\equiv 0
. Hence from (
3.27
) we get
G_{1}=\frac{1}{\zeta-z}
and
G_{2}\equiv 0
, and (
3.31
) reduces to the Cauchy-Green formula.

Next we state some existence and uniqueness results for Problem A. For homogeneous boundary value problems, we call a finite set of linearly independent solutions which spans the solution space a
complete system
.

Proposition 3.4

(
[
7
, p. 253]
Theorem 4.10, Theorem 4.12.)
.

Consider Problem A on a multiply-connected domain
\Omega
whose boundary
b\Omega
has
m+1
connected components. Let
h,h^{\prime}
be the numbers of linearly-independent solutions to the homogeneous Problem A and the homogeneous adjoint Problem
A^{\prime}
(
3.18
), respectively. Let
n
be the index of Problem A.

(1)

Suppose that
n<0
. Then

h=0,\quad h^{\prime}=m-2n-1.

The non-homogeneous Problem A has a (unique) solution if and only if the following relations are satisfied:

(3.32)

\frac{1}{2i}\int_{b\Omega}l(t)w^{\prime}_{i}(t)\gamma(t)\,dt-Re\iint_{\Omega}w^{\prime}_{i}F(z)\,dxdy=0,

for
i=1,...,m-2n-1
, and
w_{1}^{\prime},...,w^{\prime}_{m-2n-1}
is a complete system of solutions of the adjoint homogeneous Problem
A^{\prime}
(
3.18
).

(2)

Suppose that
n>m-1
. Then

h=2n+1-m,\quad h^{\prime}=0.

The non-homogeneous Problem A is always solvable and its general solution is given by:

(3.33)

w(z)=w_{0}(z)+\sum_{j=1}^{2n+1-m}c_{j}w_{j},\quad\quad\text{$c_{j}$ are real constant, }

where
\{w_{1},...,w_{2n+1-m}\}
is a complete system of solutions of the homogeneous problem A, and
w_{0}
is a particular solution of the non-homogeneous problem A.

Remark 3.5
.

When the domain is simply-connected, we have
m=0
, and the above theorem reduces to the results we obtained earlier. When
0\leq n\leq m-1
(
m\geq 1
), the existence and uniqueness of solutions to Problem A are more subtle, and we refer the reader to
[
7
, chapter 4, §5]
.

From now on we only consider the case
n>m-1
. We can impose some additional conditions on the solution of Problem A to make it unique. This will be necessary when later on we add parameter since regularity in parameter depends on the uniqueness of these solutions.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\mathbb{C}
whose boundary
b\Omega
contains
m+1
connected components, for
m
a non-negative integer. Let
z_{1},...,z_{N_{0}}
and
z_{1}^{\prime},...,z_{N_{1}}^{\prime}
be fixed points of
\Omega
and its boundary
b\Omega
, respectively, satisfying the following conditions:

(i) The numbers
N_{0}
and
N_{1}
satisfy the relation:

2N_{0}+N_{1}=2n+1-m.

(ii) There are
m
curves, e.g.
\Gamma_{i_{1}},...,\Gamma_{i_{m}}
, among the
m+1
boundary curves
b\Omega_{0},...,b\Omega_{m}
, on each of which an odd number of
z_{s}^{\prime}
are situated.

Then we call
\{\{z_{r}\}_{r=1}^{N_{0}}
,
\{z_{s}^{\prime}\}_{s=1}^{N_{1}}\}
a
normally distributed set
, henceforth denoted by
(N_{0},N_{1},\overline{\Omega})
.

Remark 3.7
.

In our choice of
N_{0}
and
N_{1}
, two extreme cases are possible:

(1)

N_{0}=0
and
N_{1}=2n+1-m
.

(2)

N_{0}=n-m
and
N_{1}=m+1
.

Consequently, we have
0\leq N_{0}\leq n-m
,
m+1\leq N_{1}\leq 2n+1-m
.
If
m=0
, i.e. the domain is simply-connected, condition (i) becomes
2N_{0}+N_{1}=2n+1
, and thus in this case
N_{1}
needs to be odd.

We can specify on
(N_{0},N_{1},\overline{\Omega})
the values of the unknown solution of Problem A:

(3.34)

\begin{gathered}w(z_{r})=a_{r}+ib_{r},\quad r=1,...,N_{0};\quad w(z_{s}^{\prime})=l(z_{s}^{\prime})(\gamma(z_{s}^{\prime})+ic_{s}),\quad s=1,...,N_{1}.\end{gathered}

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
n
be the index of Problem A on a domain
\Omega
in
\mathbb{C}
, and let
m
be the number of connected components of
b\Omega
. Assume that on a normally distributed point set
(N_{0},N_{1},\overline{\Omega})
conditions of the form (
3.34
) are given. Then for
n>m-1
, Problem A has always a solution satisfying these conditions, and this solution is uniquely determined.

The reader can refer to Vekua
[
7
, p. 285 - 287]
for the proof.

The following regularity result for simply-connected domains follows from the above construction of the solution by Vekua, though it is not explicitly stated in his book. For the reader’s convenience we provide details of the proof here. Furthermore, the same estimates hold for multiply-connected domains and one can prove it using a trick of Vekua to reduce the simply-connected case (See
[
7
, p. 228, p. 336]
.)

Proposition 3.9
.

(i) Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
be a domain with
C^{1+\mu}
boundary, for
0<\mu<1
. Let
2<p<\infty
. Suppose
A,B,F\in L^{p}(\Omega)
,
l,\gamma\in C^{\mu}(b\Omega)
. Then the solution to Problem A (
3.1
), if it exists, belongs to the class
C^{\nu}(\overline{\Omega})
, for
\nu:=\text{min}\{\alpha_{p},\mu\}
,
\alpha_{p}=\frac{p-2}{p}
, and there exists a constant
C
depending on the coefficients
A,B,l,p
, such that

|w|_{\nu}\leq C(A,B,l,p)\left(|w|_{0}+|F|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+|\gamma|_{\mu}\right).

(ii) Let
k\geq 0
, and
0<\mu<1
. Let
\Omega
be a domain in
\mathbb{C}
with
C^{k+1+\mu}
boundary. Suppose
A,B,F\in C^{k+\mu}(\overline{\Omega})
,
l,\gamma\in C^{k+1+\mu}(b\Omega)
. Then the solution
w
of Problem A, if it exists, belongs to the class
C^{k+1+\mu}(\overline{\Omega})
, and there exists a constant
C_{k}
depending on the coefficients
A,B,l
such that

|w|_{k+1+\mu}\leq C_{k}(A,B,l)\left(|w|_{0}+|F|_{k+\mu}+|\gamma|_{k+1+\mu}\right).

We remark that the regularity results are in
[
7
, p. 228 and p. 336]
.
The main purpose of the following proof is to derive the above two estimates.
The constants cannot be controlled since the proof uses the open mapping theorem.

Proof.

We treat only the non-negative index case. The proof is similar if the index is negative.

(i) By the Riemann mapping theorem it suffices to take
\Omega=\mathbb{D}
. Moreover by the earlier remarks it suffices to assume that the Problem A has the form (
3.5
).
Let
\nu=\min(\alpha_{p},\mu)
,
\alpha_{p}=\frac{p-2}{p}
. In view of equation (
3.14
), any solution
w
has the form

(3.35)

w=w_{\ast}+\sum_{j=0}^{2n}d_{j}w_{j}

where
d_{j}
are complex constants and
w_{\ast}
is the (unique) solution to the integral equation

(3.36)

w_{\ast}+Q_{n}w_{\ast}=P_{n}F+\mathcal{S}(\gamma),

and for each
0\leq j\leq 2n
,
w_{j}
is the (unique) solution to the integral equation

(3.37)

w_{j}+Q_{n}w_{j}=g_{j},\quad 0\leq j\leq 2n,

where the
g_{j}
-s are the set of
2n+1
functions
\{z^{k}-z^{2n-k},z^{k}+z^{2n-k},z^{n}\}_{k=0}^{n-1}
from expression (
3.16
).

First we estimate
w_{\ast}
. Since the operator
I+Q_{n}
is a bijective bounded linear operator from the space
C^{0}(\overline{\Omega})
to itself, by the open mapping theorem it has a bounded inverse
(I+Q_{n})^{-1}
, whose operator norm depends only on
A
and
B
. Hence in view of Proposition
7.6
and Proposition
7.11
we have for
C_{i}=C_{i}(A,B)

\displaystyle|w_{\ast}|_{0}\leq C_{1}|P_{n}F+\mathcal{S}(\gamma)|_{0}\leq C_{2}\left(|P_{n}F|_{0}+|\mathcal{S}(\gamma)|_{0}\right)\leq C_{3}\left(|F|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{D})}+|\gamma|_{\mu}\right).

Next we estimate the Hölder norm of
w_{\ast}
. From (
3.36
) we have

\displaystyle|w_{\ast}|_{\nu}

\displaystyle\leq|Q_{n}w_{\ast}|_{\alpha_{p}}+|P_{n}F|_{\alpha_{p}}+|\mathcal{S}\gamma|_{\mu}.

By Proposition
7.6
we have

\displaystyle|Q_{n}w_{\ast}|_{\alpha_{p}}=|P_{n}(Aw_{\ast}+B\overline{w_{\ast}})|_{0}\leq C(p)|Aw_{\ast}+B\overline{w_{\ast}}|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{D})}\leq C(A,B,p)|w_{\ast}|_{0},

and

|P_{n}F|_{\alpha_{p}}\leq C(p)|F|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{D})}.

By Proposition
7.11
we have

|S\gamma|_{\mu}\leq C|\gamma|_{\mu}.

Hence

(3.38)

\displaystyle|w_{\ast}|_{\nu}

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}(A,B,p)\left(|w_{\ast}|_{0}+|F|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{D})}+|\gamma|_{\mu}\right)\leq C_{1}(A,B,p)\left(|F|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{D})}+|\gamma|_{\mu}\right).

We now estimate
w_{j}
.
Applying open mapping theorem to (
3.37
) we have

\displaystyle|w_{j}|_{0}\leq C_{j}(A,B),\quad 0\leq j\leq 2n;

\displaystyle|w_{j}|_{\alpha_{p}}\leq|Q_{n}w_{j}|_{\alpha_{p}}+|g_{j}|_{\alpha_{p}}\leq C(A,B,p)(|w_{j}|_{0}+1)\leq C_{j}(A,B,p).

It remains to estimate the coefficients
d_{j}
in (
3.35
). By the remarks in
[
7
, p. 286-288, equation (6.10)]
(see also Proposition
4.4
below), they can be expressed as
in the form
\frac{P}{Q}
, where
Q\neq 0
and
P
and
Q
are linear combinations of the products of
w_{j}(z_{r}),\>w_{j}(z_{s}^{\prime})
with terms like
a_{r},\>b_{r},\>c_{s},\>\gamma(z_{s}^{\prime}),\>w_{\ast}(z_{r}),\>w_{\ast}(z_{s}^{\prime})
. Since
|(a_{r},b_{r},c_{s})|\leq|w|_{0}
, we have

(3.39)

\displaystyle|d_{j}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{j}(A,B)\left(|w|_{0}+|\gamma|_{0}+|w_{\ast}|_{0}\right)\leq C_{j}(A,B)\left(|w|_{0}+|F|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{D})}+|\gamma|_{\mu}\right).

Hence by (
3.35
) and combining the above estimates we get

\displaystyle|w|_{\nu}

\displaystyle\leq|w_{\ast}|_{\nu}+\sum_{j=0}^{2n}|d_{j}||w_{j}|_{\alpha_{p}}\leq M(A,B,p)\left(|w|_{0}+|F|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{D})}+|\gamma|_{\mu}\right).

Finally, let
\widetilde{w}
be a solution to the original Problem A (
3.1
) on
\mathbb{D}
:

(3.40)

\begin{cases}\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{A},\widetilde{B}}(\widetilde{w})\equiv\partial{}_{\overline{z}}\widetilde{w}+\widetilde{A}(z)\widetilde{w}(z)+\widetilde{B}(z)\overline{\widetilde{w}(z)}=\widetilde{F}(z)\quad\text{in $\Omega$;}\\
\alpha u+\beta v\equiv Re[\overline{l(z)}\widetilde{w}]=\widetilde{\gamma}\quad\text{on $b\Omega$. }\end{cases}

Then we have the following relations:

(3.41)

\begin{gathered}A(z)=\widetilde{A}(z),\quad B(z)=\widetilde{B}e^{2i\chi(z)},\quad F(z)=\widetilde{F}(z)e^{\chi(z)},\>z\in\mathbb{D};\\
\widetilde{w}(z)=e^{-\chi(z)}w(z),\>z\in D,\quad\gamma(\zeta)=\widetilde{\gamma}(\zeta)e^{p(\zeta)},\>\zeta\in bS^{1},\end{gathered}

where
\chi
and
p=Re(\chi)
are related to
l
by formula (
3.3
). Hence

|\widetilde{w}|_{\nu}\leq C(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{B},l,p)\left(|\widetilde{w}|_{0}+|\widetilde{F}|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{D})}+|\widetilde{\gamma}|_{\mu}\right).

(ii) Let us first consider Problem A in the reduced form (
3.5
).
Write (
3.36
) as

(3.42)

w_{\ast}=-Q_{n}w_{\ast}+P_{n}F+\mathcal{S}(\gamma)

First let us prove the statement for
k=0
. We have

\displaystyle|w_{\ast}|_{1+\mu}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(|Q_{n}w_{\ast}|_{1+\mu}+|P_{n}F|_{1+\mu}+|\mathcal{S}\gamma|_{1+\mu}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(|P_{n}(Aw_{\ast}+B\overline{w_{\ast}})|_{1+\mu}+|P_{n}F|_{1+\mu}+|\mathcal{S}\gamma|_{1+\mu}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(|Aw_{\ast}+B\overline{w_{\ast}}|_{\mu}+|F|_{\mu}+|\gamma|_{1+\mu}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left\{\left(|A|_{\mu}+|B|_{\mu}\right)|w_{\ast}|_{\mu}+|F|_{\mu}+|\gamma|_{1+\mu}\right\}.

By (
3.38
), we get
|w_{\ast}|_{\mu}\leq C(A,B)\left(|F|_{\mu}+|\gamma|_{\mu}\right).

Hence

|w_{\ast}|_{1+\mu}\leq C_{0}(A,B)\left(|F|_{\mu}+|\gamma|_{1+\mu}\right).

For
k>0
we apply induction. By Propositions
7.7
and
7.11
applied to equation (
3.42
) one gets

\displaystyle|w_{\ast}|_{k+1+\mu}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(|Q_{n}w_{\ast}|_{k+1+\mu}+|P_{n}F|_{k+1+\mu}+|\mathcal{S}\gamma|_{k+1+\mu}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(|P_{n}(Aw_{\ast}+B\overline{w_{\ast}})|_{k+1+\mu}+|P_{n}F|_{k+1+\mu}+|\mathcal{S}\gamma|_{k+1+\mu}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\left(|Aw_{\ast}+B\overline{w_{\ast}}|_{k+\mu}+|F|_{k+\mu}+|\gamma|_{k+1+\mu}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\left\{\left(|A|_{k+\mu}+|B|_{k+\mu}\right)|w_{\ast}|_{k+\mu}+|F|_{k+\mu}+|\gamma|_{k+1+\mu}\right\}.

By the induction hypothesis, we have

|w_{\ast}|_{k+\mu}\leq C_{k-1}(A,B)\left(|F|_{k-1+\mu}+|\gamma|_{k+\mu}\right).

Hence

|w_{\ast}|_{k+1+\mu}\leq C_{k}(A,B)\left(|F|_{k+\mu}+|\gamma|_{k+1+\mu}\right).

Next we estimate
w_{j}
,
0\leq j\leq 2n
. From equation (
3.37
) we have

(3.43)

w_{j}=-Q_{n}w_{j}+g_{j}.

First we prove for
k=0
, so that
A,B\in C^{\mu}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
. By part (i), we know that
w_{s}\in C^{\frac{p-2}{p}}(\overline{D})
for any
2<p<\infty
. Hence
w_{s}\in C^{1-}(\overline{\Omega})
. By Proposition
7.7
we have

\displaystyle|w_{j}|_{1+\mu}

\displaystyle\leq C(|Q_{n}w_{j}|_{1+\mu}+1)=C\left(|P_{n}(Aw_{j}+B\overline{w_{j}})|_{1+\mu}+1\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\{(|A|_{\mu}+|B|_{\mu})|w_{j}|_{\mu}+1\}\leq C(A,B).

By (
3.43
) again and iterate the process we get
w_{j}\in C^{k+1+\mu}
and

|w_{j}|_{k+1+\mu}\leq C_{k}(A,B).

Now for the coefficients
d_{j}
we have estimate (
3.39
),

|d_{j}|\leq C_{j}(A,B)\left(|w|_{0}+|F|_{\mu}+|\gamma|_{\mu}\right).

Finally putting together the estimates we get

\displaystyle|w|_{k+1+\mu}

\displaystyle\leq|w_{\ast}|_{k+1+\mu}+\sum_{j=0}^{2n}|d_{j}||w_{j}|_{k+1+\mu}\leq C_{k}(A,B)\left(|w|_{0}+|F|_{k+\mu}+|\gamma|_{k+1+\mu}\right).

Finally we let
\widetilde{w}
be the solution to the original Problem A (
3.40
) on
\mathbb{D}
. In view of the relations (
3.41
) we easily get

|\widetilde{w}|_{k+1+\mu}\leq C_{k}(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{B},l)\left(|\widetilde{w}|_{0}+|\widetilde{F}|_{k+\mu}+|\widetilde{\gamma}|_{k+1+\mu}\right).

∎

Remark 3.10
.

In our study of the boundary value problems with parameter, the above estimates imply that for each fixed
\lambda
, the solution
w^{\lambda}
satisfies

|w^{\lambda}|_{k+1+\mu}\leq C(A,B,l,\lambda)\left(|w^{\lambda}|_{0}+|F^{\lambda}|_{k+\mu}+|\gamma^{\lambda}|_{k+1+\mu}\right)

on
b\Omega^{\lambda}
. However, this estimate is not useful since we do not know how the constant depends on
\lambda
.

We end this section by connecting the oblique derivative boundary value problem for the Laplacian elliptic operator (hereafter called Problem B) to Problem A.

Problem B

Consider the following boundary value problem:

(3.44)

\begin{gathered}\Delta U+a(x,y)U_{x}+b(x,y)U_{y}=f(x,y);\\
\alpha U_{x}+\beta U_{y}=\gamma.\end{gathered}

To solve this problem we follow Vekua and reduce it to Problem A.
Let

u=U_{x},\quad v=-U_{y},\quad w=u+iv.

The equation (
3.44
) reduces to the following specialized Problem
A
:

(3.45)

\begin{gathered}\partial_{\overline{z}}w+\frac{1}{4}(a+ib)w+\frac{1}{4}(a-ib)\overline{w}=\frac{1}{2}f,\quad\text{in $\Omega$;}\\
Re[\overline{l}w]=\gamma,\quad l=\alpha-i\beta,\quad\text{on $b\Omega$. }\end{gathered}

If
w
is the solution to (
3.45
), then the solution to Problem (
3.44
) is given by

(3.46)

\displaystyle U(x,y)

\displaystyle=c_{0}+Re\int_{z_{0}}^{z}w(\zeta)\,d\zeta=c_{0}+\int_{z_{0}}^{z}U_{x}(x,y)\,dx+U_{y}(x,y)\,dy.

Now,
U
is globally well-defined only if the closed form
U_{x}\,dx+U_{y}\,dy
is exact. This occurs when the domain is simply-connected. Thus in the case of simply-connected domain, Problem
B
is completely equivalent to Problem
A
.

In the case
b\Omega
has
m+1
connected components, for
m>0
, in order to guarantee the right-hand side of (
3.46
) is single-valued, it is necessary and sufficient to add
m
solubility conditions:

(3.47)

Re\int_{\Gamma_{j}}w\,d\zeta=\int_{\Gamma_{j}}u\,dx-v\,dy=0,\quad(j=1,2,\dots,m)

where
\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{m}
are simple closed contours bounding the domain
\Omega
,
\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{m}
being situated inside
\Gamma_{0}
. Hence a solution
w
to (
3.45
) is a solution to the original Problem (
3.44
) if and only if it satisfies conditions (
3.47
).

4.
Families of simply-connected domains

In this section we prove Theorem
1.1
for families of simply-connected domains. First we need to define the index for oblique derivative boundary value problem:

Given the oblique derivative boundary value problem (
1.1
),
we define its
index
\varkappa
as the winding number of
\overline{\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}}
:

(4.1)

\varkappa:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\left[\overline{\Upsilon^{\lambda}}\right]_{b\Omega^{\lambda}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\left[\xi^{\lambda}-i\eta^{\lambda}\right].

Note that since
\varkappa
is an integer, it is stable under small perturbation of
\lambda
.

We formulate our results in two parts depending on the sign of
\varkappa
. First we consider the case
\varkappa\geq 0
.

Theorem 4.1

(Simply-connected, non-negative index case)
.

Let
k,j,\mu
be as in Theorem
1.1
. Let
\Omega
be a bounded, simply-connected domain in
\mathbb{C}
with
\mathcal{C}^{k+2+\mu}
boundary. Let
\Gamma^{\lambda}
be a family of maps that embed
\Omega
to
\Omega^{\lambda}
, with
\Gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+2+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
. Consider problem (
1.1
) with
\varkappa\geq 0
, and where
L^{\lambda}
is given by (
1.2
).
Suppose
a^{\lambda},b^{\lambda},c^{\lambda},d^{\lambda},e^{\lambda},f^{\lambda}
are functions in
\Omega^{\lambda}
such that
a^{\lambda},b^{\lambda},c^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
, and
d^{\lambda},e^{\lambda},f^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
.
Suppose that
\Upsilon^{\lambda},\gamma^{\lambda}
are functions in
b\Omega^{\lambda}
such that
\Upsilon^{\lambda},\gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(b\Omega^{\lambda})
. For each
\lambda\in[0,1]
, let
\{\{z_{r}^{\lambda}\}_{r=1}^{N_{0}}
,
(z_{s}^{\lambda})^{\prime}\}_{s=1}^{N_{1}}\}
be a normally distributed set for
\Omega^{\lambda}
, and let
u^{\lambda}
be the unique solution to (
1.1
) on
\Omega^{\lambda}
satisfying

\displaystyle u^{\lambda}_{x}(z_{r}^{\lambda})-iu^{\lambda}_{y}(z_{r}^{\lambda})=a_{r}(\lambda)+ib_{r}(\lambda)\quad\quad(r=1,...,N_{0});

\displaystyle u^{\lambda}_{x}((z_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})-iu^{\lambda}_{y}((z_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})=l^{\lambda}((z_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})(\gamma^{\lambda}((z_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})+ic_{s}(\lambda)),\quad\quad(s=1,...,N_{1}),

\displaystyle u^{\lambda}(z_{0}^{\lambda})=g(\lambda),\quad z_{0}^{\lambda}\in\Omega^{\lambda};

for some functions
a_{r},b_{r},c_{s},g\in C^{j}([0,1])
. Then
u^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+2+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
.

By Proposition
2.10
, we can reduce the problem to the following“canonical” form (Problem B) with the same index:

\displaystyle\Delta U^{\lambda}+a^{\lambda}(x,y)U^{\lambda}_{x}+b^{\lambda}(x,y)U^{\lambda}_{y}=f^{\lambda}(x,y),\quad\text{in $\Omega^{\lambda}$; }

\displaystyle\alpha^{\lambda}U_{x}^{\lambda}+\beta^{\lambda}U^{\lambda}_{y}=\gamma^{\lambda},\quad\text{on $b\Omega^{\lambda}$,}

where
a^{\lambda},b^{\lambda},f^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
,
\alpha^{\lambda},\beta^{\lambda},\gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
. Furthermore, by the remark in the last section (see (
3
)), if one sets
w^{\lambda}=U_{x}^{\lambda}-iU_{y}^{\lambda}
then

(4.2)

U^{\lambda}(x,y)=c_{0}(\lambda)+Re\int_{z_{0}}^{z}w^{\lambda}(\zeta)\,d\zeta.

Hence it suffices to consider the corresponding Problem A for
w^{\lambda}
:

(4.3)

\begin{gathered}\partial{}_{\overline{z}}w^{\lambda}+A^{\lambda}w^{\lambda}+B^{\lambda}\overline{w^{\lambda}}=F^{\lambda},\quad\text{in $\Omega^{\lambda}$; }\\
Re[\overline{l^{\lambda}}w^{\lambda}]=\gamma^{\lambda},\quad l^{\lambda}=\alpha^{\lambda}-i\beta^{\lambda},\quad\text{on $b\Omega^{\lambda}$,}\end{gathered}

with the corresponding index
n=\frac{1}{2\pi}[l^{\lambda}]_{b\Omega^{\lambda}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}[\overline{\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}}]_{b\Omega^{\lambda}}=\varkappa\geq 0
,
A^{\lambda},B^{\lambda},F^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
, and
l^{\lambda},\gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
.

Our strategy is to use a smooth family of Riemann mappings proved in
[
1
]
to reduce the problem to one on a fixed domain, where only the coefficients and the right-hand side of the equation depend on the parameter.

Let us first consider the following homogeneous Problem A on a fixed domain:

(4.4)

\begin{cases}\mathcal{L}(w^{\lambda})\equiv\partial{}_{\overline{z}}w^{\lambda}+A^{\lambda}(z)w^{\lambda}+B^{\lambda}(z)\overline{w^{\lambda}}=0\quad\text{in $\Omega$; }\\
Re[\overline{l^{\lambda}(z)}w^{\lambda}]=0\quad\text{on $b\Omega$. }\end{cases}

Let
w_{s}^{\lambda}
,
1\leq s\leq 2n+1
be the solutions to the integral equation

w^{\lambda}+Q_{n}^{\lambda}w^{\lambda}=g_{s},

where
g_{s}
is one of the functions:

(4.5)

z^{\ell}-z^{2n-\ell},\quad i(z^{\ell}+z^{2n-\ell}),\quad iz^{n},\quad\ell=0,\dots,n-1.

By (
3.14
) and (
3.16
),
\{w_{s}^{\lambda}\}_{s=1}^{2n+1}
forms a basis of
\mathbb{R}
linearly-independent solutions to Problem (
4.4
). We now prove regularity of
w^{\lambda}_{s}
with respect to
\lambda
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
k
be a non-negative integer and let
0<\mu<1
. Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
be a simply connected domain with
C^{k+1+\mu}
boundary. Let
w_{1}^{\lambda},\cdots,w_{2n+1}^{\lambda}
be linearly-independent solutions to the homogeneous Problem A (
4.4
) with parameter as given above, with index
n\geq 0
. Suppose
A^{\lambda},B^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega})
,
l^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega})
. Then
w_{s}^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega})
, for
s=1,\dots,2n+1
.

Proof.

First we reduce the problem to one on the unit disk. Since
\Omega
is a simply-connected domain, there exists a biholomorphic map
\Gamma:\Omega\to\mathbb{D}
which is
C^{k+1+\mu}
up to the boundary. Define
\widetilde{w}
on
\mathbb{D}
by
w(z)=\widetilde{w}\circ\Gamma(z)
. Then
\widetilde{w}(\zeta)
is the solution to the problem:

(4.6)

\begin{cases}\partial{}_{\overline{\zeta}}\widetilde{w}^{\lambda}+\widetilde{A}^{\lambda}(\zeta)\widetilde{w}^{\lambda}+\widetilde{B}^{\lambda}(\zeta)\overline{\widetilde{w}^{\lambda}}=0\quad\text{in $\mathbb{D}$;}\\
Re[\overline{\widetilde{l}^{\lambda}(\zeta)}\widetilde{w}^{\lambda}]=0\quad\text{on $S^{1}$, }\end{cases}

where
\widetilde{A}^{\lambda}=A^{\lambda}D^{-1},\widetilde{B}^{\lambda}=B^{\lambda}D^{-1},D(\zeta)=\overline{\partial{}_{z}\Gamma}\circ\Gamma^{-1}(\zeta)\neq 0.

Since
\Gamma\in C^{k+1+\mu}(\overline{\Omega})
and
\Gamma^{-1}\in C^{k+1+\mu}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
, we have
A^{\lambda},B^{\lambda},\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,0}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
, and
\widetilde{l}^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\mathbb{D}}).
Thus the problem is reduced to
\Omega=\mathbb{D}
.

By (
3.5
), it suffices to consider the reduced boundary value problem

(4.7)

\begin{cases}\partial{}_{\overline{z}}w^{\lambda}+A^{\lambda}w^{\lambda}+B^{\lambda}\overline{w^{\lambda}}=0,\quad\text{in $\mathbb{D}$; }\\
Re[z^{-n}w^{\lambda}]=0,\quad\text{on $S^{1}$, }\end{cases}

where

w^{\lambda}(z)=e^{\chi^{\lambda}(z)}\widetilde{w}^{\lambda}(z),A^{\lambda}=\widetilde{A}^{\lambda},B^{\lambda}=\widetilde{B}^{\lambda}e^{2iIm\,\chi^{\lambda}(z)},

and
\chi^{\lambda}(z)
is defined in (
3.3
).
By Proposition
7.11
,
we have
\chi^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega})
, and hence
A^{\lambda},B^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega})
.
By (
3.14
), for
s=1,2,\dots,2n+1
,
w_{s}^{\lambda}
is the unique solution satisfying the integral equation

(4.8)

w_{s}^{\lambda}+Q_{n}^{\lambda}w_{s}^{\lambda}=g_{s},

where
Q_{n}^{\lambda}w^{\lambda}_{s}:=P_{n}(A^{\lambda}w^{\lambda}+B^{\lambda}\overline{w^{\lambda}})
, and
g_{s}
are given by (
4.5
).

Let us first prove the statement for
k=0
. We claim
|w_{s}^{\lambda}|_{0}
is bounded uniformly in
\lambda
. Suppose this is not the case, that there exists some sequence
\lambda_{j}\in[0,1]
with
N_{j}=|w_{s}^{\lambda_{j}}|_{0}\to\infty
. Since
[0,1]
is compact, passing to a subsequence if necessary we can assume that
\lambda_{j}
converges to some
\lambda_{0}
. Dividing each side of the integral equation (
4.8
) by
N_{j}
, and writing
\widehat{w}_{s}^{\lambda_{j}}=w_{s}^{\lambda_{j}}/N_{j}
, we obtain

(4.9)

\widehat{w_{s}}^{\lambda_{j}}+Q_{n}^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{w_{s}}^{\lambda_{j}}=N_{j}^{-1}g_{s},\quad\quad|\widehat{w_{s}}^{\lambda_{j}}|_{0}=1.

By Proposition
7.6
, we have for any
0<\tau<1

|Q_{n}^{\lambda}\widehat{w_{i}}^{\lambda_{j}}|_{\tau}\leq C\left(|A^{\lambda}|_{0}+|B^{\lambda}|_{0}\right)|\widehat{w_{i}}^{\lambda_{j}}|_{0}\leq C^{\prime}.

By Arzela-Ascoli theorem, and passing to a subsequence if necessary, we have
Q_{n}^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{w_{s}}^{\lambda_{j}}
converges to some function uniformly. It follows from equation (
4.9
) that
\widehat{w_{s}}^{\lambda_{j}}
converges to some function
\widehat{w}_{s}
uniformly. We show that
Q_{n}^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{w_{s}}^{\lambda_{j}}
converges uniformly to
Q_{n}^{\lambda_{0}}\widehat{w}
. Recall that

Q_{n}^{\lambda}w^{\lambda}=P_{n}(A^{\lambda}w^{\lambda}+B^{\lambda}\overline{w^{\lambda}}),

where
P_{n}
is given by formula (
3.10
). Hence

\displaystyle Q_{n}^{\lambda_{1}}w^{\lambda_{1}}-Q_{n}^{\lambda_{2}}w^{\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(Q_{n}^{\lambda_{1}}-Q_{n}^{\lambda_{2}}\right)w^{\lambda_{1}}+Q_{n}^{\lambda_{2}}(w^{\lambda_{1}}-w^{\lambda_{2}})

\displaystyle=P_{n}\left((A^{\lambda_{1}}-A^{\lambda_{2}})w^{\lambda_{1}}\right)+P_{n}\left((B^{\lambda_{1}}-B^{\lambda_{2}})\overline{w^{\lambda_{1}}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+P_{n}\left(A^{\lambda_{2}}(w^{\lambda_{1}}-w^{\lambda_{2}})\right)+P_{n}\left(B^{\lambda_{2}}(w^{\lambda_{1}}-w^{\lambda_{2}})\right).

By Proposition
7.6
, we have
for any
0<\tau<1

\displaystyle\left|Q_{n}^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{w_{s}}^{\lambda_{j}}-Q_{n}^{\lambda_{0}}\widehat{w}\right|_{\tau}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\left|A^{\lambda_{j}}-A^{\lambda_{0}}\right|_{0}+\left|B^{\lambda_{j}}-B^{\lambda_{0}}\right|_{0}\right)|\widehat{w_{s}}^{\lambda_{j}}|_{0}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(|A^{\lambda_{0}}|_{0}+|B^{\lambda_{0}}|_{0}\right)\left|\widehat{w_{s}}^{\lambda_{j}}-\widehat{w_{s}}\right|_{0}.

Since
A^{\lambda},B^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{\mu,0}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
, we have
A,B
are continuous in
\lambda
in the sup norm. Hence by taking
\tau=0
in the above expression we get
\left|Q_{n}^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{w_{s}}^{\lambda_{j}}-Q_{n}^{\lambda_{0}}\widehat{w}\right|_{0}\longrightarrow 0
. Letting
\lambda_{j}\to\lambda_{0}
in (
4.9
) we get

\widehat{w}_{s}+Q_{n}^{\lambda_{0}}\widehat{w}_{s}=0.

By Vekua
[
7
, p. 296-298]
, there does not exist non-trivial continuous solutions for the above equation, thus
\widehat{w}_{s}=0
.
But we have
|\widehat{w}_{s}|_{0}=\lim_{j\to\infty}|\widehat{w_{s}}^{\lambda_{j}}|_{0}=1
, which is a contradiction. Thus
|w_{s}^{\lambda}|_{0}
is bounded by some constant
C_{0}
uniform in
\lambda
. It follows then

|Q_{n}^{\lambda}w_{s}^{\lambda}|_{\tau}\leq C(|A^{\lambda}|_{0}+|B^{\lambda}|_{0})|w_{s}^{\lambda}|_{0}\leq C_{0}^{\prime},

for any
0<\tau<1
.
By (
4.8
) we obtain
|w^{\lambda}|_{\tau}
is bounded uniformly in
\lambda
by some constant
C_{0}^{\prime\prime}
.

For
k>0
we apply induction. By (
4.8
) and Proposition
7.7
one has

\displaystyle|w_{s}^{\lambda}|_{k+1+\mu}

\displaystyle\leq|Q_{n}^{\lambda}w_{s}^{\lambda}|_{k+1+\mu}+C_{k}

\displaystyle=|P_{n}(A^{\lambda}w_{s}^{\lambda}+B^{\lambda}\overline{w_{s}^{\lambda}})|_{k+1+\mu}+C^{\prime}_{k}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime\prime}_{k}\left(|A^{\lambda}|_{k+\mu}+|B^{\lambda}|_{k+\mu}\right)|w_{s}^{\lambda}|_{k+\mu}+C^{\prime\prime\prime}_{k}\leq C^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}_{k}.

Hence
|w_{s}^{\lambda}|_{k+1+\mu}
is bounded uniformly in
\lambda
.

Finally we show that
w_{s}^{\lambda}
is continuous in the
C^{0}
-norm. Suppose this is not the case, we can then find a sequence
\lambda_{j}\to\lambda_{0}
,
|w_{s}^{\lambda_{j}}-w_{s}^{\lambda_{0}}|_{0}\geq\delta>0
. Since
|w_{s}^{\lambda_{j}}|_{k+1+\mu}
is bounded, passing to a subsequence if necessary,
w_{s}^{\lambda_{j}}
converges to some function
\widehat{w_{s}}
in the
C^{k+1}
-norm. We have shown above that
Q_{n}^{\lambda_{j}}w_{s}^{\lambda_{j}}
converges uniformly to
Q_{n}^{\lambda_{0}}\widehat{w_{s}}
. Letting
\lambda_{j}\to\infty
in equation (
4.8
) we have

\widehat{w_{s}}+Q_{n}^{\lambda_{0}}\widehat{w_{s}}=g_{s}.

This equation has a unique solution
w^{\lambda_{0}}_{s}
, thus
\widehat{w_{s}}=w^{\lambda_{0}}_{s}
. But this contradicts the fact that
