|\widehat{w_{s}}-w^{\lambda_{0}}_{s}|_{0}\geq\delta>0
. Thus we have proved that
w_{s}^{\lambda}
is continuous in
\lambda
in the
C^{0}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
-norm. The continuity of
w_{s}^{\lambda}
in the
C^{k+1}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
-norm then follows from Proposition
2.3
.
∎

Next we show the smooth dependence in parameter for a particular solution of the non-homogenous Problem A.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
k
be a non-negative integer and let
0<\mu<1
. Consider Problem A with parameter on a simply-connected domain
\Omega
:

(4.10)

\begin{cases}\partial{}_{\overline{z}}w^{\lambda}+A^{\lambda}(z)w^{\lambda}+B^{\lambda}(z)\overline{w^{\lambda}}=F^{\lambda}\quad\text{in $\Omega$;}\\
Re[\overline{l^{\lambda}(z)}w^{\lambda}]=\gamma^{\lambda}\quad\text{on $b\Omega$, }\end{cases}

where the index
n\geq 0
. Suppose
A^{\lambda},B^{\lambda},F^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega})
,
l^{\lambda},\gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(b\Omega)
. Then there exists a solution
w_{0}
in the class
\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega})
. Moreover there exists some constant
C
independent of
\lambda
such that

(4.11)

\displaystyle\|w_{0}^{\lambda}\|_{0,0}:=\sup_{\lambda\in[0,1]}|w_{0}^{\lambda}|_{0}\leq C\left(\|F^{\lambda}\|_{0,0}+\|\gamma^{\lambda}\|_{\mu,0}\right),

(4.12)

\displaystyle\|w_{0}^{\lambda}\|_{k+1+\mu,0}\leq C\left(\|F^{\lambda}\|_{k+\mu,0}+\|\gamma^{\lambda}\|_{k+1+\mu,0}\right).

Proof.

To prove estimate (
4.11
) (resp.(
4.12
)), it suffices to show that
\|w_{0}\|_{0,0}\leq C
(resp.
\|w_{0}\|_{k+1+\mu,0}\leq C
) if each norm on the right-hand side is bounded by
1
, since the equations are linear.
As before we can assume the problem takes the form (
4.7
).
In view of equation (
3.14
), we can take
w_{0}^{\lambda}
to be the unique solution in
C^{0}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
to the integral equation in
\mathbb{D}
:

(4.13)

w^{\lambda}+Q_{n}^{\lambda}w^{\lambda}=P_{n}F^{\lambda}+\frac{z^{n}}{2\pi i}\int_{S^{1}}\gamma^{\lambda}(t)\frac{t+z}{t-z}\frac{dt}{t},

where

P_{n}F^{\lambda}=T_{\mathbb{D}}F^{\lambda}+z^{2n+1}T_{\mathbb{D}^{c}}F_{1}^{\lambda},Q_{n}^{\lambda}w^{\lambda}=P_{n}(A^{\lambda}w^{\lambda}+B^{\lambda}\overline{w^{\lambda}}),

and
F_{1}^{\lambda}
and
F^{\lambda}
are related by the second expression in (
3.11
).

The proof is identical to that of the previous theorem, once we show that the right-hand side of equation (
4.13
) belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
. By Proposition
7.7
,

|P_{n}F^{\lambda}|_{k+1+\mu}\leq C|F^{\lambda}|_{k+\mu}.

The second term is
z^{n}
times
\mathcal{S}\gamma^{\lambda}
, the Schwarz transform of
\gamma^{\lambda}
on
\mathbb{D}
. We have

|\mathcal{S}\gamma^{\lambda}|_{k+1+\mu}\leq C|\gamma^{\lambda}|_{k+1+\mu}

by Proposition
7.11
. Thus
|P_{n}F^{\lambda}+z^{n}\mathcal{S}\gamma^{\lambda}|_{k+1+\mu}
is bounded. Fix
\tau\in(0,1)
we have

\displaystyle|P_{n}F^{\lambda_{1}}-P_{n}F^{\lambda_{2}}|_{0}\leq|P_{n}F^{\lambda_{1}}-P_{n}F^{\lambda_{2}}|_{\tau}\leq C|F^{\lambda_{1}}-F^{\lambda_{2}}|_{0},

\displaystyle|\mathcal{S}\gamma^{\lambda_{1}}-\mathcal{S}\gamma^{\lambda_{2}}|_{0}\leq|\mathcal{S}\gamma^{\lambda_{1}}-\mathcal{S}\gamma^{\lambda_{2}}|_{\tau}\leq C|\gamma^{\lambda_{1}}-\gamma^{\lambda_{2}}|_{\tau}.

By the above two inequalities and the assumptions, we see that
P_{n}F^{\lambda}+z^{n}\mathcal{S}\gamma^{\lambda}
is continuous in the
C^{0}
-norm. By Proposition
2.3
,
P_{n}F^{\lambda}+S\gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
.
∎

We can now prove the following result for Problem A on fixed domain:

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
k
be a non-negative integer and let
0<\mu<1
. Consider the non-homogeneous Problem A (
4.10
) on a simply-connected domain
\Omega
, with index
n\geq 0
. Suppose
A,B,F\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega})
,
l,\gamma\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(b\Omega)
,
a_{r},b_{r},c_{s}\in C^{0}([0,1])
. Then for each
\lambda
there exists a unique solution
w^{\lambda}
satisfying the following conditions on a normally distributed set (see (
3.6
)).

(4.14)

\begin{gathered}w^{\lambda}(z_{r})=a_{r}(\lambda)+ib_{r}(\lambda),\quad\quad r=1,...,N_{0};\\
w^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})=l^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})(\gamma^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})+ic_{s}(\lambda)),\quad\quad s=1,...,N_{1}\end{gathered}

where
2N_{0}+N_{1}=2n+1
. The family
w^{\lambda}
belongs to the class
\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega})
. Moreover there exists some constant
C
independent of
\lambda
so that

(4.15)

\displaystyle\|w^{\lambda}\|_{0,0}\leq C\left(\|F^{\lambda}\|_{0,0}+\|\gamma^{\lambda}\|_{\mu,0}+\sum_{r=1}^{N_{0}}\left(|a_{r}|_{0}+|b_{r}|_{0}\right)+\sum_{s=1}^{N_{1}}|c_{s}|_{0}\right),

(4.16)

\displaystyle\|w^{\lambda}\|_{k+1+\mu,0}\leq C\left(\|F^{\lambda}\|_{k+\mu,0}+\|\gamma^{\lambda}\|_{k+1+\mu,0}+\sum_{r=1}^{N_{0}}\left(|a_{r}|_{0}+|b_{r}|_{0}\right)+\sum_{s=1}^{N_{1}}|c_{s}|_{0}\right),

where we denote
|a_{r}|_{0}:=\sup_{\lambda\in[0,1]}|a_{r}(\lambda)|
and similarly for
|b_{r}|_{0}
and
|c_{s}|_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
w_{0}^{\lambda}
be the particular solution to the non-homogeneous Problem A given in Proposition
4.3
, and let
w^{\lambda}_{\ell}
-s be the
2n+1

\mathbb{R}
-linearly independent solution to the homogeneous Problem A given in Proposition
4.2
. Then
w^{\lambda}
is given by the formula:

(4.17)

\displaystyle w^{\lambda}(z)=w_{0}^{\lambda}(z)+\sum_{\ell=1}^{2n+1}d_{\ell}(\lambda)w_{\ell}^{\lambda}(z),

where
d_{\ell}
are real-valued functions on
[0,1]
.

From (
4.14
), we have the following linear system of equations for the determination of the functions
d_{s}(\lambda)
:

(4.18)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{2n+1}d_{\ell}(\lambda)w_{\ell}^{\lambda}(z_{r})=a_{r}(\lambda)+ib_{r}(\lambda)-w^{\lambda}_{0}(z_{r})\quad\quad r=1,...,N_{0},

(4.19)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{2n+1}d_{\ell}(\lambda)w_{\ell}^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})=l^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})(\gamma^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})+ic_{s}(\lambda))-w^{\lambda}_{0}(z_{s}^{\prime})\qquad s=1,...,N_{1}.

We can write the above as
2N_{0}+2N_{1}
real equations. However, there are in fact only
2N_{0}+N_{1}=2n+1
equations, since the imaginary parts of equations (
4.19
) are obtained from the real parts of equation (
4.19
), by multiplying
-\frac{\alpha^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})}{\beta^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})}
(assume that
\beta^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})\neq 0
.) Indeed, we have for
z_{s}^{\prime}\in b\Omega
,

\displaystyle 0=Re[\overline{l^{\lambda}}w_{\ell}^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})]=\alpha^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})Re[w_{\ell}^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})]+\beta^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})Im[w_{\ell}^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})],\quad 1\leq\ell\leq 2n+1,

\displaystyle\gamma^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})=Re[\overline{l^{\lambda}}w_{0}^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})]=\alpha^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})Re[w_{0}^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})]+\beta^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})Im[w_{0}^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})],

where we set
l^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})=\alpha^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})+i\beta^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})
. Thus we can view the linear system (
4.18
)-(
4.19
) as:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}Re[w^{\lambda}_{1}(z_{1})]&\ldots&Re[w^{\lambda}_{2n+1}(z_{1})]\\
Im[w^{\lambda}_{1}(z_{1})]&\ldots&Im[w^{\lambda}_{2n+1}(z_{1})]\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
Re[w^{\lambda}_{1}(z_{N_{0}})]&\ldots&Re[w^{\lambda}_{2n+1}(z_{N_{0}})]\\
Im[w^{\lambda}_{1}(z_{N_{0}})]&\ldots&Im[w^{\lambda}_{2n+1}(z_{N_{0}})]\\
Re[w^{\lambda}_{1}(z_{1}^{\prime})]&\ldots&Re[w^{\lambda}_{2n+1}(z_{1}^{\prime})]\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
Re[w^{\lambda}_{1}(z_{N_{1}}^{\prime})]&\ldots&Re[w^{\lambda}_{2n+1}(z_{N_{1}}^{\prime})]\end{pmatrix}\times\begin{pmatrix}d_{1}(\lambda)\\
\vdots\\
d_{2n+1}(\lambda)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}Re(h^{\lambda}(z_{1}))\\
Im(h^{\lambda}(z_{1}))\\
\vdots\\
Re(h^{\lambda}(z_{N_{0}}))\\
Im(h^{\lambda}(z_{N_{0}}))\\
\vdots\\
Re(e^{\lambda}(z_{N_{1}}^{\prime}))\\
\vdots\\
Re(e^{\lambda}(z_{N_{1}}^{\prime}))\end{pmatrix}

where
h^{\lambda}(z_{r})
and
e^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})
denote the right-hand side of (
4.18
) and (
4.19
), respectively.
Write the above matrix equation as
Mx=\textbf{v}
. By
Vekua
[
7
, p. 286-287]
, for fixed
\lambda
and a normally distributed set
\{N_{0},N_{1},\overline{\Omega}\}
,
M
is invertible, thus we can write
d_{s}(\lambda)
as
\frac{P}{Q}
,
Q\neq 0
, and
P
and
Q
are linear combination of products of

Re[w_{\ell}^{\lambda}(z_{r})],\quad Im[w_{\ell}^{\lambda}(z_{r})],\quad Re[w_{\ell}^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})],\quad Im[w_{\ell}^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})],

(Re,Im)[a_{r}(\lambda)+ib_{r}(\lambda)-w^{\lambda}_{0}(z_{r})],\quad Re[l^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})(\gamma^{\lambda}(z_{s}^{\prime})+ic_{j}(\lambda))-w^{\lambda}_{0}(z_{s}^{\prime})].

By the assumption, Proposition
4.2
and
4.3
, for fixed
z_{r}
and
z_{s}^{\prime}
the above functions are continuous in
\lambda
, thus
d_{\ell}
-s are continuous functions of
\lambda
, and moreover
by (
4.11
) we have

(4.20)

|d_{\ell}|_{0}\leq C\left(\sum_{r=1}^{N_{0}}\left(|a_{r}|_{0}+|b_{r}|_{0}\right)+\left(\sum_{s=1}^{N_{1}}|c_{s}|_{0}\right)+\|F\|_{0,0}+\|\gamma\|_{\mu,0}\right).

Since
w_{0}^{\lambda},w_{\ell}^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
, it follow from (
4.17
) that
w^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega})
. Putting together estimates (
4.11
), (
4.12
) and (
4.20
) we get (
4.15
) and (
4.16
).
∎

Remark 4.5
.

Since our proof uses a contradiction argument, the constant
C
in (
4.15
) and (
4.16
) is not explicit. In particular we do not know how it depends on the coefficient functions
A^{\lambda}
,
B^{\lambda}
and
l^{\lambda}
.

We now prove the analog of Proposition
4.4
when the index is negative.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
k
be a non-negative integer, and let
0<\mu<1
.
Consider Problem A (
4.10
) with negative index. Suppose for each
\lambda
, conditions (
3.17
) is fulfilled. Suppose
A^{\lambda},B^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega})
,
l^{\lambda},\gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(b\Omega)
. Then for each
\lambda
there exists a unique solution
w^{\lambda}
on
\Omega
. The family
w^{\lambda}
lies in the class
\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega})
, and the following estimate hold

\|w\|_{k+1+\mu,0}\leq C(A,B,l)\left(\|F\|_{k+\mu,0}+\|\gamma\|_{k+1+\mu,0}\right).

Proof.

Reducing the problem to the canonical form (
4.7
). By Vekua
[
7
, p. 300, (7.32), (7.33)]
,
w^{\lambda}
satisfies the integral equation (
3.19
)

(4.21)

w^{\lambda}+(Q_{n^{\prime}}^{\ast})^{\lambda}w^{\lambda}=P_{k}^{\ast}F^{\lambda}+\frac{1}{\pi i}\int_{S^{1}}\frac{\gamma^{\lambda}(t)\,dt}{t^{k}(t-z)},\quad n^{\prime}=-n,

where
Q^{\ast}_{n^{\prime}}w=P^{\ast}_{n^{\prime}}(A^{\lambda}w^{\lambda}+B^{\lambda}\overline{w^{\lambda}})
and
P^{\ast}_{n^{\prime}}
is given by (
3.20
).
Vekua proved that for fixed
\lambda
, the above equation is uniquely soluble for
w^{\lambda}
given any right-hand side in
L^{q}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
, for
q\geq\frac{p}{p-1}
, and
Q^{\ast}_{n^{\prime}}
is a compact operator on
L^{q}(\mathbb{D})
satisfying the estimate

|(Q_{n^{\prime}}^{\ast})^{\lambda}f|_{k+1+\mu}\leq C|B^{\lambda}|_{k+\mu}|f|_{k+1+\mu}.

Following the same argument as for the positive index case, it suffices to show the right-hand side of (
4.21
) lies in the space
\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\mathbb{D}}\times[0,1])
. For the first term we have the estimate

|P_{k}^{\ast}F^{\lambda}|_{k+1+\mu}\leq C|F^{\lambda}|_{k+\mu},\quad|P_{k}^{\ast}F^{\lambda_{1}}-P_{k}^{\ast}F^{\lambda_{2}}|\leq C|F^{\lambda_{1}}-F^{\lambda_{2}}|_{0}.

The second term is the Cauchy transform of the function
g^{\lambda}=\gamma^{\lambda}/t^{k}
, and we have the estimate by Proposition
7.11
:

|\mathfrak{C}g^{\lambda}|_{k+1+\mu}\leq C|\gamma^{\lambda}|_{k+1+\mu},\quad|\mathfrak{C}g^{\lambda_{1}}-\mathfrak{C}g^{\lambda_{2}}|_{0}\leq C|\gamma^{\lambda_{1}}-\gamma^{\lambda_{2}}|_{1}.

Since
F^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,0}(\mathbb{D})
and
\gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(S^{1})
, then
P^{\ast}_{n^{\prime}}F^{\lambda}
and
\mathfrak{C}(g^{\lambda})
are in
\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
.
∎

We now combine Proposition
4.4
and Proposition
2.6
to prove the following result for Problem A on families of simply-connected domains. We state and prove the theorem only for
n\geq 0
. When
n<0
, the same conclusion holds if we assume conditions (
3.17
) hold. The proof can be done similarly as in Theorem
4.7
by using Proposition
4.6
and we leave the details to the reader.

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
k,j,\mu
be as in Theorem
1.1
. Let
\Omega^{\lambda}
be a
\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}
family of bounded simply-connected domains. That is, there exists a family of maps
\Gamma^{\lambda}:\overline{\Omega}\to\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}}
, with
\Gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
.
Suppose the index
n\geq 0
. Let
\{z_{r}\in\Omega,z_{s}^{\prime}\in b\Omega\}
be a normally distributed set in
\overline{\Omega}
(Definition
3.6
). Set
z^{\lambda}_{r}=\Gamma^{\lambda}(z_{r})
,
(z^{\prime}_{s})^{\lambda}=\Gamma^{\lambda}(z^{\prime}_{s})
. Then
\{z^{\lambda}_{r},(z^{\prime}_{s})^{\lambda}\}
is a normally distributed set in
\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}}
.
Let
w^{\lambda}
be the unique solution to Problem (
4.3
) satisfying the following:

(4.22)

\begin{gathered}w^{\lambda}(z_{r}^{\lambda})=a_{r}(\lambda)+ib_{r}(\lambda),\quad\quad(r=1,...,N_{0});\\
w^{\lambda}((z_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})=l^{\lambda}((z_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})[\gamma^{\lambda}((z_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})+ic_{s}(\lambda)],\quad\quad(s=1,...,N_{1})\end{gathered}

where
2N_{0}+N_{1}=2n+1
.

(i)
Suppose
A^{\lambda},B^{\lambda},F^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
, and
l^{\lambda},\gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(b\Omega^{\lambda})
, and
a_{r},b_{r},c_{s}\in C^{0}([0,1])
. Then
w^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
, and there exists some constant
C
independent of
\lambda
such that

(4.23)

\displaystyle\|w^{\lambda}\|_{0,0}\leq C\left(\|F^{\lambda}\|_{0,0}+\|\gamma^{\lambda}\|_{\mu,0}+\sum_{r=1}^{N_{0}}\left(|a_{r}|_{0}+|b_{r}|_{0}\right)+\sum_{s=1}^{N_{1}}|c_{s}|_{0}\right);

(4.24)

\displaystyle\|w^{\lambda}\|_{k+1+\mu,0}\leq C\left(\|F^{\lambda}\|_{k+\mu,0}+\|\gamma^{\lambda}\|_{k+1+\mu,0}+\sum_{r=1}^{N_{0}}\left(|a_{r}|_{0}+|b_{r}|_{0}\right)+\sum_{s=1}^{N_{1}}|c_{s}|_{0}\right).

(ii) Suppose
A^{\lambda},B^{\lambda},F^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
, and
l^{\lambda},\gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{b\Omega^{\lambda}})
.
Let
w^{\lambda}
be the solution to Problem (
4.3
) satisfying the condition (
4.22
), where
a_{r},b_{r},c_{s}\in C^{j}([0,1])
. Then
w\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
.

Proof.

(i)
By Proposition
2.6
, there exists a family of Riemann mappings
R^{\lambda}:\Omega^{\lambda}\to\mathbb{D}
with
\widetilde{R}^{\lambda}(z):=R^{\lambda}(\Gamma^{\lambda}(z))\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
.
By Proposition
2.7
,
(R^{\lambda})^{-1}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
. i.e.
(R^{\lambda})^{-1}:\mathbb{D}\to\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}}
is also a
\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}
family of embeddings.
Define
v^{\lambda}(\zeta)=w^{\lambda}\circ(R^{\lambda})^{-1}(\zeta)
,
\zeta\in\mathbb{D}
, and write
z^{\lambda}=(R^{\lambda})^{-1}(\zeta)
. Since
(R^{\lambda})^{-1}
is holomorphic, we have the boundary value problem for
v^{\lambda}
on
\mathbb{D}
:

(4.25)

\begin{cases}\partial{}_{\overline{\zeta}}v^{\lambda}(\zeta)+A^{\lambda}_{1}(\zeta)v^{\lambda}(\zeta)+B^{\lambda}_{1}(\zeta)\overline{v^{\lambda}(\zeta)}=F^{\lambda}_{1}(\zeta)&\text{in $\mathbb{D}$;}\\
Re[\overline{l_{1}^{\lambda}}(\zeta)v^{\lambda}(\zeta)]=\gamma_{1}^{\lambda}(\zeta)&\text{on $S^{1}$, }\end{cases}

where
l^{\lambda}_{1}:=l^{\lambda}\circ(R^{\lambda})^{-1}
,
\gamma^{\lambda}_{1}:=\gamma^{\lambda}\circ(R^{\lambda})^{-1}
, and

\displaystyle A_{1}^{\lambda}=A^{\lambda}\circ(R^{\lambda})^{-1}(\zeta)D^{\lambda}(\zeta),\quad B_{1}^{\lambda}=B^{\lambda}\circ(R^{\lambda})^{-1}(\zeta)D^{\lambda}(\zeta),\quad

\displaystyle F_{1}^{\lambda}=F^{\lambda}\circ(R^{\lambda})^{-1}(\zeta)D^{\lambda}(\zeta),\quad D^{\lambda}=\partial{}_{\overline{\zeta}}\overline{(R^{\lambda})^{-1}}.

By Proposition
2.4
,
A^{\lambda}_{1},B^{\lambda}_{1},F^{\lambda}_{1}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
, and
\overline{l_{1}^{\lambda}}
,
\gamma_{1}^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
. Also
D^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
. Hence
A_{1}^{\lambda}
,
B_{1}^{\lambda},F_{1}^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
.
The index
n=\frac{1}{2\pi}[l^{\lambda}]_{b\Omega}
is an integer independent of
\lambda
.
Let

\zeta_{r}^{\lambda}:=R^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda}(z_{r}),\quad(\zeta_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda}:=R^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda}(z_{s}).

Then for each
\lambda
,
\{\zeta^{\lambda}_{r},(\zeta^{\prime}_{s})^{\lambda}\}
is a normally distributed set in
\Omega^{\lambda}
.
v^{\lambda}
satisfies the condition

\displaystyle v^{\lambda}(\zeta_{r}^{\lambda})=w^{\lambda}(z_{r}^{\lambda})=a_{r}(\lambda)+ib_{r}(\lambda),\quad r=1,...,N_{0};

\displaystyle v^{\lambda}((\zeta_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})=w^{\lambda}((z_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})=l^{\lambda}((z_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})[\gamma^{\lambda}((z_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})+ic_{s}(\lambda)],\quad s=1,...,N_{1}.

Since
v^{\lambda}=v_{0}^{\lambda}+\sum_{\ell}d_{\ell}(\lambda)v_{\ell}^{\lambda}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{2n+1}d_{\ell}(\lambda)v_{\ell}^{\lambda}(\zeta_{r}^{\lambda})=a_{r}(\lambda)+ib_{r}(\lambda)-v_{0}^{\lambda}(\zeta_{r}^{\lambda}),

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{2n+1}d_{\ell}(\lambda)v_{\ell}^{\lambda}((\zeta_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})=l^{\lambda}((z_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})[\gamma^{\lambda}((z_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})+ic_{s}(\lambda)]-v_{0}^{\lambda}((\zeta_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda}),

where
v_{0}^{\lambda}
is a particular solution to the non-homogeneous problem (
4.25
) as given in Proposition
4.3
, and
v_{1}^{\lambda},...,v_{2n+1}^{\lambda}
are linearly independent solutions to the corresponding homogeneous problem as given in Proposition
4.2
. We have
v^{\lambda}_{0},v^{\lambda}_{1}
,
\dots,v^{\lambda}_{2n+1}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
.
Since
\zeta^{\lambda}_{r}
and
(\zeta^{\prime}_{s})^{\lambda}
depend on
\lambda
continuously,
v_{0}^{\lambda}(\zeta_{r}^{\lambda})
,
v_{\ell}^{\lambda}(\zeta_{r}^{\lambda})
,
v_{0}^{\lambda}((\zeta_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})
,
v_{\ell}^{\lambda}((\zeta_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})
depend on
\lambda
continuously.
Same reasoning as in the proof of Proposition
4.4
then shows that
d_{l}\in C^{0}([0,1])
, and consequently
v^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
, and we have

\|v^{\lambda}\|_{k+1+\mu,0}\leq C\left(\|F_{1}^{\lambda}\|_{k+\mu,0}+\|\gamma_{1}^{\lambda}\|_{k+1+\mu,0}+\sum_{r=1}^{N_{0}}\left(|a_{r}|_{0}+|b_{r}|_{0}\right)+\sum_{s=1}^{N_{1}}|c_{s}|_{0}\right).

Now
w^{\lambda}:=v^{\lambda}\circ R^{\lambda}
is the unique solution to Problem (
4.3
),
w^{\lambda}\circ(R^{\lambda})^{-1}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
, and
w^{\lambda}
satisfies the estimate (
4.24
).
By Lemma
2.4
,
w^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega})
, or
w^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
.

(ii) The
j=0
case is done in (i). Assume now that
j\geq 1
, and we use induction. We use the notation
D^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}v(\zeta)
from (
2.6
).
In view of (
4.25
)
D^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}v(\zeta)
is a solution to

(4.26)

\begin{cases}\partial{}_{\overline{\zeta}}u+A^{\lambda_{0}}_{1}u+B_{1}^{\lambda_{0}}\overline{u}=F_{\ast}^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}\quad&\text{in $\mathbb{D}$; }\\
Re[\overline{\widetilde{l}^{\lambda_{0}}}u]=\gamma_{\ast}^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}&\text{on $S^{1}$,}\end{cases}

where

F_{\ast}^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}:=D^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}F_{1}-(D^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}A_{1})v^{\lambda}-(D^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}B_{1})\overline{v^{\lambda}}
and
\gamma_{\ast}^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}:=D^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}\widetilde{\gamma}-Re[(D^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}\overline{\widetilde{l}})v^{\lambda}].
We also have

(4.27)

\begin{gathered}D^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}v(\zeta_{r})=h_{r}^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}:=D^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}a_{r}+iD^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}b_{r},\\
D^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}v(\zeta_{s}^{\prime})=g_{s}^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}:=D^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}\left\{l^{\bullet}((\zeta_{s}^{\prime})^{\bullet})[\gamma^{\bullet}((\zeta_{s}^{\prime})^{\bullet})+ic_{s}(\bullet)]\right\}.\end{gathered}

For every
\lambda_{0}
, denote by
\partial{}_{\lambda}v(\cdot,\lambda_{0})
the solution to the problem:

(4.28)

\begin{cases}\partial{}_{\overline{\zeta}}u+A^{\lambda_{0}}_{1}u+B_{1}^{\lambda_{0}}\overline{u}=F_{\ast}^{\lambda_{0}}\quad&\text{in $\mathbb{D}$; }\\
Re[\overline{\widetilde{l}^{\lambda_{0}}}u]=\gamma_{\ast}^{\lambda_{0}}&\text{on $S^{1}$,}\end{cases}

satisfying the condition

(4.29)

u^{\lambda_{0}}(\zeta_{r})=h_{r}(\lambda_{0}),\quad u(\zeta^{\prime}_{s},\lambda_{0})=g_{s}(\lambda_{0}),

where

(4.30)

\displaystyle F_{\ast}^{\lambda_{0}}:=\partial{}_{\lambda}F_{1}(\cdot,\lambda_{0})-\partial{}_{\lambda}A_{1}(\cdot,\lambda_{0})v^{\lambda_{0}}-\partial{}_{\lambda}B_{1}(\cdot,\lambda_{0})\overline{v^{\lambda_{0}}},

(4.31)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\ast}^{\lambda_{0}}:=\partial{}_{\lambda}\widetilde{\gamma}(\cdot,\lambda_{0})-Re[(\partial{}_{\lambda}\overline{\widetilde{l}}(\cdot,\lambda_{0})v^{\lambda_{0}}],

(4.32)

\displaystyle h_{r}(\lambda_{0})=a_{r}^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})+ib_{r}^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}),\quad g_{s}(\lambda_{0})=\partial{}_{\lambda_{0}}\{\widetilde{l^{\lambda}}(\zeta_{s}^{\prime})[\widetilde{\gamma^{\lambda}}(\zeta_{s}^{\prime})+ic_{s}(\lambda)]\}.

Note that the solution to (
4.28
) exists since the index
\frac{1}{2\pi}[\widetilde{l}^{\lambda_{0}}]
is non-negative.

By the induction hypothesis, we have
w^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j-1}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
, and therefore
v^{\lambda}=w^{\lambda}\circ(R^{\lambda})^{-1}(\zeta)\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j-1}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
. As shown in (i),
A_{1},B_{1},F_{1}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
, thus
\partial{}_{\lambda}A_{1}
,
\partial{}_{\lambda}B_{1}
,
\partial{}_{\lambda}F_{1}\in\mathcal{C}^{k-1+\mu,j-1}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
.
From (
4.30
) we have
F_{\ast}^{\lambda_{0}}\in\mathcal{C}^{k-1+\mu,j-1}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
. On the other hand we have
\partial{}_{\lambda}\widetilde{\gamma},\partial{}_{\lambda}\overline{\widetilde{l}}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j-1}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
, thus by (
4.31
),
\gamma_{\ast}^{\lambda_{0}}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j-1}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
.

Since
a_{r}^{\prime},b_{r}^{\prime},c_{s}^{\prime}\in C^{j-1}([0,1])
, by (
4.32
) and (
4.32
),
h_{r},g_{s}\in\mathcal{C}^{j-1}([0,1])
. Applying (i) to problem (
4.28
)-(
4.29
), we obtain
\partial{}_{\lambda}v(\cdot,\lambda_{0})\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j-1}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
.

We now show that
\partial{}_{\lambda}v
is indeed the
\lambda
-derivative of
v^{\lambda}
, namely
D^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}v
converges to
\partial{}_{\lambda}v(\cdot,\lambda_{0})
uniformly on
\mathbb{D}
, as
\lambda\to\lambda_{0}
.

Now
u^{\lambda}:=D^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}v-\partial{}_{\lambda}v(\cdot,\lambda_{0})
is the unique solution to

(4.33)

\begin{cases}\partial{}_{\overline{\zeta}}u^{\lambda}+A_{1}^{\lambda_{0}}u^{\lambda}+B_{1}^{\lambda_{0}}\overline{u^{\lambda}}=F^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}_{\ast}-F^{\lambda_{0}}_{\ast}&\text{in $\mathbb{D}$;}\\
Re[\overline{\widetilde{l^{\lambda_{0}}}}u^{\lambda}]=\gamma_{\ast}^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}-\gamma^{\lambda_{0}}_{\ast}\quad\quad\text{on $S^{1}$,}\end{cases}

which satisfies the condition: for
r=1,\dots,N_{0}
and
s=1,\dots,N_{1},

(4.34)

\begin{split}u^{\lambda}(\zeta_{r})=h_{r}^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}-h_{r}(\lambda_{0}),\quad u^{\lambda}(\zeta_{s}^{\prime})=g_{s}^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}-g_{s}(\lambda_{0}).\end{split}

where the right-hand sides are defined by (
4.27
).
We now show that
|u^{\lambda}|_{0}
converges to
0
as
\lambda\to\lambda_{0}
. Reducing the problem as before to the form

(4.35)

\begin{cases}\partial{}_{\overline{\zeta}}\widehat{u}^{\lambda}+A^{\lambda_{0}}_{0}\widehat{u}^{\lambda}+B^{\lambda_{0}}_{0}\overline{\widehat{u}^{\lambda}}=F^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}_{0}&\text{in $\mathbb{D}$;}\\
Re[\zeta^{-n}\widehat{u}^{\lambda}]=\gamma_{0}^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}&\text{on $S^{1}$}\end{cases}

satisfying the conditions:

(4.36)

\begin{split}\widehat{u}^{\lambda}(\zeta_{r})=\widehat{h}_{r}(\lambda),\quad(r=1,...,N_{0});\quad\widehat{u}^{\lambda}(\zeta_{s}^{\prime})=\widehat{g}_{s}(\lambda),\quad(s=1,...,N_{1})\end{split}

where

\displaystyle\widehat{u}^{\lambda}=e^{\chi^{\lambda_{0}}}u^{\lambda},\quad A_{0}^{\lambda_{0}}=A_{1}^{\lambda_{0}},\qquad B_{0}^{\lambda_{0}}=B_{1}^{\lambda_{0}}e^{2iIm(\chi^{\lambda_{0}})},

(4.37)

\displaystyle F_{0}^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}=\left(F^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}_{\ast}-F^{\lambda_{0}}_{\ast}\right)e^{\chi^{\lambda_{0}}},\qquad\gamma_{0}^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}=\left(\gamma_{\ast}^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}-\gamma^{\lambda_{0}}_{\ast}\right)e^{p^{\lambda_{0}}},

(4.38)

\displaystyle\widehat{h}_{r}(\lambda)=h_{r}^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}-h_{r}(\lambda_{0}),\qquad\widehat{g}_{s}(\lambda)=g_{s}^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}-g_{s}(\lambda_{0}),

where as before
\chi^{\lambda_{0}}
is the holomorphic function in
\mathbb{D}
defined by (
3.3
).
The solution to (
4.35
)-(
4.36
) is given by

(4.39)

\widehat{u}^{\lambda}=u_{0}^{\lambda}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{2n+1}d_{\ell}(\lambda)u_{\ell}^{\lambda},

where
u_{0}^{\lambda}
is a particular solution which uniquely solves the integral equation:

u_{0}^{\lambda}+Q_{n}^{\lambda_{0}}u_{0}^{\lambda}=P_{n}(F_{0}^{\lambda})+\frac{z^{n}}{2\pi i}\int_{b\mathbb{D}}\gamma_{0}^{\lambda}(t)\frac{t+z}{t-z}\frac{dt}{t}.

Here
Q_{n}^{\lambda_{0}}
and
P_{n}^{\lambda_{0}}
are the compact operators in the space
C^{0}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
defined by (
3.15
) and (
3.11
), respectively.
Since
I+Q^{\lambda_{0}}_{n}
is an injective (Vekua
[
7
, p. 268]
), by Fredholm alternative, it is also surjective. By the open mapping theorem, the inverse
(I-Q^{\lambda_{0}}_{n})^{-1}
is continuous. Denoting its operator norm by
M_{0}
, we get

|u_{0}^{\lambda}|_{0}\leq M_{0}\left(|F_{0}^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}|_{0}+|\gamma_{0}^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}|_{0}\right).

Since
v\in C^{0,0}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
from part (i), we obtain from expressions (
4.37
) that the right-hand side of the above equation converges to
0
as
\lambda\to\lambda_{0}
. Hence
|u_{0}^{\lambda}|_{0}\to 0
.

Finally we show
d_{\ell}(\lambda)
converges to
0
. We have

\displaystyle\widehat{h}_{r}(\lambda)=\widehat{u}^{\lambda}(\zeta_{r})=u_{0}^{\lambda}(\zeta_{r})+\sum_{\ell=1}^{2n+1}d_{\ell}(\lambda)u_{\ell}^{\lambda}(\zeta_{r}),\quad r=1,...,N_{0};

\displaystyle\widehat{g}_{s}(\lambda)=\widehat{u}^{\lambda}(\zeta_{s}^{\prime})=u_{0}^{\lambda}(\zeta_{s}^{\prime})+\sum_{\ell=1}^{2n+1}d_{\ell}(\lambda)u_{\ell}^{\lambda}(\zeta_{s}^{\prime}),\quad s=1,...,N_{1}.

By (
4.38
),
\widehat{h}_{r}(\lambda)
,
\widehat{g}_{s}(\lambda)
converge to
0
as
\lambda\to\lambda_{0}
. Also
u_{0}^{\lambda}(\zeta_{r})
and
u_{0}^{\lambda}(\zeta_{s}^{\prime})
all converge to
0
as
\lambda\to\lambda_{0}
, and therefore same reasoning as before shows that
d_{\ell}(\lambda)
converges to
0
. By (
4.39
),
|\widehat{u}^{\lambda}|_{0}
converges to
0
, which implies
D^{\lambda,\lambda_{0}}v
converges to
\partial{}_{\lambda}v(\cdot,\lambda_{0})\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j-1}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
in the sup-norm. This completes the induction and we obtain
v\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
. Since
w^{\lambda}=v^{\lambda}\circ R^{\lambda}
,
w\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.
Proposition
4.7
gives a solution
w^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
. To finish the proof we need to integrate
w^{\lambda}
(as in (
4.2
)) to get a solution
U^{\lambda}
for the original elliptic boundary value problem. Denote the resulting integration constant by
c_{0}(\lambda)
. Then by combining
w^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
and the condition
u^{\lambda}(z_{0}^{\lambda})\in C^{j}([0,1])
in the statement of the theorem, we get
c_{0}\in C^{j}[0,1]
, and the proof of Theorem
4.1
is complete. ∎

5.
Kernels with parameter

Starting in this section we study the parameter problem on families of multiply-connected domains. As explained in the introduction, we can no longer fix the domain while preserving
\overline{\partial}
in the highest order term, since there is no Riemann mapping theorem in this case. More significantly, unlike in the simply-connected case where the solutions satisfy certain Fredholm equation, the solutions on multiply-connected domains in general cannot be expressed as solutions of integral equations directly. Instead we will follow Vekua to represent solutions by integral formulas (
3.31
). In this section we show how the kernels
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
depend on
\lambda
.

For a function
f^{\lambda}
defined on the domain
\Omega^{\lambda}
we will adopt the following notation:

f(z,\lambda):=f^{\lambda}\circ\Gamma^{\lambda}(z),\quad z\in\Omega,\quad\lambda\in[0,1].

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
be a bounded domain with
C^{1}
boundary, and
\Gamma^{\lambda}:\overline{\Omega}\to\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}}
be a
C^{1,0}
-embedding. Let
1\leq p\leq 2
. For each
\lambda
, suppose
f^{\lambda}\in L^{p}(\Omega^{\lambda})
and
|f(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}
is uniformly bounded in
\lambda
. Define

T_{\Omega^{\lambda}}f(\cdot,\lambda):=\iint_{\Omega}\frac{f(\zeta,\lambda)}{\zeta^{\lambda}-z^{\lambda}}\,dA(\zeta^{\lambda}).

Then the following inequality hold:

(5.1)

|T_{\Omega^{\lambda}}f(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{\gamma}_{\beta}(\Omega)}\leq C_{1,0}C(r,\gamma,\Omega)|f(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)},

where
\gamma
is an arbitrary number satisfying the inequality

1<\gamma<\frac{2p}{2-p},

and

\beta=\frac{1}{\gamma}-\frac{2-p}{2p}>0.

The constant
C(p,\gamma,\Omega)
depends only on
p,\gamma
and
\Omega
and does not depend on
\lambda
.

Proof.

Recall definition (
2.3
) for the norm
|\cdot|_{L^{\gamma}_{\beta}}
, so we have

\displaystyle|T_{\Omega^{\lambda}}f^{\lambda}(\cdot)|_{L^{\gamma}_{\beta}(\Omega^{\lambda})}=\left(\int_{\Omega}\left|\frac{f(\zeta,\lambda)}{\zeta^{\lambda}-z^{\lambda}}\right|^{\gamma}a^{\lambda}(\zeta)\,dA(\zeta)\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}

\displaystyle\quad+\sup_{h\in\mathbb{C}}\frac{1}{|h|^{\beta}}\left(\int_{\Omega}|f(\zeta+h,\lambda)-f(\zeta,\lambda)|^{\gamma}a^{\lambda}(\zeta)\,dA(\zeta)\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}\left\{\left(\int_{\Omega}\left|\frac{f(\zeta,\lambda)}{\zeta-z}\right|^{\gamma}\,d|\zeta|\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}+\sup_{h\in\mathbb{C}}\frac{1}{|h|^{\beta}}\left(\int_{\Omega}|f(\zeta+h,\lambda)-f(\zeta,\lambda)|^{\gamma}\,dA(\zeta)\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\right\}

\displaystyle=C_{1,0}|T_{\Omega}f(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{\gamma}_{\beta}(\Omega)},

where
a^{\lambda}(\zeta)=|\det(\Gamma^{\lambda})|
.
By the result in
[
7
, p. 47]
, for
\gamma,\beta,p
satisfying the above conditions, we have

|T_{\Omega}f^{\lambda}(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{\gamma}_{\beta}(\Omega)}\leq C(p,\gamma,\Omega)|f(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)},

from which estimate (
5.1
) then follows.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
2<p<\infty
. Suppose
\lambda\mapsto A(\cdot,\lambda),B(\cdot,\lambda)
are continuous maps from
[0,1]
to
L^{p}(\mathbb{C})
, where for each
\lambda\in[0,1]
,
A(\cdot,\lambda),B(\cdot,\lambda)\equiv 0
outside
\Omega
. For
j=1,2
, let

(5.2)

\omega_{j}(z,t,\lambda)=\frac{t^{\lambda}-z^{\lambda}}{\pi}\iint_{\Omega}\frac{A(\zeta,\lambda)X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda)+B(\zeta,\lambda)\overline{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda)}}{(\zeta^{\lambda}-z^{\lambda})(t^{\lambda}-\zeta^{\lambda})X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda)}\,dA(\zeta^{\lambda}).

Then we have

(5.3)

|\omega_{j}(\cdot,t,\lambda)|_{\alpha_{p}}\leq C_{1,0}C(p,\Omega)\left(|A(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+|B(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\right),\quad\alpha_{p}=\frac{p-2}{p},

where the constants is uniform in
\lambda
and
t\in K
, for any compact subset
K
of
\mathbb{C}
.
In particular, if
A^{\lambda},B^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{0,0}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
, then

|\omega_{j}(\cdot,t,\lambda)|_{\tau}\leq C_{1,0}C(\tau,\Omega)\left(\|A^{\lambda}\|_{0,0}+\|B^{\lambda}\|_{0,0}\right),\quad\quad\text{for any $0<\tau<1$}.

Proof.

Let
a^{\lambda}(\zeta)=|\det\Gamma^{\lambda}(\zeta)|
. We can rewrite
\omega_{j}
as

\displaystyle\omega_{j}(z,t,\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Omega}\left[\frac{1}{\zeta^{\lambda}-z^{\lambda}}-\frac{1}{\zeta^{\lambda}-t^{\lambda}}\right]\left[A(\zeta,\lambda)+B(\zeta,\lambda)\frac{\overline{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda)}}{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda)}\right]a^{\lambda}(\zeta)\,dA(\zeta).

By Hölder’s inequality and Proposition
7.3
, we have for any
z,t\in\mathbb{C}
,

(5.4)

|\omega_{j}(z,t,\lambda)|\leq C_{1,0}C(p,\Omega)\left(|A(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+|B(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\right);

(5.5)

|\omega_{j}(z,t,\lambda)|\leq C_{1,0}C_{p}\left(|A(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+|B(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\right)|z-t|^{\alpha_{p}};

(5.6)

\displaystyle|\omega_{j}(z_{1},t,\lambda)-\omega_{j}(z_{2},t,\lambda)|\leq C_{1,0}C_{p}\left(|A(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+|B(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\right)|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{\alpha_{p}}

for any
z_{1},z_{2},t\in\mathbb{C}
. ∎

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\mathbb{C}
with
C^{1}
boundary, and
\Gamma:\Omega\to\Omega^{\lambda}
be a
\mathcal{C}^{1,0}
embedding. Let
2<p<\infty
, and
p^{\prime}=\frac{p}{p-1}
be the conjugate of
p
, with
1<p^{\prime}<2
. Suppose
\lambda\mapsto A(\cdot,\lambda),B(\cdot,\lambda)
are continuous maps from
[0,1]
to
L^{p}(\mathbb{C})
, where for each
\lambda\in[0,1]
,
A(\cdot,\lambda)
,
B(\cdot,\lambda)

\equiv 0
outside
\Omega
(In particular
A,B\in L_{p,2}(\mathbb{C})
,
p>2
.) Let
K
be a compact subset of
\mathbb{C}
. For any
q
with
1<p^{\prime}\leq q<2
, and for each
\lambda\in[0,1]
, let
X_{1}(z,t,\lambda)
and
X_{2}(z,t,\lambda)
be the unique solution in
L_{q,0}(\mathbb{C})
to the integral equations (see
[
7
, p. 156]
):
for
i=1,2
,

(5.7)

X_{j}(z,t,\lambda)-\frac{1}{\pi}\iint_{\Omega}\frac{A(\zeta,\lambda)X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda)+B(\zeta,\lambda)\overline{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda)}}{\zeta^{\lambda}-z^{\lambda}}\,dA(\zeta^{\lambda})=g_{i}(z,t,\lambda),

where
g_{1}(z,t,\lambda)=\frac{1}{2(t^{\lambda}-z^{\lambda})}
and
g_{2}(z,t,\lambda)=\frac{1}{2i(t^{\lambda}-z^{\lambda})}
.
Then the following statements hold:

(i) For each
t\in\mathbb{C}
,
\lambda\mapsto X_{j}(\cdot,t,\lambda)
is a continuous map from
[0,1]
to
L^{q}(\Omega)
. Moreover, for all
t\in K
we have

(5.8)

\left|X_{j}(\cdot,t,\lambda)\right|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}\leq C_{1,0}C(p,\Omega)\left(\left|A(\cdot,\lambda)\right|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\left|B(\cdot,\lambda)\right|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\right),

where the constants on the right-hand side is uniform in
\lambda
and
t\in K
.

(ii)

\sup_{t\in K}|X_{j}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{1})-X_{j}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{2})|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}\to 0\quad\text{as $|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|\to 0$.}

(iii) For each
\varepsilon>0
, let
E_{\varepsilon}(t)=\{z\in\Omega:|z-t|>\varepsilon\}
. Then we have

\sup_{t\in K}|X_{j}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{1})-X_{j}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{2})|_{C^{0}(E_{\varepsilon}(t))}\to 0\quad\text{as $|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|\to 0$.}

Proof.

(i) We shall only prove the lemma for
X_{1}
, as the proof for
X_{2}
is identical. First, we show the
L^{q}
norm of
X_{j}
is uniformly bounded in
\lambda
and
t\in K
. Seeking a contradiction, suppose there exists a sequence
\lambda_{j}\in[0,1]
such that
N_{j}:=|X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}\to\infty
as
j\to\infty
. Since
[0,1]
is compact we can assume that
\lambda_{j}\to\lambda_{0}\in[0,1]
for some
\lambda_{0}
. Dividing both sides of equation (
5.7
) by
N_{j}
, and writing
\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})=\frac{1}{N_{j}}X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})
, we obtain

(5.9)

\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})-P^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})=\frac{1}{2(t^{\lambda_{j}}-z^{\lambda_{j}})N_{j}},

where
|\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}=1
and we set

\displaystyle P^{\lambda}f(z,t,\lambda)=T_{\mathbb{C}}[A(\cdot,\lambda)f(\cdot,t,\lambda)+B(\cdot,\lambda)\overline{f(\cdot,t,\lambda)}](z,t,\lambda).

Setting
r=1
in Lemma
5.1
and applying Hölder’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\left|P^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})\right|_{L^{\gamma}_{\alpha}(\Omega)}\leq C(p,\gamma,\Omega)\left|(A+B)(\cdot,\lambda_{j})\widehat{X}_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})\right|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C(p,\gamma,\Omega)\left(|A(\cdot,\lambda_{j})|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+|B(\cdot,\lambda_{j})|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\right)|\widehat{X}_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}

for
1<\gamma<\frac{2\cdot 1}{2-1}=2
and
\alpha=\frac{1}{\gamma}-\frac{1}{2}>0
.
In particular we can choose
\gamma=q
, since
1<p^{\prime}\leq q<2
.
By
[
7
, Theorem 1.3]
applied to the sequence
P^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})
, and passing to a subsequence if necessary, we can assume that
P^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})
converges to some limit in
L^{q}(\Omega)
.
Meanwhile the right-hand side of (
5.9
) converges to
0
in
L^{q}(\Omega)
. Therefore by (
5.9
)
\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})
converges to some
\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t)
in
L^{q}(\Omega)
.

We show that
|P^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})-P^{\lambda_{0}}\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t)|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}\to 0
as
\lambda_{j}\to\lambda_{0}
.
In what follows we write
a^{\lambda}(\zeta)
for
|\det D\Gamma^{\lambda}(\zeta)|
.
Writing
D^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{X_{1}}(\zeta,t)=\widehat{X_{1}}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{j})-\widehat{X_{1}}(\zeta,t)
, we have

(5.10)

|P^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})-P^{\lambda_{0}}\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t)|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}\leq D_{1}+D_{2}+D_{3}+D_{4},

where

\displaystyle D_{1}=\left|\int_{\Omega}\frac{(\delta^{\lambda_{j},\lambda_{0}}A)\widehat{X_{1}}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{j})+A(\zeta,\lambda_{0})(D^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{X_{1}}(\zeta,t))}{\zeta^{\lambda_{j}}-z^{\lambda_{j}}}\,dA(\zeta^{\lambda_{j}})|\right|_{L^{q}(\Omega)},

\displaystyle D_{2}=\left|\int_{\Omega}\frac{(\delta^{\lambda_{j},\lambda_{0}}B)\widehat{X_{1}}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{j})+B(\zeta,\lambda_{0})\overline{D^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{X_{1}}(\zeta,t)}}{\zeta^{\lambda_{j}}-z^{\lambda_{j}}}\,dA(\zeta^{\lambda_{j}})|\right|_{L^{q}(\Omega)},

\displaystyle D_{3}=\left|\int_{\Omega}\frac{A(\zeta,\lambda_{0})\widehat{X_{1}}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{0})+B(\zeta,\lambda_{0})\overline{\widehat{X_{1}}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{0})}}{\zeta^{\lambda_{j}}-z^{\lambda_{j}}}\left\{a^{\lambda_{j}}(\zeta)-a^{\lambda_{0}}(\zeta)\right\}\,dA(\zeta)\right|_{L^{q}(\Omega)},

\displaystyle D_{4}=\left|\int_{\Omega}\left(A(\zeta,\lambda_{0})\widehat{X_{1}}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{0})+B(\zeta,\lambda_{0})\overline{\widehat{X_{1}}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{0})}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\zeta^{\lambda_{j}}-z^{\lambda_{j}}}-\frac{1}{\zeta^{\lambda_{0}}-z^{\lambda_{0}}}\right)\,dA(\zeta^{\lambda_{0}})\right|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}.

By estimate (
5.1
) applied to
\gamma=q
and
r=1
and Hölder’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}C(q,\Omega)\left(|(\delta^{\lambda_{j},\lambda_{0}}A)\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t,\lambda)|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}+|A(\cdot,\lambda_{0})(D^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t))|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}C(q,\Omega)\left(|\delta^{\lambda_{j},\lambda_{0}}A|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}|\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t,\lambda)|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}+|A(\zeta,\lambda_{0})|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}|D^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{X_{1}}|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}\right),

where
1<p^{\prime}\leq q<2
. By assumption, this expression goes to
0
. Similarly we can show that
D_{2}
converges to
0
, and also

D_{3}\leq C_{1,0}C(q,\Omega)\left|\Gamma^{\lambda_{j}}-\Gamma^{\lambda_{0}}\right|_{1}\left\{\left|A(\cdot,\lambda_{0})\right|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\left|B(\cdot,\lambda_{0})\right|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\right\}|\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{0})|_{L^{q}(\Omega)},

\displaystyle D_{4}

\displaystyle\leq\left|\int_{\Omega}\left(A(\zeta,\lambda_{0})\widehat{X_{1}}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{0})+B(\zeta,\lambda_{0})\overline{\widehat{X_{1}}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{0})}\right)\frac{\delta_{\zeta,z}(\Gamma^{\lambda_{0}}-\Gamma^{\lambda_{j}})\,dA(\zeta^{\lambda_{0}})}{(\zeta^{\lambda_{j}}-z^{\lambda_{j}})(\zeta^{\lambda_{0}}-z^{\lambda_{0}})}\right|_{L^{q}_{z}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}C(q,\Omega)\left|\Gamma^{\lambda_{0}}-\Gamma^{\lambda_{j}}\right|_{1}\left\{\left|A(\cdot,\lambda_{0})\right|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\left|B(\cdot,\lambda_{0})\right|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\right\}|\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{0})|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}.

Hence
D_{3},D_{4}\to 0
as
|\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{0}|\to 0
. By (
5.10
),
|P^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})-P^{\lambda_{0}}\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t)|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}
tends to
0
as
|\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{0}|\to 0
.

Letting
j\to\infty
in equation (
5.9
), we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\widehat{X_{1}}(z,t)-T_{\mathbb{C}}[A(\cdot,\lambda_{0})\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t)+B(\cdot,\lambda_{0})\overline{\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t)}](z,t)

holds in
L^{q}(\Omega)
.
By the result in
[
7
, p. 156]
, the equation has only the trivial solution, so
\widehat{X_{1}}\equiv 0
. But
|\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t)|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}=\lim_{j\to\infty}|\widehat{X_{1}}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}=1
, which is a contradiction. Thus
|X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda)|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}
is bounded, for any
q
with
1<p^{\prime}\leq q<2
.

Next, we show that
X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda)
is continuous in the
L^{q}(\Omega)
-norm, for any
q
with
1<p^{\prime}\leq q<2
. Otherwise there exists some sequence
\lambda_{j}\to\lambda_{0}
, such that
|X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})-X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{0})|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}\geq\delta>0
.
Since we have already shown that
|X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda)|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}
is bounded uniformly in
\lambda
, by the computation above,
|P^{\lambda_{j}}X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})|_{L^{q}_{\alpha}(\Omega)}
is bounded uniformly in
\lambda
.
Passing to a subsequence if necessary,
P^{\lambda_{j}}X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})
converges to some function in
L^{q}(\Omega)
. The right-hand side of the integral equation (
5.7
) converges to
\frac{1}{t^{\lambda_{0}}-z^{\lambda_{0}}}
in
L^{q}({\Omega})
. Indeed, we have

\left(\iint_{\Omega}\left|\frac{1}{t^{\lambda_{j}}-z^{\lambda_{j}}}-\frac{1}{t^{\lambda_{0}}-z^{\lambda_{0}}}\right|^{q}\,dA(z)\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq C_{1,0}|\Gamma^{\lambda_{j}}-\Gamma^{\lambda_{0}}|_{1}\left(\iint_{\Omega}\frac{1}{|t-z|^{q}}\,dA(z)\right)^{\frac{1}{q}},

which converges to
0
as
|\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{0}|\to 0
, since
\Gamma\in C^{1,0}(\overline{\Omega})
.
It follows from equation (
5.7
) that
X_{1}(z,t,\lambda_{j})
converges to some
X^{\ast}_{1}(z,t)
in
L^{q}(\Omega)
. As before we can show that
|P^{\lambda_{j}}X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{j})-P^{\lambda_{0}}X_{1}^{\ast}(\cdot,t)|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}\to 0
. Taking
j\to\infty
in equation (
5.7
) we obtain

X_{1}^{\ast}(z,t)-P^{\lambda_{0}}X_{1}^{\ast}(z,t)=\frac{1}{2(t^{\lambda_{0}}-z^{\lambda_{0}})},\quad\quad\text{in $L^{q}(\Omega)$}.

On the other hand, since
X_{1}(z,t,\lambda_{0})
satisfies the same equation. By uniqueness of solution we have

X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{0})=X_{1}^{\ast}(\cdot,t)
in
L^{q}(\Omega)
. But
|X_{1}^{\ast}(\cdot,t)-X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{0})|_{L^{q}(\overline{\Omega})}\geq\delta>0
, which is a contradiction. This shows that of
X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda)
in continuous in
\lambda
in the
L^{q}(\Omega)
- norm.

Finally to obtain estimate (
5.8
) we write
X_{1}
in the form (
3.22
):

(5.11)

X_{1}(z,t,\lambda)=\frac{e^{\omega_{1}(z,t,\lambda)}}{t^{\lambda}-z^{\lambda}}.

By (
5.3
), we have

|\omega_{j}(\cdot,t,\lambda)|_{C^{\alpha_{p}}(\overline{\Omega})}\leq C_{1,0}C(p,\Omega)\left(|A(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+|B(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\right),

where
\alpha_{p}=\frac{p-2}{p}
, and the constants are independent of
\lambda
and
t\in K
. Estimate (
5.8
) then readily follows.
∎

(ii)
As in part (i) we only need to prove the statements for
X_{1}
. Let
D^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}X_{1}=X_{1}(z,t,\lambda_{1})-X_{1}(z,t,\lambda_{2})
. Then by (
5.3
), we see that
D^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}X_{1}
satisfies the following integral equation

D^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}X_{1}(z,t)+P^{\lambda_{2}}(D^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}X_{1})(z,t)=\sum_{i=1}^{4}R_{i}(z,t,\lambda_{1}),

where

P^{\lambda_{2}}f(z,t)=T_{\mathbb{C}}\left[A(\cdot,\lambda_{2})f(\cdot,t)+B(\cdot,\lambda_{2})\overline{f(\cdot,t)}\right]
and

\displaystyle R_{1}(z,t,\lambda_{1})=\iint_{\Omega}\frac{\left(A(\cdot,\lambda_{1})-A(\cdot,\lambda_{2})\right)X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{1})}{\zeta^{\lambda_{1}}-z^{\lambda_{1}}}\,dA(\zeta^{\lambda_{1}}),

\displaystyle R_{2}(z,t,\lambda_{1})=\iint_{\Omega}\frac{A(\cdot,\lambda_{2})X(\cdot,t,\lambda_{1})(a^{\lambda_{1}}-a^{\lambda_{2}})}{\zeta^{\lambda_{1}}-z^{\lambda_{1}}}\,dA(\zeta),

\displaystyle R_{3}(z,t,\lambda_{1})=\iint_{\Omega}A(\cdot,\lambda_{2})X(\cdot,t,\lambda_{1})\left(\frac{1}{\zeta^{\lambda_{1}}-z^{\lambda_{1}}}-\frac{1}{\zeta^{\lambda_{2}}-z^{\lambda_{2}}}\right)\,dA(\zeta^{\lambda_{2}}),

\displaystyle R_{4}(z,t,\lambda_{1})=\frac{1}{t^{\lambda_{1}}-z^{\lambda_{1}}}-\frac{1}{t^{\lambda_{2}}-z^{\lambda_{2}}}.

Since
|X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda)|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}
is bounded, we can show by the same proof as in (i) that
\sup_{t\in K}

|R_{i}(z,t,\lambda_{1})|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}
converges to
0
as
|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|\to 0
,
1\leq i\leq 4
. Let
(I+P^{\lambda_{2}})^{-1}:L^{q}(\Omega)\to L^{q}(\Omega)
be the inverse operator of
I+P^{\lambda_{2}}
. By the open mapping theorem,
(I+P^{\lambda_{2}})^{-1}
is continuous. Denoting its operator norm by
M_{\lambda_{2}}
, we have

\sup_{t\in K}|D^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}X_{1}(\cdot,t)|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}\leq M_{\lambda_{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{4}\sup_{t\in K}\left|R_{i}(z,t,\lambda_{1})\right|_{L^{q}(\Omega)},

which converges to
0
as
|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|\to 0
. ∎

(iii) We write
X_{1}
in the form (
5.11
),
where
\omega_{1}
is given by expression (
5.2
).
By Lemma
5.2
, if
|A(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}
,
|B(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}
are bounded uniformly in
\lambda
, then
|\omega_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda)|_{C^{\alpha_{p}}(\overline{\Omega})}
is bounded uniformly in
\lambda\in[0,1]
and
t\in K
, for

\alpha_{p}=\frac{p-2}{p}
. In view of (
5.11
),
|X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda)|_{C^{\alpha_{p}}(E_{\varepsilon})}
is bounded uniformly in
\lambda
and
t
. By part (ii),
\sup_{t\in K}|X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{1})-X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{2})|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}
converges to
0
as
|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|\to 0
. Applying Proposition
7.20
we get

\sup_{t\in K}|X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{1})-X_{1}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{2})|_{C^{0}(\Omega\cap E_{\varepsilon})}\longrightarrow 0.\qquad\qed

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
2<p<\infty
. Suppose
\lambda\mapsto A(\cdot,\lambda),B(\cdot,\lambda)
are continuous maps from
[0,1]
to
L^{p}(\mathbb{C})
, where for each
\lambda\in[0,1]
,
A(\cdot,\lambda),B(\cdot,\lambda)\equiv 0
outside
\Omega
. For
j=1,2
, let

\omega_{j}(z,t,\lambda)=\frac{t^{\lambda}-z^{\lambda}}{\pi}\iint_{\Omega^{\lambda}}\frac{A(\zeta,\lambda)X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda)+B(\zeta,\lambda)\overline{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda)}}{(\zeta^{\lambda}-z^{\lambda})(t^{\lambda}-\zeta^{\lambda})X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda)}\,dA(\zeta^{\lambda}).

Then for each
t
,
\lambda\mapsto\omega_{j}(\cdot,t,\lambda)\in C^{0}(\overline{\Omega})
is a continuous map from
[0,1]
to
C^{0}(\overline{\Omega})
. Moreover for any compact subset
K
in
\mathbb{C}
, we have

(5.12)

\sup_{t\in K}|\omega_{j}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{1})-\omega_{j}(\cdot,t,\lambda_{2})|_{C^{0}(\overline{\Omega})}\to 0\quad\text{as $|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|\to 0$.}

Proof.

Rewrite
\omega_{j}
as

\omega_{j}(z,t,\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\iint_{\Omega}\left[\frac{1}{\zeta^{\lambda}-z^{\lambda}}-\frac{1}{\zeta^{\lambda}-t^{\lambda}}\right]\\
\left[A(\zeta,\lambda)+B(\zeta,\lambda)\frac{\overline{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda)}}{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda)}\right]a(\zeta,\lambda)\,dA(\zeta),

where
a(\zeta,\lambda)=|(\det\Gamma^{\lambda})(\zeta)|
. We have

(5.13)

|\omega_{j}(z,t,\lambda_{1})-\omega_{j}(z,t,\lambda_{2})|\leq D_{1}+D_{2}+D_{3},

where

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{C}}\left\{\left|\frac{1}{\zeta^{\lambda_{1}}-z^{\lambda_{1}}}-\frac{1}{\zeta^{\lambda_{2}}-z^{\lambda_{2}}}\right|+\left|\frac{1}{\zeta^{\lambda_{1}}-t^{\lambda_{1}}}-\frac{1}{\zeta^{\lambda_{2}}-t^{\lambda_{2}}}\right|\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left|A(\zeta,\lambda_{1})+B(\zeta,\lambda_{1})\frac{\overline{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{1})}}{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{1})}\right|\,dA(\zeta^{\lambda_{1}});

\displaystyle D_{2}=\iint_{\mathbb{C}}\left|\frac{1}{\zeta^{\lambda_{2}}-z^{\lambda_{2}}}+\frac{1}{t^{\lambda_{2}}-\zeta^{\lambda_{2}}}\right||(Aa)(\zeta,\lambda_{1})-(Aa)(\zeta,\lambda_{2})|\,dA(\zeta);

\displaystyle D_{3}

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{C}}\left|\frac{1}{\zeta^{\lambda_{2}}-z^{\lambda_{2}}}+\frac{1}{t^{\lambda_{2}}-\zeta^{\lambda_{2}}}\right|\left|(Ba)(\zeta,\lambda_{1})\frac{\overline{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{1})}}{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{1})}-(Ba)(\zeta,\lambda_{2})\frac{\overline{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{2})}}{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{2})}\right|\,dA(\zeta).

Let
p^{\prime}
be the conjugate of
p
, with
1<p^{\prime}<2
. By Hölder’s inequality we have

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}|\Gamma^{\lambda_{1}}-\Gamma^{\lambda_{2}}|_{1}\int_{\Omega}\left(\frac{1}{|\zeta-z|}+\frac{1}{|\zeta-t|}\right)\left|A(\zeta,\lambda_{1})+B(\zeta,\lambda_{1})\frac{\overline{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{1})}}{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{1})}\right|\,dA(\zeta^{\lambda_{1}})

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}|\Gamma^{\lambda_{1}}-\Gamma^{\lambda_{2}}|_{1}\left(\|(\zeta-z)^{-1}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}+\|(\zeta-t)^{-1}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}\right)\left(|A(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+|B(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}C_{p}|\Gamma^{\lambda_{1}}-\Gamma^{\lambda_{2}}|_{1}\left(|A(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+|B(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\right),

and similarly for
D_{2}
:

D_{2}\leq C_{1,0}C_{p}\left(|\delta^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}A|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+|A(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}|\delta^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}\Gamma|_{1}\right).

Hence by our assumption,
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
converge to
0
and the convergence is uniform in
t\in K
.
For
D_{3}
we have
D_{3}\leq D_{31}+D_{32}
,
where

\displaystyle D_{31}=\int_{\mathbb{C}}\left|\frac{1}{\zeta^{\lambda_{2}}-z^{\lambda_{2}}}+\frac{1}{t^{\lambda_{2}}-\zeta^{\lambda_{2}}}\right|\left|\delta^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(Ba)\right|\left|\frac{\overline{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{1})}}{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{1})}\right|\,dA(\zeta),

\displaystyle D_{32}=\int_{\mathbb{C}}\left|\frac{(Ba)(\zeta,\lambda_{2})}{\zeta^{\lambda_{2}}-z^{\lambda_{2}}}+\frac{(Ba)(\zeta,\lambda_{2})}{t^{\lambda_{2}}-\zeta^{\lambda_{2}}}\right|\left|\frac{\overline{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{1})}}{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{1})}-\frac{\overline{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{2})}}{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{2})}\right|\,dA(\zeta).

By Hölder’s inequality,

D_{31}\leq C_{1,0}C_{p}|(Ba)(\cdot,\lambda_{1})-(Ba)(\cdot,\lambda_{2})|_{L^{p}(\Omega)},

which by assumption goes to
0
as
|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|\to 0
. On the other hand,

(5.14)

\displaystyle D_{32}\leq 2\int_{\mathbb{C}}\left|\frac{(Ba)(\zeta,\lambda_{2})}{\zeta^{\lambda_{2}}-z^{\lambda_{2}}}+\frac{(Ba)(\zeta,\lambda_{2})}{t^{\lambda_{2}}-\zeta^{\lambda_{2}}}\right|\left|\frac{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{1})-X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{2})}{X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda_{1})}\right|\,dA(\zeta).

By (
5.4
),
|\omega_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda)|
is bounded uniformly for any
\zeta,t\in\mathbb{C}
and
\lambda\in[0,1]
, and

X_{1}(\zeta,t,\lambda)=\frac{e^{\omega_{1}(\zeta,t,\lambda)}}{2(t^{\lambda}-\zeta^{\lambda})},\quad X_{2}(\zeta,t,\lambda)=\frac{e^{\omega_{2}(\zeta,t,\lambda)}}{2i(t^{\lambda}-\zeta^{\lambda})}.

Hence
\left|X_{j}(\zeta,t,\lambda)\right|\geq\frac{C_{1,0}}{|\zeta-t|}.

In what follows we write
L^{1}_{\zeta}(\Omega)
to indicate the integral is with respect to
\zeta
. Using the above estimate in (
5.14
) we have

\displaystyle D_{32}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}\left(\left|\frac{B(\zeta,\lambda_{2})}{\zeta^{\lambda_{2}}-z^{\lambda_{2}}}\left[\delta^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}X_{j}(\zeta,t)\right]\right|_{L^{1}_{\zeta}(\Omega)}+\left|B(\zeta,\lambda_{2})\left[\delta^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}X_{j}(\zeta,t)\right]\right|_{L^{1}_{\zeta}(\Omega)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}\left|B(\cdot,\lambda_{2})\right|_{L^{p}(\overline{\Omega})}\left(\left|\frac{\delta^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}X_{j}(\zeta,t)}{\zeta^{\lambda_{2}}-z^{\lambda_{2}}}\right|_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{\zeta}(\overline{\Omega})}+\left|\delta^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}X_{j}(\zeta,t)\right|_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{\zeta}(\overline{\Omega})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}\left|B(\cdot,\lambda_{2})\right|_{L^{p}(\overline{\Omega})}\left\{\left(\int_{\{\zeta:|\zeta-z|<\delta^{\prime}\}}\left|\frac{\delta^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}X_{j}(\zeta,t)}{\zeta-z}\right|^{p^{\prime}}\,dA(\zeta)\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}\right.

\displaystyle\quad+\left.\left(\int_{\{\zeta:|\zeta-z|\geq\delta^{\prime}\}}\left|\frac{\delta^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}X_{j}(\zeta,t)}{\zeta-z}\right|^{p^{\prime}}\,dA(\zeta)\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}+\left|\delta^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}X_{j}(\zeta,t)\right|_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{\zeta}(\overline{\Omega})}\right\},

where
\delta^{\prime}>0
is to be determined.
By Lemma
5.3
(ii),
\sup_{t\in K}|\delta^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}X_{j}(\cdot,t)|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}\to 0
as
|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|\to 0
. So for fixed
\delta^{\prime}
the last two terms in the above bracket converge to
0
.
By estimate (
5.5
),

\displaystyle|\omega_{j}(z,t,\lambda_{1})-\omega_{j}(z,t,\lambda_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq|\omega_{j}(z,t,\lambda_{1})|+|\omega_{j}(z,t,\lambda_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}C_{p}\left(|A(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\overline{\Omega})}+|B(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\overline{\Omega})}\right)|z-t|^{\alpha_{p}}.

For any
\varepsilon>0
, we can pick
r>0
such that for any
z\in\overline{\Omega}
and
t\in K
with
|z-t|<r
, one has
|\omega_{j}(z,t,\lambda_{1})-\omega_{j}(z,t,\lambda_{2})|<\varepsilon
. Then it suffices to consider the points
z
for which
|z-t|\geq r
.

Now for this fixed
r
, we can let
\delta^{\prime}=\frac{r}{2}
be such that for any
z
with
|z-t|\geq r
and
|\zeta-z|<\delta^{\prime}
, we have
|\zeta-t|\geq|t-z|-|z-\zeta|\geq\delta^{\prime}=\frac{r}{2}
, and so

\{\zeta:|\zeta-z|<\delta^{\prime}\}\subset\{\zeta:|\zeta-t|\geq\delta^{\prime}\}.

Hence

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\{\zeta:|\zeta-z|<\delta^{\prime}\}}\left|\frac{\delta^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}X_{j}(\zeta,t)}{\zeta-z}\right|^{p^{\prime}}\,dA(\zeta)\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\{\zeta:|\zeta-t|\geq\delta^{\prime}\}}\left|\frac{\delta^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}X_{j}(\zeta,t)}{\zeta-z}\right|^{p^{\prime}}\,dA(\zeta)\right)^{\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p^{\prime}}\sup_{t\in K}|\delta^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}X_{j}(\zeta,t)|_{C^{0}(\{\zeta:|\zeta-t|\geq\delta^{\prime}\})}.

The last expression goes to
0
as
|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|\to 0
, by Lemma
5.3
(iii). This shows that
D_{32}
converges to
0
as
|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|\to 0
and the convergence is uniform in
t\in K
. The proof of (
5.12
) is now complete.
∎

6.
Families of multiply-connected domains

In this section we prove Theorem
1.1
for families of bounded multiply-connected domains. We will first follow Vekua to reduce the problem to a singular integral equation. We then obtain regularities in all variables by transforming it into an equivalent Fredholm equation, so that a compactness argument can be used.

Just like in the simply-connected case, it suffices to consider the corresponding problem in the form (
4.3
).

Theorem 6.1

(Problem A on multiply-connected domains)
.

Let
k,j,\mu
be as in Theorem
1.1
.
Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\mathbb{C}
whose boundary is
C^{1+\mu}
and which has
m+1
connected components.
Let
\Gamma^{\lambda}:\Omega\to\Omega^{\lambda}
be a family of embeddings of class
\mathcal{C}^{1,0}(\Omega)
.
Fix a normally distributed set (Definition
3.6
)
\{\{z_{r}^{\lambda}\}_{r=1}^{N_{0}}
,
\{z_{s}^{\lambda}\}_{s=1}^{N_{1}}\}
in
\Omega
so that
\{z_{r}^{\lambda}=\Gamma^{\lambda}(z_{r}),z_{a}^{\lambda}=\Gamma^{\lambda}(z_{s})\}
forms a normally distributed set in
\Omega^{\lambda}
. For each
\lambda
, let
w^{\lambda}
be the unique solution to

(6.1)

\begin{cases}\partial{}_{\overline{z}}w^{\lambda}(z^{\lambda})+A^{\lambda}(z^{\lambda})w^{\lambda}(z^{\lambda})+B^{\lambda}(z^{\lambda})\overline{w^{\lambda}(z^{\lambda})}=F^{\lambda}(z^{\lambda})&\text{in $\Omega^{\lambda}$;}\\
Re[\overline{l^{\lambda}(z^{\lambda})}w^{\lambda}(z^{\lambda})]=\gamma^{\lambda}(z^{\lambda})&\text{on $b\Omega^{\lambda}$, }\end{cases}

where the index
n
(Definition
3.1
) satisfies
n>m-1
and
w^{\lambda}
satisfies the additional conditions

(6.2)

\begin{gathered}w^{\lambda}(z_{r}^{\lambda})=a_{r}(\lambda)+ib_{r}(\lambda),\quad\quad(r=1,...,N_{0});\\
w^{\lambda}((z_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})=l_{j}(z_{s}^{\prime},\lambda)(\gamma_{j}(z_{s}^{\prime},\lambda)+ic_{s}(\lambda)),\quad\quad(s=1,...,N_{1}).\end{gathered}

(i) Suppose for
2<p<\infty
,
\lambda\to A^{\lambda},B^{\lambda},F^{\lambda}
are continuous maps from
[0,1]
to
L^{p}(\Omega)
, and that
l^{\lambda},\gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{\mu,0}(b\Omega^{\lambda})
,
a_{r},b_{r},c_{s}\in C^{0}([0,1])
. Then
w^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{\nu,0}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
, where
\nu=min(\alpha_{p},\mu)
and
\alpha_{p}=\frac{p-2}{p}
.

(ii) If
b\Omega\in C^{k+1+\mu}
,
\Gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega})
,
A^{\lambda},B^{\lambda},F^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
,
l^{\lambda},\gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(b\Omega^{\lambda})
, then
w^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
, and there exists some constant
C
independent of
\lambda
such that

\displaystyle\|w^{\lambda}\|_{0,0}\leq C\left(\|F^{\lambda}\|_{0,0}+\|\gamma^{\lambda}\|_{\mu,0}+\sum_{r=1}^{N_{0}}\left(|a_{r}|_{0}+|b_{r}|_{0}\right)+\sum_{s=1}^{N_{1}}|c_{s}|_{0}\right),

\displaystyle\|w^{\lambda}\|_{k+1+\mu,0}\leq C\left(\|F^{\lambda}\|_{k+\mu,0}+\|\gamma^{\lambda}\|_{k+1+\mu,0}+\sum_{r=1}^{N_{0}}(|a_{r}|_{0}+|b_{r}|_{0})+\sum_{s=1}^{N_{1}}|c_{s}|_{0}\right).

(iii) If
b\Omega\in C^{k+1+\mu}
,
\Gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
,
A^{\lambda},B^{\lambda},F^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
,
l^{\lambda},\gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(b\Omega^{\lambda})
, then
w^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
, and there exists some constant
C
independent of
\lambda
such that

\|w^{\lambda}\|_{k+1+\mu,j}\leq C\left(\|F^{\lambda}\|_{k+\mu,j}+\|\gamma^{\lambda}\|_{k+1+\mu,j}+\sum_{r=1}^{N_{0}}\left(|a_{r}|_{j}+|b_{r}|_{j}\right)+\sum_{s=1}^{N_{1}}|c_{s}|_{j}\right).

As an immediate consequence of Theorem
6.1
, we can now prove Theorem
1.1
for the case
\varkappa>m-1
, where
\varkappa
is the index for the oblique derivative boundary value problem. (See Definition
4.1
).

Theorem 6.2

(Multiply-connected domains,
\varkappa>m-1
)
.

Let
k,j,\mu
be as in Theorem
1.1
. Let
\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\mathbb{C}
with
\mathcal{C}^{k+2+\mu}
boundary. Let
\Gamma^{\lambda}
be a family of maps that embed
\Omega
to
\Omega^{\lambda}
, with
\Gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+2+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
. Consider the following oblique derivative problem:

(6.3)

\begin{gathered}a^{\lambda}(x,y)u^{\lambda}_{xx}+2b^{\lambda}(x,y)u^{\lambda}_{xy}+c^{\lambda}(x,y)u^{\lambda}_{yy}+d^{\lambda}(x,y)u^{\lambda}_{x}+e^{\lambda}(x,y)u^{\lambda}_{y}=f^{\lambda},\quad\text{in $\Omega^{\lambda}$; }\\
\frac{du^{\lambda}}{d\Upsilon^{\lambda}}=\gamma^{\lambda}\quad\text{on $b\Omega^{\lambda}$, }\end{gathered}

with index
\varkappa>m-1
. Here
\Upsilon^{\lambda}=\xi^{\lambda}+i\eta^{\lambda}
is a non-vanishing vector field along
b\Omega^{\lambda}
. Suppose
a^{\lambda},b^{\lambda},c^{\lambda},d^{\lambda},e^{\lambda},f^{\lambda}
are functions in
\Omega^{\lambda}
such that
a^{\lambda},b^{\lambda},c^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
, and
d^{\lambda},e^{\lambda},f^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
.
Suppose further that
\Upsilon^{\lambda},\gamma^{\lambda}
are functions in
b\Omega^{\lambda}
such that
\Upsilon^{\lambda},\gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(b\Omega^{\lambda})
.
For each
\lambda\in[0,1]
, let
\{\{z_{r}^{\lambda}\}_{r=1}^{N_{0}}
,
\{z_{s}^{\lambda}\}_{s=1}^{N_{1}}\}
be a normally distributed set for
\Omega^{\lambda}
, and let
u^{\lambda}
be the unique solution to (
6.3
) on
\Omega^{\lambda}
satisfying the conditions (see
[
7
, Theorem 4.13]
)

\displaystyle u^{\lambda}_{x}(z_{r}^{\lambda})-iu^{\lambda}_{y}(z_{r}^{\lambda})=a_{r}(\lambda)+ib_{r}(\lambda)\quad\quad(r=1,...,N_{0});

\displaystyle u^{\lambda}_{x}((z_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})-iu^{\lambda}_{y}((z_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})=l^{\lambda}((z_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})(\gamma^{\lambda}((z_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})+ic_{s}(\lambda)),\quad\quad(s=1,...,N_{1});

(6.4)

\displaystyle u^{\lambda}(z_{0}^{\lambda})=e(\lambda),\quad z_{0}^{\lambda}\in\Omega^{\lambda},

where
a_{r},b_{r},c_{s},e\in C^{j}([0,1])
. Then
u^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+2+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi^{\lambda}
be the maps defined as in Proposition
2.10
. Then
\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}:=u^{\lambda}\circ(\varphi^{\lambda})^{-1}
is a solution to the following problem

\displaystyle\Delta\,\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}+p^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta)\,\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}_{\xi}+q^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta)\,\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}_{\eta}=h^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta),\quad\text{in $D^{\lambda}$;}

\displaystyle\nu_{1}^{\lambda}\,\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}_{\xi}+\nu_{2}^{\lambda}\,\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}_{\eta}=g^{\lambda}(\xi,\eta),\quad\text{on $bD^{\lambda}$,}

where
p^{\lambda},q^{\lambda},h^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+\mu,j}(\overline{D^{\lambda}})
and
\nu_{1}^{\lambda},\nu_{2}^{\lambda},g^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{D^{\lambda}})
. Furthermore
\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}_{\xi}(\zeta_{r}^{\lambda})-i\,\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}_{\eta}(\zeta_{r}^{\lambda})=\widetilde{a_{r}}(\lambda)+i\widetilde{b_{r}}(\lambda)\quad\quad(r=1,...,N_{0});

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}_{\xi}((\zeta_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})-i\,\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}_{\eta}((\zeta_{s}^{\prime})^{\lambda})=\widetilde{c}_{s}(\lambda)+i\widetilde{d}_{s}(\lambda),\quad\quad(s=1,...,N_{1});

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}(\zeta_{0}^{\lambda})=\widetilde{e}(\lambda),\quad z_{0}^{\lambda}\in\Omega^{\lambda},

where
\{\zeta_{r}^{\lambda},(\zeta^{\prime}_{s})^{\lambda}\}
is the image of
\{z^{\lambda}_{r},(z^{\prime}_{s})^{\lambda}\}
under the map
\varphi^{\lambda}
, and
\widetilde{a_{r}},\widetilde{b_{r}},\widetilde{c_{s}},\widetilde{d_{s}},\widetilde{e}\in C^{j}([0,1])
.

Let
w^{\lambda}=\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}_{\xi}-i\,\mathcal{U}_{\eta}^{\lambda}
be the unique solution in
\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega})
to problem (
6.1
) as given in Theorem
6.1
, where we take

\displaystyle A^{\lambda}=\frac{1}{4}\left(p^{\lambda}+iq^{\lambda}\right),\ B^{\lambda}=\frac{1}{4}\left(p^{\lambda}-iq^{\lambda}\right),\quad F^{\lambda}=\frac{1}{2}h^{\lambda},\quad l^{\lambda}=\nu_{1}^{\lambda}-i\nu_{2}^{\lambda},\quad\gamma^{\lambda}=g^{\lambda}.

and
w^{\lambda}
satisfies

\displaystyle w^{\lambda}(\zeta_{r}^{\lambda})=\widetilde{a_{r}}(\lambda)+i\widetilde{b_{r}}(\lambda),\quad w^{\lambda}((\zeta^{\prime}_{s})^{\lambda})=\widetilde{c}_{s}(\lambda)+i\widetilde{d}_{s}(\lambda).

for
r=1,\dots,N_{0}
and
s=1,\dots,N_{1}
.
Set

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}(\zeta^{\lambda})=c_{0}(\lambda)+Re\int_{\zeta_{0}^{\lambda}}^{z^{\lambda}}w^{\lambda}(\zeta)\,d\zeta^{\lambda},\quad z^{\lambda}=x^{\lambda}+iy^{\lambda}\in\Omega^{\lambda}.

\displaystyle u^{\lambda}(z^{\lambda})=\mathcal{U}^{\lambda}(\varphi^{\lambda}(z^{\lambda}))=c_{0}(\lambda)+Re\int_{\zeta_{0}^{\lambda}}^{\varphi^{\lambda}(z^{\lambda})}w^{\lambda}(\zeta)\,d\zeta^{\lambda}.

By (
6.4
) and
e\in C^{j}([0,1])
, we have
c_{0}\in C^{j}([0,1])
. Hence
u^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{k+2+\mu,j}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
.
∎

For the rest of the section we prove Theorem
6.1
.
In §6.1 and §6.2 we prove part (i). We reduce problem A (
6.1
) to a singular integral equation with parameter. We then transform the singular integral equation into a Fredholm equation and derive all the relevant estimates. In the end we apply a standard compactness argument to show that the solution
w^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{\nu^{\prime},0}(b\Omega^{\lambda})
, for any
0<\nu^{\prime}<\nu
, where
\nu=\min(\alpha_{p},\mu)
,
\alpha_{p}=\frac{p-2}{p}
. We then use the
\mathcal{C}^{\nu-,0}(b\Omega^{\lambda})
estimate to prove the
\mathcal{C}^{\nu,0}(b\Omega^{\lambda})
estimate. For our proof we apply a trick of Vekua to reduce the problem to the case for families of simply-connected domains.
In §6.3, we derive the
\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,0}(b\Omega^{\lambda})
estimate for
w^{\lambda}
.
In §6.4, we take the derivatives in
\lambda
and derive the
\mathcal{C}^{k+1+\mu,j}(b\Omega^{\lambda})
estimate for
w^{\lambda}
. This completes the proof.

6.1.
The
\mathcal{C}^{\nu^{-},0}
estimate

Recall the notation:
f(z,\lambda):=f^{\lambda}(z^{\lambda})
, for
(z,\lambda)\in\overline{\Omega}\times[0,1]
.

Step 1: Setup of the singular integral equation.

For each
\lambda
, let
w^{\lambda}
be the unique solution to the boundary value problem (
6.1
) satisfying condition (
6.2
).
Since
Re[\overline{l(t,\lambda)}w(t,\lambda)]=\gamma(t,\lambda)
on
b\Omega
, there exists a real-valued function
\eta
such that

(6.5)

w(t,\lambda)=l(t,\lambda)\gamma(t,\lambda)+il(t,\lambda)\eta(t,\lambda),\quad(t,\lambda)\in b\Omega\times[0,1].

Then the solution
w^{\lambda}
can be represented by formula (
3.31
):

(6.6)

w(z,\lambda)=w_{\gamma}(z,\lambda)+w_{\eta}(z,\lambda)+w_{F}(z,\lambda),\quad z\in\Omega,

where

(6.7)

\displaystyle w_{\gamma}(z,\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{b\Omega}G_{1}(z,t,\lambda)l(t,\lambda)\gamma(t,\lambda)\,dt^{\lambda}-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{b\Omega}G_{2}(z,t,\lambda)\overline{l(t,\lambda)}\gamma(t,\lambda)\,\overline{dt^{\lambda}},

(6.8)

\displaystyle w_{\eta}(z,\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{b\Omega}G_{1}(z,t,\lambda)l(t,\lambda)\eta(t,\lambda)\,dt^{\lambda}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{b\Omega}G_{2}(z,t,\lambda)\overline{l(t,\lambda)}\eta(t,\lambda)\,\overline{dt^{\lambda}},

(6.9)

\displaystyle w_{F}(z,\lambda)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{\pi}\iint_{\Omega}G_{1}(z,\zeta,\lambda)F(\zeta,\lambda)\,dA(\zeta^{\lambda})-\frac{1}{\pi}\iint_{\Omega}G_{2}(z,\zeta,\lambda)\overline{F(\zeta,\lambda)}\,dA(\zeta^{\lambda}).

Here
G_{i}(z,t,\lambda)
is the fundamental kernel of the equation:

\partial{}_{\overline{z}}w+A(z,\lambda)w+B(z,\lambda)\overline{w}=0,\quad z\in\mathbb{C},

where for
z\notin\Omega
, we take
A(z,\lambda)\equiv B(z,\lambda)\equiv 0
.

According to (
3.27
),
G_{i}(z,t,\lambda)
has the representations:

G_{1}(z,t,\lambda)=\frac{e^{\omega_{1}(z,t,\lambda)}+e^{\omega_{2}(z,t,\lambda)}}{t^{\lambda}-z^{\lambda}},\quad G_{2}(z,t,\lambda)=\frac{e^{\omega_{1}(z,t,\lambda)}-e^{\omega_{2}(z,t,\lambda)}}{t^{\lambda}-z^{\lambda}},

where
\omega_{i}(z,t,\lambda)-s
are given by formula (
5.2
).

By (
3.29
), it can be shown easily that for fixed
\lambda
and
F(\cdot,\lambda)\in L^{p}(\Omega)
,
w_{F}(\cdot,\lambda)
is continuous in
\mathbb{C}
.

Referring to
[
7
, p. 234]
,
\eta
satisfies the singular integral equation

(6.10)

\mathcal{K}^{\lambda}\eta(\zeta,\lambda)=\int_{b\Omega}K(\zeta,t,\lambda)\eta(t,\lambda)\,ds=\gamma_{0}(\zeta,\lambda),

where

(6.11)

\displaystyle\gamma_{0}(\zeta,\lambda)=\gamma(\zeta,\lambda)-Re[\overline{l(\zeta,\lambda)}w_{\gamma}^{+}(\zeta,\lambda)]-Re[\overline{l(\zeta,\lambda)}w_{F}(\zeta,\lambda)],

\displaystyle K(\zeta,t,\lambda)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}Re\left[l(t,\lambda)(\rho^{\lambda})^{\prime}(s)\left(G_{1}(\zeta,t,\lambda)\overline{l(\zeta,\lambda)}+\overline{G_{2}(\zeta,t,\lambda)}l(\zeta,\lambda)\right)\right].

In addition,
\eta
satisfies the following conditions:

(6.12)

\begin{gathered}w_{\eta}(z_{r},\lambda)=a_{r}(\lambda)+ib_{r}(\lambda)-w_{\gamma}(z_{r},\lambda)-w_{F}(z_{r},\lambda)\\
w_{\eta}^{+}(z_{s}^{\prime},\lambda)=l(z_{s}^{\prime},\lambda)(\gamma(z_{s}^{\prime},\lambda)+ic_{s}(\lambda))-w_{\gamma}^{+}(z_{s}^{\prime},\lambda)-w_{F}(z_{s}^{\prime},\lambda)\end{gathered}

where
\{z_{r},z_{s}^{\prime}\}
for
r=1,\dots,N_{0}
and
s=1,\dots,N_{1}
is a normally distributed set on
\Omega
. Here we use
w_{\gamma}^{+}
(resp.
w_{\eta}^{+}
) to denote the limiting value of
w_{\gamma}(z,\lambda)
(resp.
w_{\eta}^{+}(z,\lambda)
) as
z
approaches to
z_{s}^{\prime}\in b\Omega
from the interior of
\Omega
. For fixed
\lambda
,
\eta(\cdot,\lambda)\in C^{\nu}(\overline{\Omega})
by Proposition
3.9
(i), and thus according to Proposition
7.13
,
w_{\eta}^{+}
is given by (
7.6
).

Referring to
[
7
, p. 235]
, the homogeneous integral equation

\mathcal{K}^{\lambda}\eta(\zeta,\lambda)=0

has
2n+1
linearly independent solutions.
We apply the theory of one dimensional singular integrals. The reader can refer to the classic book of Muskhelishvili
[
5
]
.

Step 2: Estimates for
w_{\gamma}^{\lambda}
and
w_{F}^{\lambda}
.

We first show that the right-hand side of (
6.10
) is in
C^{\nu,0}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\gamma^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{\mu,0}(b\Omega^{\lambda})
, and let
w_{\gamma}
be defined by formula (
6.7
),
then
w_{\gamma}^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{\mu,0}(b\Omega^{\lambda})
.

Proof.

By equations (
3.28
)
w_{\gamma}^{\lambda}
satisfies the equation

\partial{}_{\overline{z^{\lambda}}}w_{\gamma}+A(z,\lambda)w_{\gamma}(z,\lambda)+B(z,\lambda)\overline{w_{\gamma}(z,\lambda)}=0.

By Proposition
7.14
,
w_{\gamma}^{\lambda}
is the unique solution to the integral equation:

(6.13)

w_{\gamma}(z,\lambda)+P^{\lambda}w_{\gamma}(z,\lambda)=\Phi(z,\lambda),\quad z\in\Omega,

where

\displaystyle P^{\lambda}w_{\gamma}(z,\lambda):=T_{\Omega^{\lambda}}(A^{\lambda}w^{\lambda}_{\gamma}+B^{\lambda}\overline{w^{\lambda}_{\gamma}})(z^{\lambda}),\quad\Phi(z,\lambda):=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{b\Omega}\frac{(l\gamma)(t,\lambda)}{t^{\lambda}-z^{\lambda}}\,dt^{\lambda}.

By Proposition
7.12
we know for fixed
\lambda
,
w_{\gamma}(\cdot,\lambda)\in\mathcal{C}^{\nu}(\overline{\Omega})
, where
\nu=\min(\alpha_{p},\mu)
,
\alpha_{p}=\frac{p-2}{p}
.

We make a few observations:

1) By Proposition
7.3
, we have

(6.14)

\displaystyle\left|P^{\lambda}w_{\gamma}(\cdot,\lambda)\right|_{C^{\alpha_{p}}(\overline{\Omega})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}C_{p}(|A(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\overline{\Omega})}+|B(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\overline{\Omega})})|w_{\gamma}(\cdot,\lambda)|_{C^{0}(\overline{\Omega})}.

In other words, the operator
P^{\lambda}
is a compact operator for each fixed
\lambda
.

2) By standard estimates for Cauchy integral (or see
[
1
, p. 176]
), we have

(6.15)

\|\Phi\|_{0,0}\leq C_{1,0}\|l\gamma\|_{\mu,0},\quad\|\Phi\|_{\mu,0}\leq C_{1+\mu,0}\|l\gamma\|_{\mu,0}.

3) The homogeneous integral equation

w_{\gamma}(z,\lambda)+T_{\Omega^{\lambda}}(A^{\lambda}w^{\lambda}_{\gamma}+B^{\lambda}\overline{w^{\lambda}_{\gamma}})(z^{\lambda})=0,\quad z\in\Omega

has only the trivial solution for each
\lambda
.
For the existence and uniqueness, see the argument in the proof of Proposition
7.14
.

First, we show that
|w_{\gamma}(\cdot,\lambda)|_{0}
is bounded uniformly in
\lambda
. Seeking contradiction, suppose that for some sequence
\{\lambda_{j}\}
converging to
\lambda_{0}
, one has
N_{j}:=|\gamma(\cdot,\lambda_{j})|_{0}\to\infty
. Dividing both sides of (
6.13
) by
N_{j}
, we have

(6.16)

\widehat{w_{\gamma}}(z,\lambda_{j})+P^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{w_{\gamma}}(z,\lambda_{j})=\frac{1}{N_{j}}\Phi(z,\lambda_{j}),\quad z\in\Omega,

where
\widehat{w_{\gamma}}(z,\lambda_{j})=\frac{1}{N_{j}}\widetilde{w_{\gamma}}(z,\lambda_{j})
.
By estimate (
6.14
),
|P^{\lambda}\widehat{w_{\gamma}}(\cdot,\lambda)|_{\alpha_{p}}
is bounded by a constant uniform in
\lambda
.
By Arzela-Ascoli theorem and passing to a subsequence if necessary,
P^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{w_{\gamma}}
converges uniformly to some function. The right-hand side of the above equation converges to
0
by (
6.15
), so in view of (
6.16
)
\widehat{w_{\gamma}}
converges uniformly to some function
\widetilde{w}
. One can check that
P^{\lambda_{j}}\widehat{w_{\gamma}}
converges uniformly to
P^{\lambda_{0}}\widetilde{w}
, thus passing to the limit as
j\to\infty
we get

\widetilde{w}+P^{\lambda_{0}}\widetilde{w}=0.

By the above observation 3),
\widetilde{w}\equiv 0
which contradicts the fact that
|\widetilde{w}|_{0}=

\lim_{j\to\infty}

|\widehat{w_{\gamma}}(\cdot,\lambda)|_{0}=1
. Hence
|w_{\gamma}(\cdot,\lambda)|_{0}
is bounded uniformly in
\lambda
.
Applying estimate (
6.14
) we get

\displaystyle\|w_{\gamma}^{\lambda}\|_{\nu,0}\leq\|P^{\lambda}w_{\gamma}^{\lambda}\|_{\alpha_{p},0}+\|\Phi^{\lambda}\|_{\mu,0}\leq C(\|w_{\gamma}^{\lambda}\|_{0,0}+\|l^{\lambda}\gamma^{\lambda}\|_{\mu,0}),

where
C
is some constant independent of
\lambda
.

Next we show
w_{\gamma}(\cdot,\lambda)
is continuous in
\lambda
in the
C^{0}(b\Omega)
-norm. Suppose this is not the case, then there exists some
\lambda_{0}
and a sequence
\lambda_{j}\to\lambda_{0}
, such that
|w_{\gamma}(\cdot,\lambda_{j})-w_{\gamma}(\cdot,\lambda_{0})|_{0}\geq\varepsilon>0
.
Since we have shown that
|w_{\gamma}^{\lambda}|_{\nu}
is bounded uniformly in
\lambda
. Passing to a subsequence if necessary, we have that
w_{\gamma}^{\lambda_{j}}
converges to some
w_{\ast}
in the
C^{\nu^{\prime}}(b\Omega)
-norm, for
0<\nu^{\prime}<\nu
.
As before we can show that
|P^{\lambda_{j}}w_{\gamma}^{\lambda_{j}}-P^{\lambda_{0}}w_{\ast}|_{0}\to 0
.
Letting
|\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{0}|\to 0
in (
6.13
) we get

w_{\ast}(z)+P^{\lambda_{0}}w_{\ast}(z,\lambda)=\Phi(z,\lambda_{0})
for
z\in\Omega
.
Since we know that
w^{\lambda_{0}}_{\gamma}
satisfies the same equation, again by the above observation 3) we must have

w_{\ast}=w_{\gamma}(\cdot,\lambda_{0}).

But this contradicts the fact that
|w_{\gamma}(\cdot,\lambda_{j})-w_{\gamma}(\cdot,\lambda_{0})|\geq\varepsilon>0
. Hence
w_{\gamma}(\cdot,\lambda)
is continuous in
\lambda
in the
C^{0}(b\Omega)
norm. By Proposition
2.3
, the results imply
w_{\gamma}^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{\nu,0}(b\Omega^{\lambda})
.
∎

Next we show that
w_{F}^{\lambda}\in\mathcal{C}^{\alpha_{p},0}(b\Omega^{\lambda})
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
2<p<\infty
and
\alpha_{p}=\frac{p-2}{p}
. Suppose
\lambda\mapsto A(\cdot,\lambda),B(\cdot,\lambda),F(\cdot,\lambda)
are continuous maps from
[0,1]
to
L^{p}(\Omega)
. For
z\in\Omega
, let
w^{\lambda}_{F}
be given by formula (
6.9
).
Then for each
\lambda
,
w_{F}^{\lambda}
can be extended to a function in
C^{\alpha_{p}}(\overline{\Omega})
, and
w^{\lambda}_{F}\in\mathcal{C}^{\alpha_{p},0}(\overline{\Omega^{\lambda}})
. Moreover we have the following estimate

\|w_{F}^{\lambda}\|_{\alpha_{p},0}\leq C(p,\Omega)(\sup_{\lambda\in[0,1]}|A(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\sup_{\lambda\in[0,1]}|B(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}),

where the constant depends only on
p
and
\Omega
and is independent of
\lambda
.

Proof.

By Hölder’s inequality we have

\displaystyle|w_{F}(z,\lambda)|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(|G_{1}(\zeta,\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}+|G_{2}(\zeta,\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\Omega)}\right)|F(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}C_{p}\left(\Sigma_{i=1,2}\|\omega_{i}\|_{0,0}\right)|F(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)},\quad 2<p<\infty,

where
\|\omega_{i}\|_{0,0}=\sup_{\zeta\in\overline{\Omega},\lambda\in[0,1]}|\omega_{i}(\cdot,t,\lambda)|_{0}
. We have

\displaystyle|w_{F}(z,\lambda)-w_{F}(z^{\prime},\lambda)|

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{i=1,2}\iint_{\Omega}\left|G_{i}(z,\zeta,\lambda)-G_{i}(z^{\prime},\zeta,\lambda)\right||F(\zeta,\lambda)|\,dA(\zeta^{\lambda})

\displaystyle\leq C\iint_{\Omega}\frac{\Sigma_{i=1,2}\left|e^{\omega_{i}(z,\zeta,\lambda)}-e^{\omega_{i}(z^{\prime},\zeta,\lambda)}\right|}{|\zeta^{\lambda}-z^{\lambda}|}|F(\zeta,\lambda)|\,dA(\zeta^{\lambda})

\displaystyle+C\iint_{\Omega}\left(\Sigma_{i=1,2}\left|e^{\omega_{i}(z^{\prime},\zeta,\lambda)}\right|\right)\left|\frac{1}{\zeta^{\lambda}-z^{\lambda}}-\frac{1}{\zeta^{\lambda}-(z^{\prime})^{\lambda}}\right||F(t,\lambda)|\,dA(\zeta^{\lambda}).

Denote the first integral above by
I
and the second integral by
II
. Applying Hölder’s inequality for
p>2
we have

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}(\Sigma_{i=1,2}\|\omega_{i}\|_{\alpha_{p},0})|z-z^{\prime}|^{\alpha_{p}}\iint_{\Omega}\frac{|F(\zeta,\lambda)|}{|z-\zeta|}\,dA(\zeta)

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}C_{p}(\Sigma_{i=1,2}\|\omega_{i}\|_{\alpha_{p},0})|F(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}|z-z^{\prime}|^{\alpha_{p}},

where
\|\omega_{i}\|_{\alpha_{p},0}:=\sup_{\zeta\in\overline{\Omega},\lambda\in[0,1]}|\omega_{i}(\cdot,t,\lambda)|_{\alpha_{p}}
. Also

\displaystyle II

\displaystyle=\left\{\iint_{|z-\zeta|<2|z-z^{\prime}|}+\iint_{|z-\zeta|\geq|z-z^{\prime}|}\right\}\left(\Sigma_{i=1,2}\left|e^{\omega_{i}(z^{\prime},\zeta,\lambda)}\right|\right)\left|\frac{1}{z^{\lambda}-\zeta^{\lambda}}-\frac{1}{(z^{\prime})^{\lambda}-\zeta^{\lambda}}\right||F(\zeta,\lambda)|\,dA(\zeta^{\lambda})

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}(\Sigma_{i=1,2}\|\omega_{i}\|_{0,0})\left\{\iint_{|z-\zeta|<2|z-z^{\prime}|}\frac{|F(\zeta,\lambda)|}{|z-\zeta|}\,dA(\zeta)+\iint_{|z^{\prime}-\zeta|<3|z-z^{\prime}|}\frac{|F(\zeta,\lambda)|}{|z^{\prime}-\zeta|}\,dA(\zeta)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.+\iint_{|z-\zeta|\geq 2|z-z^{\prime}|}\left|\frac{1}{z^{\lambda}-\zeta^{\lambda}}-\frac{1}{(z^{\prime})^{\lambda}-\zeta^{\lambda}}\right||F(\zeta,\lambda)|\,dA(\zeta)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}(\Sigma_{i=1,2}\|\omega_{i}\|_{0,0})\left\{|F(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\overline{\Omega})}|z-z^{\prime}|^{\alpha_{p}}+|z-z^{\prime}|\iint_{|z-\zeta|\geq 2|z-z^{\prime}|}\frac{|F(\zeta,\lambda)|}{|z-\zeta||z^{\prime}-\zeta|}\,dA(\zeta)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}C_{p}(\Sigma_{i=1,2}\|\omega_{i}\|_{0,0})|F(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\overline{\Omega})}\left\{|z-z^{\prime}|^{\alpha_{p}}+|z-z^{\prime}|(C+|z-z^{\prime}|^{-\frac{2}{p}})\right\}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}C_{p}(\Sigma_{i=1,2}\|\omega_{i}\|_{0,0})|F(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\overline{\Omega})}|z-z^{\prime}|^{\alpha_{p}}.

Thus we obtain

(6.17)

\displaystyle|w_{F}(\cdot,\lambda)|_{\alpha_{p}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1,0}C_{p}(\Sigma_{i=1,2}\|\omega_{i}\|_{\alpha_{p},0})|F(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\left(|A(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+|B(\cdot,\lambda)|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\right),

where we used (
5.3
), and the constant
C
depends only on
p
and
\Omega
and is independent of
\lambda
.

Next we show
w_{F}^{\lambda}
is continuous in
C^{0}
-norm. For simplicity we omit the constant
-\frac{1}{\pi}
. Set

dA(\zeta^{\lambda})=a(\zeta,\lambda)dA(\zeta),\quad a(\zeta,\lambda):=|det(D\Gamma^{\lambda})|.

We have, using notation (
2.5
) for
\delta^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}
.

\displaystyle|w_{F}(z,\lambda_{1})-w_{F}(z,\lambda_{2})|\leq\sum_{i=1,2}\left|\iint_{\Omega}\delta^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}[G_{i}(z,\zeta)F(t)a(\zeta)]\,dA(\zeta)\right|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sum_{i=1,2}\iint_{\Omega}[\delta^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}G_{i}(z,t)](Fa)(\zeta,\lambda_{1})\,dA(\zeta)+\iint_{\Omega}G_{i}(z,\zeta,\lambda_{2})[\delta^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}(Fa)(t)]\,dA(\zeta)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\iint_{\Omega}\frac{\Sigma_{i=1,2}|e^{\omega_{i}(z,\zeta,\lambda_{1})}-e^{\omega_{i}(z,\zeta,\lambda_{2})}|}{|\zeta^{\lambda_{1}}-z^{\lambda_{1}}|}|(Fa)(\zeta,\lambda_{1})|+\iint_{\Omega}\Sigma_{i}|e^{\omega_{i}(z,\zeta,\lambda_{2})}||(Fa)(\zeta,\lambda_{1})|
